"Everything. The Knuckle's bearing, your paces since, the pitch of the ground. I've walked this by song all my life. You've walked it by sum for three hours, and you've been right every time the cloud tore." He turned to face her. "Take the bearing. I'll hold the rope."

She stood there with the cold biting her face. It was not a gift. It was a man giving the lead to the one person who could be wrong in front of him and not lose a village by it. It must cost him a great deal. She put the thought away until she had somewhere to set it down.

"Ninety-four degrees to the Knuckle at five hundred and sixty paces," said Ila. "The wall lies north-east by north, three hundred of your paces past the stone. Let the stone be somewhere in this hump. Two hundred and seventy of mine." She drew a breath. "I make it nine in ten."

"Then walk."

She walked. He came behind, paying out rope, a hand's width at a time.

Snow squeaked underfoot. She counted aloud, and the numbers went out into the white and did not come back. At two hundred and forty, the surface began to boom.

The sound came up through her boots before her ears caught it, a flat hollow note, like a knock on a cupboard. She had half a breath to know what it was.

The slab went.

It dropped out from under her with a crack she felt in her teeth, and she went down into the white to her ribs, then to her armpits, her boots kicking at nothing. Her field book slid from her coat. Her hands clawed the crust and the crust came away in plates.

The rope hit its end.

It hit hard, a wrenching jerk under her arms that squeezed the breath out of her. Then it stopped. She hung in the hole with her elbows on a ledge of broken snow and a black throat of rock under her boots. Above her came a grunt, a boot skidding on ice, and a long, low swear in the hill dialect.

"Don't move," said Kunal.

"I wasn't going to."

"I have you." He was sitting. She could see him now, six spans off, heels dug in, with the rope round his hip and his back against the slope. He had taken her whole weight on his body with no anchor. "I have you, I'm not letting go. Kick the wall. Find a hold."

She kicked. Her toe found a knob of rock. She pushed, and he hauled, and the two things happened together, and then she was out on her stomach on the crust, breathing like a bellows.

For a moment neither spoke.

"Where," said Ila, from the snow, "did you learn that?"

"Goats."

"*Goats.*"

"A kid goes down a crevice every spring." He was flexing his hands. They were bleeding a little at the palms. "You sit, you brace on your hip and you swear. It works on surveyors."

She laughed. It came up out of her with no warning, loud and cracked and stupid, flat on her face in the snow. It rang off the cloud. He looked at her as if she had struck a bell in church, and then he began to laugh too, helplessly, with his hands held out from his sides.

"I've never been on that end of it," she said at last, when she could. "Of a rope."

"I know. You hold like a woman who's never expected to be held."

"I expected to be careful."

"Yes," said Kunal. "It shows."

She sat up. The book was gone, down the hole. She looked at the black throat of rock, and a cold went through her that had nothing to do with weather. Then she crawled to the lip, put her arm in to the shoulder, and found the book wedged on a ledge. She came up with it in her fist, and it was dry.

"Five," said Kunal, looking at her. "I believe that's five of mine."

"It's one of yours." She tucked it away. "My sums saved you. Your hip saved me. I'd call that level."

"I'd call that a debt," said Kunal, "but I'll take level."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They reached the cairn at full dark, and she would have missed it.

It was knee-high and grey, a few flat stones heaped by someone who had not expected to be thanked. Beyond it stood a half-ring of dry wall, waist high, where the snow had not drifted. The ewes' bed. Forty paces past her line.

"Forty," said Ila. "Yours or mine?"

"Mine."

"Then I was thirty-odd out. That's my best result in a week."

Kunal put his hand on the top stone, and the cairn began to hum.

It was low at first, a note she felt in the roots of her teeth. Then it rose and held, and a faint blue thread uncoiled from the stones and ran off over the plateau, thin as hair, picking out a line across the dark. The cold went on, and the wind went on, but the world had a path in it again.

She had not known how badly she wanted a line to follow. She stood looking at it for longer than she meant to.

"The last of the high cairns," said Kunal. "Ewe's Rest is a short way below. I won't take that descent in the dark." He nodded at the wall. "We'll sleep in the lee."

They ate sattu, standing, then sitting, with their backs against cold stone and the rope lying slack between them. The blue thread glowed on. Ila took out her field book and found her pencil unbroken.

"I'd like to name my price," said Kunal.

She looked up. He was not smiling. He sat with his bleeding hands turned up on his knees, as if he had not noticed them.

"You said I'd earn them," she said. "I said price first. Name it."

"Each night I give you one true Neshar name. For a feature you've measured that day, and only that, and in our own word. You write it as I say it." He paused. "In return you answer one question about your orders. True. Or you refuse. Refusal is free."

"And my questions to you?"

"Ask what you like. You always have." He shook his head. "This is the other half. You told me the true road is the only kind you draw. I'm asking you for a true answer, in exchange for the names I've kept out of ink all my life."

She turned it over. It was a bargain, and a clean one, and she distrusted how clean it was. He gave a word. She gave a fact about the queen. She would write down his name for the world, and he would learn her orders a line at a time.

"It's uneven," said Ila.

"Yes."

"Your word is a key. My answer is a sentence."

"A sentence from the queen's own surveyor." He met her eyes. "I think we may be even."

"Names go on the sheet exactly as you say them."

"Exactly."

"I may refuse. Free. Every night."

"Every night." A corner of his mouth moved. "I'd be a poor student of the rope if I tried to take that from you."

She considered the blue thread running off into the dark. The wind had dropped. The stones hummed under her palm, low and quiet.

"There was a spire," she said. "When the cloud tore at dusk, north of the wall. A black pinnacle with a notch in it. I took it at three-fifty, about a mile and a quarter."

He didn't look. He knew where it stood.

"Old Woman's Tooth," said Kunal. "There's water under it in spring."

She wrote it. She wrote the name in his own hill script, because he told her how, and beside it she put the bearing and the distance. Then she added, in her small square hand, *water under, spring*. She stopped with the pencil lifted.

It was a long line for one night. A name, and a place a column might camp, and the season it would be safe to. He had given her more than he had needed to, and he had watched her write all of it.

"You didn't have to give me the spring," she said.

"It's part of the name. You'd not understand the name without it." He rubbed his hands together and winced. "My turn."

The wind came back, a little, over the wall.

"Does your commission say garrisons?"

It was a plain sentence. He said it quietly, without the courtly lift, and his eyes were on her face and not her hands. He had been carrying that question since Garh Neshar, she understood. He had carried it up the Stair and across the white and had put it down here at last.

She knew what the answer was. She had read the paper a dozen times in Taravan, and once more by lamp in the guest-house, and she knew every word. She could have said a kind thing, and the rope would have shown it. She could have said nothing, and it would have cost her nothing at all.

"It says the crown may hold every way," said Ila. "That's the phrase. I don't know what holding means to them."

Her fingers lay on her knee in the blue light. She did not look at them. She did not need to.

Kunal looked. His eyes went to her hands, pink and ordinary, and stayed there, and the stones went on humming, low and level and true.

"Every way," he said.

"Yes."

"That's not no."

"It's not yes either. It's what it says." She closed the field book on the Old Woman's Tooth. "I'll answer anything else I know. I won't make up what I don't."

He said nothing. He sat against the wall with the rope across his knees and the light on his bloodied palms, and she saw him take the words and try to weigh them, and find that they did not weigh on either side.

Her hands stayed warm. She was not sure that made it better.

# Chapter 7: Ewe's Rest

Ewe's Rest was three stone houses under a cliff, and Ila had measured all of it before anyone saw them.

Slate roofs, turfed along the ridge. Two chimneys breathing juniper smoke, a third one cold. A pen wall round a trodden yard, ninety paces, give or take a goat. The cliff leaned over the lot like a clerk over a ledger.

"Whatever Dhanu says about me," said Kunal, on the path above her, "you may enjoy it. I ask only that you don't write it down."

"I write everything down."

"I know. It's what I'm afraid of."

He said it lightly. The rope lay quiet between them, and she put the field book away without being asked, which annoyed her.

A voice called from the wall. "Is that a prince, or has someone dropped a sack?"

A broad man in a patched brown pattu had come out of the nearest house. He crossed the yard at a lurch, right foot, then the left swung out and round, like a gate on a bent hinge. A boyhood fall, she guessed, and badly set. He was laughing before he reached them.

"Dhanu," said Kunal, and nothing else, because Dhanu had him by both shoulders. For a moment they stood forehead to forehead. Then Dhanu stepped back and cuffed him round the ear, hard enough to ring.

"That's for no word since first snow."

"Dhanu—"

"That's for being thin." A second cuff. "And that's for tying yourself to a crown woman without asking me."

"Three," said Kunal, rubbing his ear. "You said one was interest."

"I'm charging by the month." Dhanu swung his bright black eyes to Ila. "You're the surveyor. I told my wife he'd lose you on the Stair."

"He nearly did," said Ila. "Ninth tread."

"Who held whom?"

"I held him."

"Gauri!" Dhanu bellowed at the house. "She *counts*!"

Kunal laughed. It was not the courtly lift she had heard across the drafting table at Garh Neshar. It was a bark, undignified, head back, and it went on until he had to lean on the wall. She had not known he owned it.

In the doorway stood a tall woman with flour to the elbows and her arms folded. She eyed the pencil showing in Ila's pocket the way you would eye a snake in the woodpile. A small face hung round the edge of her skirt, one round eye and a fist of braid.

"Boots," said Gauri. "Off. The prince first. He tracks."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Inside there was one long room, a chulha at the heart of it, bread on a hot stone, and a smell of juniper and wet wool that Ila could have drawn from memory. She sat where she was put. The rope lay across the flags to Kunal's wrist, and nobody stepped over it twice.

Dhanu had a story for every year of Kunal's life.

"Ten winters old," he said, tearing bread, "and he's sure he can carry a ewe. He gets her up on his shoulders. She sits down. He is *stuck*. Under a sheep. Weeping with rage."

"I was not weeping."

"In the feed trough, the pair of them."

"It was a bucket."

"Mini," said Dhanu gravely. "A trough or a bucket?"

"Trough," said the child, who had crept as far as the chulha and was staring at Ila's rope. Her voice was certain. She was six, and she had not been there.

"Betrayed," said Kunal, "by my own household."

"Outvoted," said Dhanu. "It's a hill custom."

Gauri set a bowl down in front of Kunal and cuffed him in passing, one flat smack round the ear, as if she were swatting a fly. "Elbows."

He took his elbows off the board. He was laughing again, and he lost the next argument as well, over whether he had ever once beaten Dhanu at stones. He had not. Dhanu proved it with three pebbles and a long account of Kunal's cheating, and Kunal conceded with both hands up, a prince in defeat, looking happier than she had seen him in a fortnight.

Ila laughed so suddenly that Mini jumped. Then Mini laughed too, in the same loud crack, and gazed at her with deep respect.

*He is loved,* thought Ila. She had not measured for that. In Taravan she had filed him under his uncle: an heir in a coat, playing at rebellion for a crown. The man cuffed round the ear in this kitchen did not fit the file.

Gauri stopped her at the door afterwards, a hand on the post.

"You're the one who measures." Her face gave away nothing. "The last of your lot told my mother the hill was crown land, and wrote it down, and went. So. My wall. Tell me if it'll hold."

The pen wall stood shoulder high, dry stone, laid with care. Ila walked it with a knotted cord and her palm flat on every course. She did not write. At the north side she knelt and put her hand into the gap at the foot, where the base met the turf.

She stood up slowly.

"Say it plain," said Gauri.

"A wall this high wants a base half its height wide. Yours is a hand short, along the whole north side." She kept her voice level. "It'll hold till thaw. Then the snow comes off the cliff and sits on it, and the north corner goes into the ewes."

Gauri looked at the wall for as long as it takes to hate a fact. Across the yard, Kunal had come to the door with the rope trailing, and he did not speak.

"His father built that."

"He built it well. He didn't have the cord." Ila held it out. "Lay a course of flat stones along the foot, a hand wide, running outward. It'll take two days and a back."

"Two days I haven't got." But Gauri took the cord and ran it through her fingers. "My husband says you don't lie. That your wrist won't let you."

"Not cheaply."

"Then I'll have it done by thaw." She turned the knotted cord over once. "Ila. You'll want a bigger bowl at supper."

It was the first time she had used the name. Ila found, with some surprise, that she wanted very much to have earned it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At supper Dhanu told the story of the high way. It was a long one, with a bad bridge and a worse mule in it. He did not name where the way began or ended, and Ila did not ask. The rope hummed nothing. She noted that he told it with his eyes on Kunal, and that Kunal let him.

"And you were never afraid of it," Dhanu said. "Not once, even at nine. The first time you walked these ways, you went alone. All alone, the first time."

Kunal reached for the dish with his whole arm.

"Gauri, is there more of the barley? I could eat a ewe." He turned the dish to the light. "Ila, tell Dhanu what you called the black crag on the Stair. He'll want to hear."

It was a very quick swerve. Dhanu's eyes rested on him a moment too long. Gauri looked at her bowl.

*The first time,* thought Ila. *Which means there was a second.* She put the sentence aside, whole, where she kept things she meant to come back to, and told them.

"Nimmi's Knuckle," she said. "After a healer we both know. She'd hate it."

"She'd be insufferable," said Kunal.

Dhanu roared. Gauri, against her will, snorted into her bread. Mini, small and fierce at the end of the board, waited for the noise to drop and then said, "Can you do a star?"


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cloud had pulled back to the north and the sky over the cliff was a clean black with holes in it. Mini towed Ila into the yard by two fingers. Kunal followed, as the rope required, and leaned in the doorway with his arms folded and a look of great innocence.

"That one." Mini pointed at a small yellow star low in the notch of the cliff. "It hasn't got a name. Baba says it's too small."

"Stars have names," said Ila. "Old ones. A name is only a handle. I don't give them out, I write down what's already—"

"Uncle says you named a rock."

"A crag."

"A rock after a lady." Mini's chin came up. "I want one."

Ila looked at the star. It was a poor little thing, third magnitude at best, hanging over a slate roof with a crooked chimney. She thought of a dozen things to say. A neutral woman would have said, *I'm sorry, that's not how it's done.*

"Then it's Mini," said Ila.

The child went rigid. "That's *me*."

"It's yours. It's the one over your house. I'll write it down, if your father lets me."

"Does it go on the map?"

"A star doesn't tell anyone where a hut is," said Kunal from the doorway, dry as dust. "I think we can spare it."

Mini ran inside to tell everyone, shrieking. The door banged. Ila stood alone in the yard with the cold in her teeth and found that she was smiling.

Kunal came out and stood a pace from her, which was as far as the rope allowed without comment. For a while he said nothing. His breath smoked.

"May I ask a question," he said. "Not one of mine, from the bargain. A private one."

"You may ask."

"You told me a map is only ever true or false. That it has no more feeling in it than a plumb line." He kept his eyes on the star. "You named a crag for Nimmi. You named a ridge for a cook who fed you. Now a star for a child you met at noon. I am trying to find a place on your sheet where you named something for a person you dislike."

She opened her mouth.

There was a thing she could say. A label. A handle. A convenience of the margin. She had said it to her brother, to the Taravan survey office, and once to a very tired examiner who had asked about Tamar's Shoulder. She said it in her head now, and it sat there, round and smooth and true.

It was not the answer. She knew it and he knew it.

"There isn't one," said Ila.

"No."

"I haven't thought about why."

"I believe that." His voice was gentle, the voice she had heard him use on Mini. "That's what I wanted to know. It's all right. I only wanted to see if you knew."

"I don't." She heard how small it sounded. She held her bare hands out in the starlight, pink and ordinary, and he did not look at them. He looked at her.

"Then we're even," said Kunal. "Neither of us knew."

He went in. She stayed a moment longer under the little yellow light, and for the first time in her life she did not know what she meant to write beside it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Ila lay on the sleeping shelf in the long room, under a blanket that smelled of goat, and did not sleep.

The rope ran from her wrist across the floor and out under the door. On the other side Kunal sat on the step with his back to the planks. Dhanu sat beside him. She could hear the scrape of Dhanu's bad foot as he stretched it out. The chulha gave a last ticking breath, and the voices began, low enough that a sleeper would hear nothing.

She was not a sleeper. She lay with her eyes open and counted the roof slates and listened.

"Raghu's pairs passed the upper fold two days ago," said Dhanu.

Nothing from Kunal.

"Two pairs, loaded, going up fast. They didn't stop for water. My boy saw them from the high pasture and ran. They were heading for the Hollow Moon."

The step creaked. The rope on her wrist went tight and stayed tight, the way it did when a man stood up and forgot himself.

"Two days," said Kunal.

*Two days.* Ila did the sum before she could stop herself. A day ahead of them at the burned hut. A day of climbing since. She had been right to the hour about the ash, and for once she wished she had been a poor reader of ground.

"Sit," said Dhanu. "You can't walk it tonight."

"I know."

"Then sit, Kunal."

The rope eased. The planks creaked as he sat.

She did not know where the Hollow Moon was. It was on no sheet she had drawn, in no shepherd's mouth she had asked, and she lay in the dark under a goat-smelling blanket with her hands quite warm, understanding that she was not supposed to.

# Chapter 8: A Cold Man's Whistle

"Take her back over the Saddle," said Dhanu.

He said it to the chulha, not to Kunal, and he said it low. The long room had gone dark but for the coals. At its far end the sleeping shelf held a long shape under a goat blanket. The pale rope ran from that shape across the flags to the stool where Kunal sat with his knees nearly in the ash.

"Tomorrow night, when the cloud's gone. Down the Shilak side, to her brother's table. Let the rope run out at the Meadow Cairn's thaw and let her go home with her pencil. We keep the ways. She keeps her paper. A bright line, Kunal. You can draw it with a stick."

"I like bright lines," said Kunal.

"You like them when somebody else is standing behind them."

That was fair. Kunal poked a coal with a juniper twig and watched it flare. Hari had kept the Shepherd's Stair thirty winters on a line as bright as Dhanu's. His hut was a black square on the snow now.

"The crown's cover sheet to the council names a way beneath the Shilak Cairn," he said. "I read it twice in the hall while Chetu argued about grain. Her own note doesn't say it. She hasn't seen it."

"Beneath."

"Beneath. Have you ever heard of one?"

Dhanu thought. The coals ticked. "A cairn is a heap of stones. Under a heap of stones there's ground."

"Then why does the queen want the ground?" Kunal turned the twig over. "If I send Ila home, I send away the only door I have into what the crown knows. I would sooner learn about a way I've never heard of from her surveyor in the snow than from a garrison captain in spring."

"And what does she learn from you?"

"As little as she can draw."

"She drew my wall in a breath." Dhanu's voice was flat. "A hand short, the whole north side, and my father laid it with his own eyes. Don't tell me she's slow."

"I don't think she's slow."

"Then you're a fool who's fond of her, and the rope will tell you so." Dhanu grunted and put two fingers into his shirt. "Look here."

He drew out a thong with a horn on it. It was a goat's horn, a finger long, yellow as old tooth, with a black ring worn into the mouth where a lip had rested for twenty years. He held it in the coal light as if showing a child a coin.

"Two long, one short," said Dhanu. "That's the high way. *Come on, it's clear.* Three short is *turn back.* That's the whole of the way, to a stranger. A horn and a noise." He closed his fist round it. "You know why I wear it against my skin?"

"Tell me."

"If they ever take me, I'll swallow the route. The bridge, the mule, every stone of it. And they'll get my whistle off a cold man."

He grinned. Kunal did not.

"That isn't funny."

"It's a joke we both know is a joke," said Dhanu. "Mostly."

*Off a cold man.* Kunal looked at the horn until Dhanu tucked it away. He had told himself that a song was safer than ink, because a song went only where its singers went. Then Hari had chalked *They ask for the princes' way* on a marker stone, and somebody had sung Raghu the Stair. Whoever it was had not needed to take a man cold. He had only needed to take a man.

"Say it," said Dhanu, quieter. "What's all this for? I like to hear it."

Kunal set the twig down.

"No garrisons in the hill villages. Not one barrack at Sarn, not one crown lamp in a tower. And a seat for Neshar's council at Taravan, won at the thaw with the queen's hand on the paper." He listened to how it sounded. "Won. Not given."

"And the ways?"

"Stay where they are. In our heads."

"Then take her over the Saddle."

"I can't, and I've told you why." He looked at the shelf. The blanket hadn't moved, and the rope between them lay slack on the flags. Slack was not the same as asleep. "I'll have to be careful what I give her. And she'll have to be careful what she asks."

Dhanu sat a while with his bad foot out. Then he said, "Gauri and I go ahead by the short way tomorrow noon. We'll stock the lake huts and sit on the high ground and watch for Raghu's pairs. You come after, when you've done what you've got to do."

"By the Hollow Moon."

"Where his men went."

"Which is why I want them seen."

"Then see them," said Dhanu, and cuffed him round the ear, softly, for the fourth time that day.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


In the morning Kunal sat in the doorway mending a pack strap and listened to Ila teach his friend's daughter to count.

"No, don't copy me," Ila was saying. She stood in the trodden yard with the knotted cord in one hand. "My stride's no use to you. Walk to the wall. Say each step. Now again, and see if you get the same number. If you do, that's your pace, and it's yours for good."

"Twenty-two," said Mini, from the wall.

"Again."

"Twenty-two!"

"Then you know how big the pen is. Not in my spans. In Mini." Ila crouched and held out the cord. "Everything in the world can be told in your own steps, until someone bigger turns up with a rule."

"Is a goat bigger?"

"Not in the ways that count."

Mini considered, then walked the wall again with her tongue between her teeth. Kunal's needle had stopped in the strap. A small, cold, unreasonable alarm rose in him.

He had taught that child the Stair song as nonsense, a counting rhyme about goats and ghosts, so it would go on being carried without anyone knowing what it carried. And here was the crown's surveyor teaching her a different kind of carrying. A number was a way, as a name was a way. A child who could pace could walk a line off a sheet in the dark. A child who sang could walk a line off a song.

*Paper goes where paper goes,* he had told her. He had not said, because he had not known it until now, that people went there too.

Ila looked up and caught him watching. She did not look away, so he did not either.

"Two hundred and four," Mini announced, round the whole pen.

"Two hundred and four of Mini," said Ila gravely. "That's a long way for short legs. Write it down. In your head. Not on my sheet."

At noon Dhanu and Gauri left with the pony and a pack of lamp oil and barley, and Mini riding on top of both. The short way kept under the snow-line, where the jogini asked no rope. Gauri shook Ila's hand once, hard, like a woman closing a bargain. Dhanu looked at the rope, then at Kunal, and said nothing at all. It was the most dangerous thing he had said in a week.

The yard emptied, and the smoke of the one lit chimney stood straight up.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They had the house to themselves that night. The chulha burned low. The wind, which had been loud all afternoon, dropped to a mutter in the roof.

Ila sat on the flags with her field book shut on her knee. She had measured the cliff behind the houses that afternoon, and he had given her the name for its notch, *the clerk's stoop*, with its season and its use. She had written it exactly as he said it and asked no more. She had not used her question for the night, and he had been waiting for it, waiting for a slate to slide.

"A question," said Ila. "Not from the bargain. Mine to ask. Yours to refuse."

"Then ask."

"At supper Dhanu said that at nine, the first time you walked these ways, you went alone. *All alone the first time.* You steered us off it as you'd steer a cart round a hole." She said it without edge. "What did he mean?"

He had known it was coming, and still the answer rose in him ready-made. It had a use already. It could be said so that she'd think kindly of him, or so that she'd think nothing, or so that she'd stop asking. He caught himself weighing it, as he weighed everything, and was tired.

"My uncle taught me the ways," said Kunal.

His hands were spread to the coals, bare, the fingertips pink in the glow. Her eyes went to them, quick and brief, the look of a warden's sister who had learned what to read on a bound man's skin.

"That's true," said Ila.

"It is."

"It isn't the answer."

"No. It's half."

"Which half?"

"The half I have ready." He kept his eyes on the coals. "I'm sorry. I haven't a better way to say it."

She was quiet. He waited for the follow-up, the plain, exact question that would cost him the rest, and he did not know what he would do.

"I'll take the half," said Ila, and opened her book, and wrote nothing in it.

She had not asked what use the other half might be to her. She had only declined to take it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He woke her in the dark before dawn, with a hand on her shoulder and no excuse, and took her up the goat path behind the houses. She came without argument, her compass in her fist. He had decided he would show her the Hollow Moon from above before tonight. He had not decided what else he would say.

The cliff top was a bare shelf of rock and old snow. The sky in the east had gone the colour of pewter, and below them Ewe's Rest lay folded in its own shadow, the pen wall like a chalk line round the sheep. To the north the land fell away into a long wide bowl, white and rumpled, with the first light lying on its far rim.

"The Hollow Moon," said Kunal. "The summer way to Kuhl, for the flocks. A night's walk across the lip, and no cairns worth the name."

"Bearing to the far rim, ten degrees west of north." She had the compass out. "Eight hundred paces down, the fan of loose stone. That's where the snow's thin. A man would keep to the left of it."

"A man would," he said, "if he had the song."

She nodded, and went on looking.

He should have stopped there. He knew every reason to stop, and he had weighed them all while he climbed. A true answer was a line on the crown's map. A true answer was a way, and a way was a lock, and he had spent his life holding the key. But the light was coming, and she had been kind about the half. She had stood in his friend's yard with a child's hand in hers and taught her to count in her own steps. Something in him had come loose and was running before he could catch it.

"When I was nine," said Kunal, "my uncle—"

"Don't turn your head."

He stopped, with his mouth still open on the next word.

Ila had not moved. Her voice had gone flat and low and perfectly exact, the voice she used for a bearing. She was looking past his shoulder, across the bowl, and her compass hand was quite still on her coat.

"The west lip of the bowl," she said. "Three hundred and fifteen degrees, above the stone fan. A black outcrop, with a pale scar of scree beneath it. Look at it with your eyes only."

He let his eyes slide. The sun had cleared the ridge behind them and laid a long gold bar across the far slope. On the rim, in the dark of the outcrop, a point of light flashed once and went out.

Then it flashed again.

"That isn't water," said Ila. "It isn't ice. Ice doesn't wink twice and stop. I've been reading these slopes since we climbed, because I read slopes. That's glass, Kunal. A lens, turned to catch the sun. It was pointed at the hamlet before we came up, and it's pointed at us now."

The wind moved. Far below them, the one chimney of Ewe's Rest went on breathing its thin straight line of smoke.

"Someone," said Ila, "is watching your house."

# Chapter 9: Between the Cairns

They went into the Hollow Moon at moonset, eight spans of pale rope between them and no light but the stars.

The line here was nothing like the Meadow Cairn's. It showed only at the old cairns, waist-high heaps spaced a few hundred paces apart, each wearing a thin blue skin of glow like frost with a fever. Between them the snow lay black and blank. The mountain showed you where you could stand. It left you to cross the rest on your own wits.

Ila had two wits to cross it with. The steady star over the north rim gave her a bearing. Her paces gave her distance. Kunal had a third, and he muttered it under his breath as he walked, a shepherd's rhyme with the tune filed off:

*Fan on the left hand, cold ridge on the right,*
*count three hundred where the snow goes blue,*
*and a hundred more to light.*

"Three hundred and eight," said Ila, as the snow went blue under the starlight.

"The song says three hundred."

"The song is eight paces short."

"The song," said Kunal, "allows for shepherds."

"The song allows for sheep."

He made a sound in his chest that wasn't quite a laugh, because they had agreed on the cliff that nothing louder than a breath would cross the bowl tonight. The sound went into his collar and stayed there. She noted, without wanting to, how he had kept it.

On the west lip, a mile and more off at three-fifteen, a red coal sat on the rim.

The watchers' fire did not flicker. Somebody fed it. It was a steady, careless, comfortable fire, and that was what she disliked about it. A man who thought no one was coming could afford one. So could a man who wanted to be looked at.

"They've lit one," she said. "A trap would light two."

"Why two?"

"So you'd look at both and miss the dark between."

He turned his head a fraction. "That's a very sinister thing to know."

"It's what a surveyor does with a spare hour."

They kept to the east wall, where its shadow fell long across the snow and swallowed the pale rope. Cairn by cairn, the line led them north.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The slope came after the third cairn, and Ila knew it before she saw it.

She knew it by the sound under her boots. Ahead, the east wall let down a long white tongue of snow between two ribs of rock. The starlight lay on it smooth and fine, like a sheet pulled tight. The line ran straight across the middle.

"Stop."

She went forward the length of the rope and knelt. She laid her palm flat on the snow, then rapped it with her knuckles. Underneath came a faint, low *whump*, a drum with nothing in it.

"Thirty-eight degrees," said Ila. "Near enough. The wind loaded it from the north-west, two days ago, maybe three. That's a slab on a rotten layer, and the layer is talking to me." She took her hand back. "One chance in five it goes if we cross high and quiet. More if either of us is clumsy."

"Is that a stated odds, or a comfort?"

"It's what I'd put in the field book."

Kunal came up beside her, as far as the rope let him. His face was a pale shape and his voice was perfectly level. "The lower third's no good either. Summer water runs under it. A spring comes out of the rock above and cuts a channel through the ground. The snow there's a lid on a hole." He pointed with his chin. "Under that bare patch, see the dimple? Last July I put a mule in it to the shoulder."

"Then low is out."

"And high is where it breaks."

"Yes." She studied the slope. "Middle, then. Above your dimple and below the crown. It's the slowest way and the dullest. We step in my steps, one at a time, no talk. A weight is a weight, so we don't pile ours on the same ground."

"The rope's eight spans."

"Then I go to the end of it, and you wait here, and you come when I stop."

He did not argue. She saw him not arguing, the whole long effort of it, a man who had taken a hundred slopes on his own say-so. Then he bowed to her, a small dry bow in the dark.

"You read. I'll hold the other end of the rope and the dimple."

She went out onto the white.

It took a long time. She put each boot down as if the snow were a thing she owed money to. Halfway across, the slab spoke under her with a deep, soft *whump*, and a crack ran away from her left boot, bright as a hair, and went on a long way. Then it stopped.

She stood in it and did not breathe. She counted to forty. Nothing slid.

"Come," she said, to the air. Her voice came out almost normal. "In my steps. At the crack, step wide, don't jump."

He came, careful and quick, and at the crack he took her whole stride and not an inch more. Then he stood beside her on the far rib of rock, and for twenty breaths neither of them spoke.

"One in five," said Kunal, finally.

"It was a good five."

"I will tell it at weddings. 'The crown surveyor said, one in five, and I believed her.'"

"It's a published figure."

That time the laugh was loud enough that she put a finger to her lips, and he doubled over to smother it, shaking, without a sound.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They went on. The line crept north in its dim blue beads. Between the fourth cairn and the fifth, Kunal said, "The next light is half a mile on."

"It's four hundred and ten paces."

"That's about half a mile."

"It's nearer a seventh, and the seventh is mine to say." She did not look round. "A thing that's four hundred and ten paces off is not about anything."

He laughed again without sound, a long silent shake that she felt as a pull on the rope. "Four hundred and ten. I am corrected. I'll try to be more certain of my own hills."

"You don't need to be certain. You need to say what you don't know."

"That is a great deal to ask of a prince."

"It's the whole job of a map."

At the fifth cairn the wall made a lee, a scoop of still air where the wind could not reach, and she let them stop. She took out the field book and her bearings pencil, sighted back across the bowl to the red coal, and put the point to the page.

The lead snapped.

It was the third time since dusk. She swore under her breath, quite fluently, in the dialect of the Taravan survey office, and began picking at the case with fingers that did not want to work.

"It's the cold," said Kunal. "It makes them brittle."

"I know why."

"You said once," said Kunal, "on the Stair. To nobody. That they were breaking on you."

She had said it to the air, mid-climb, half a sentence of complaint, and gone on climbing. She had forgotten it entirely.

He held out his hand, palm up, a bare brown hand with the rope-scrape still healing across it. "Give me the rest of them. They'll keep in my coat."

It was a small thing. It was her pencils. She had carried her own instruments across eleven winters of hills, as she carried her own opinions, and the only person she had ever let touch the case was herself.

She held it a moment longer than she needed to. Then she tipped the leads into his palm.

He opened his coat and put them inside, against his chest, over the shirt, and buttoned it again. She watched him do it with a feeling she could not file. Then they waited in the lee for the cold to go out of her fingers, and neither of them said what they were waiting for.

"Give me one," said Ila, after a time.

He took one out, slowly, with two fingers. It was warm as bread, warm as a hand just out of a pocket. She took it, and it did not snap.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"A name," said Ila. "You owe me tonight's."

"You have it. I gave it on the cliff, too freely." Kunal leaned his back against the cairn, which hummed under his shoulders with a low, dim, blue patience. "The Hollow Moon. The shepherds' summer way to Kuhl."

"You said that this morning, and I didn't write it."

"No. I noticed."

"You'd said it like a man who hadn't decided to." She uncapped the case. "I didn't want to take a name you hadn't meant to give."

He was quiet. Then, in a different voice, slower, he said it again, each word set down by itself. "The Hollow Moon. The shepherds' summer way to Kuhl."

She wrote it exactly, in the margin of the bowl, with the warm lead. The cairn hummed on, low and level.

"My question, then," he said.

"Ask."

"When you finish this map, do you give it to the queen alone?"

She had known it would come. She had not known how it would feel to be asked by a man who had just handed her back her own name for his hills.

"To the queen," said Ila, "and the archive." She made herself go on. "I don't know who reads the archive. I asked, last autumn, for the file on the Shilak Cairn. The clerk told me the crown keeps it in a drawer I haven't a key to. Whoever has the key will read this, too."

"That's more than I asked."

"You asked who. That's the true size of the answer."

He looked at her hands in her lap. She did not know why. He looked, and then he looked at her face, and whatever he had been braced for was not what he found there.

"Thank you," said Kunal.

"Don't. It isn't a gift. It's a line."

"I know." His voice was rough. "That's why."

She turned to the page so she would not have to see him.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The bearing of the fire had come out the same from the cliff at dawn and from here, within three degrees. From her two stations and the baseline she had paced, she could fix it. The figures came before she wanted them. A mile and an eighth, a little over, on the black shoulder of rock where the glass had winked. And in the glow around the fire, the long smear of shapes: two low humps, which were tents, and a third dark thing she thought was a horse line, which she would mark *unclear*.

She sat with the pencil an inch above the paper.

It was true. It was there. It was four, five men, someone's lamp trimmed, and a sheet that showed a camp on the Hollow Moon's west lip was a sheet that told the crown where to find them. That was a use of her line. It wasn't a use she had chosen.

She had never chosen, she'd told herself. She had only drawn.

She drew the fire as a small ring. She drew the humps and wrote *tents?*, drew the horse line and wrote *unclear*, put the date and the bearing beside it, and looked up.

"I've put them on the sheet," said Ila. "The fire and what's round it. I'm telling you now so you don't find it later."

His face did a quick, complicated thing in the dark. "May I see?"

She turned the book. He bent over it, close, and the rope between them lay along his arm. He read the figures and then the margin. He read *unclear.*

"It's good," he said, as if it hurt. "It's very good. Every line I'd have drawn myself." He straightened. "That's the first mark on your whole sheet I'd have put there."

"That's chance. It's not a policy."

"I know. It's why I'm afraid of you."

"You said that at Ewe's Rest."

"It's more true tonight."

The rope between them hung quiet. Neither of them did anything about it. After a while she put the lead back in his hand, and he tucked it inside his coat, and the buttons went down in the dark one by one.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dawn came up grey out of the north rim, and with it came the end of the bowl.

The land tilted. The snow gave way to a stony shoulder, the first rock since the slope. At the top of it, where the route left the Hollow Moon for the high pastures of Kuhl, there should have been a keeper's cairn, a tall one, with a slab on top and a niche for a lamp. Dhanu had called it the door.

It lay on its side.

The stones had been torn apart and flung down the slope. The slab was cracked in two. The great flat stones, old, grey, lichened over with a hundred years of shepherds' thumbs, were scattered like bones across the scree. Ila walked among them with the rope trailing, and the hairs stood up along her arms.

On the face of the nearest, someone had cut a line of marks with a knife-point. Fresh, white, deep in the lichen. A bell. A round-shouldered brass bell with a crossed hatch for the clapper, drawn three times, the same bell, on three stones.

Behind her, Kunal made no sound at all.

"You know it," said Ila.

"I know it." He crouched and laid two fingers on the scratches as if he could feel the heat of the hand that made them. "It's Raghu's."

"When?"

"I can't say."

She looked at the snow around the base. It had been trodden, and trodden again, the prints softened and filled with blown grain, so that she could not tell whether it had been a day or two or three. She was a woman who read ground for her living. That was what she did not like.

The stones should have been lit. She had seen the blue on every cairn through the night. Now they lay dull in the grey light, and the line that should have gone on north beyond them, faint and patient, was nowhere she could see.

The rope between them hummed nothing at all.

# Chapter 10: The Princes' Way

Raghu's men had dug at the foot of the door for most of a night, and they had dug in the wrong place.

Kunal stood in the wreck of it and counted the holes. Nine, in a ring round the cairn's footing, each deep as a man's forearm, the spoil heaped beside them and frozen to a grey crust. They had levered up the great flat stone at the base and turned it face down. Someone had scraped its underside with a knife point, hunting for a cut sign. They had found lichen. A pick-head lay in the scree where its haft had snapped in the frost.

"They wanted something under it," said Ila. She stood on a fallen slab with the rope slack round her boots. "Not a body. You'd dig for a body once. This is a search."

"A mark," said Kunal. "A carved marker, the kind your office sets in rock. *Here the way begins.*" He turned a clod of crust with his boot. "They think a way is a thing somebody carved."

"And it isn't?"

"There's no mark on this cairn. There never was."

He heard the relief in his own voice and was ashamed of how much of it there was. Hari had been taken, or burned out, or worse, and the last thing he had chalked was a warning. But Raghu had come up here with picks and a lantern and gone home with lichen. He had the name. He had a notion that the way began at some cairn of this valley. He did not have the beginning. Whoever had sold him the Stair had not sold him that.

*Yet,* said a small voice in Kunal's head, in his uncle's silken tones.

"Then I'll ask now, while it's quiet," said Ila. She had the field book shut under her arm. "Hari's chalk said *They ask for the princes' way.* You read it aloud and the stone hummed level. I wrote down the words and not the thing. What is the princes' way?"

There it was. He had known since the Stair that she would ask, and he had spent four days building the answer that cost least. He looked at it now, whole, in the grey light, and found he did not want to hand her the cheap one.

"What do you think it is?" he said, and heard himself, and winced. "No. That's a habit. Forgive it."

"I'll forgive it once."

"It's a route," said Kunal. "My family keeps it. Not a road, and not a secret I invented. My father kept it, and his father. And it has one rule. No one of our blood walks it between first snow and thaw."

"Not ever?"

"Not in winter, ever. It is older than the Closing Law, and older than the crown's interest in us."

"Where does it go?"

"I'll keep that."

Her eyes dropped to his hands. They were bare, pink at the fingertips, and still. "That's free," she said, to herself, and waited.

He could have stopped. He knew exactly where the stopping place was. But she had put a sentence on a sheet last night and told him so before he could find it, and a man who takes that and gives back only fog is a poorer thing than he likes to be.

"It begins at the Glass Lakes," said Kunal.

Ila's left hand went to her coat. It lay flat against the ribs, over the lining, for the length of one breath, as a woman checks a purse in a crowd. Then it came away and she looked at the cairn.

He noted it and filed it. A pocket of sheets, he thought. She was a woman who counted her paper.

"Why that rule?" she said. "A family doesn't forbid itself its own road without a reason."

"No." He sat down on the slab opposite hers, because the story was not one he told standing. "There's a reason. It's a story, and it's the only one we have, so you must take it as the family tells it."

"Tell it as they tell it."

"My grandfather was Prince Udai. When he was young, before he was anyone, he walked the way in winter with a guest." The old cadence came up in his mouth, his grandmother's, with its stops where she had put them. "He came down alone. He never went up again. Not in winter, and not in summer either, though he lived a long time after, and kept the way, and trained my father to keep it. And he made the rule."

"The guest?"

"Is just *the guest.*"

"Who?"

"No one says." He picked a splinter of slate off the slab. "I asked my grandmother when I was ten. She said it as though there were a stone in the word. My father said she'd never told him either. My grandfather died without saying, and he was not a man who forgot things."

"When was this?"

"Sixty winters ago. The year before the Closing Law was written, as the old women reckon it."

Ila said nothing. It was not a pause for effect. She had simply stopped, pencil case half out of her pocket, eyes on nothing, the way he had seen her stop at the edge of a slope.

"Sixty," she said. It was not a question.

"Near enough. You've gone quiet."

"I'm counting."

Her fingertips, on the slab's edge, stayed pink. Of course she was counting. He nodded, and thought no more of it, and was to think of it a great deal later.

"It isn't much," he said. "A prince walked up with someone, and came back without. That's the whole of what my family knows about the most important thing it owns."

"It's a good deal."

"It's a hole with a rule round it."

"Most things I draw are," said Ila. "Holes with rules round them."

He laughed, once, short, and was surprised by it.

Then what he'd said came down on him in order. *Hollow Moon,* on the cliff, careless; she had refused to write it until he said it again on purpose. *Glass Lakes* he had not said again. He had said it once, to the air, like a man putting down a cup. And she had stood there with the book under her arm, and her hand had gone to her coat.

"May I write the lakes?" said Ila.

She had seen it on his face. Of course she had.

"You may write the name," he said slowly. "Not a bearing. I haven't given you one."

"No. I'll want one. Not today." She opened the book and wrote four words, exactly, the pencil scratching in the cold. "I'm telling you now, so you know where I stand. I'll ask for the place of them, and you may refuse, and I'll mark the refusal."

"You're very fair."

"I'm very exact. It sometimes looks the same."

He watched her write. The rope between them lay along the slab and did not hum. He had given a true answer, and a line was on the crown's sheet because of it, and he sat in the broken doorway of his own people's country and felt it as he would feel a door left unlatched behind him. He felt it, and he did not go back to the latch.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Cloud came down from the north rim before the sun was well up. It poured over the lip like milk over a bowl's edge and filled the Hollow Moon to the knee, then to the waist, and the stones went grey and soft in it.

The line came back in the cloud.

He had feared it would not. Beyond the ruined door, where the route climbed a stony shoulder toward the high pastures of Kuhl, a blue thread lay low along the ground, so faint that he saw it first as a trick of the eye. It brightened at each old cairn and dimmed between. The mountain had not shut its eyes. It had only turned them, as a man does when he has been insulted and will not say so.

"Cairn-light," said Ila, with a long breath. "I thought they'd taken it."

"They broke the door. They didn't break the road."

"That's a very comforting thing to say."

"It's also what I believe."

The shoulder climbed, and levelled. At its top stood a double cairn, two heaps within a stone's throw of each other, and from between them the thread of light went two ways.

Kunal stopped.

The right-hand fork ran north-east along a shelf of the rim, straight and clean, and on its first hundred paces the snow had been trodden hard. He read it without wanting to. Four men, two pairs, loaded, a day gone, perhaps two. They had followed the broken line to the first place it forked, as men follow a rumour. Beyond the shelf the fork dropped toward the Kuhl cut, and from the shoulder over it a man with a glass could look straight down into the head of the lakes.

The left-hand fork went round. It was a curve along the lip of the bowl, high and cold and slow, and it would cost them half a day.

"Which?" said Ila.

She said it neutrally, the compass in her hand, and he understood that she was giving him the choice because it was his to make and, he thought, because she wished to watch him make it.

*She would stand on that shoulder,* he thought, *and take a bearing in one breath. She would turn round with the sun behind her and see the lakes laid out like a plate, and a bearing is not a name. A bearing is the place.* He had given the name freely. He could not give the place the same way, in one sweep of her pencil, on a morning that was not his choosing.

"The left," said Kunal. "It's the longer way. It's the quieter one."

"Raghu's men went right."

"They did."

"Is that why we go left?"

"It's one reason."

She considered that. Her fingertips were pink on the compass, and she did not ask the second reason, and he led.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The lip of the bowl was a bad road for a prince with something to hide. It was narrow and windy and open on both sides, and in the cloud there was nothing to look at but the next heap of stones and the rope going out ahead of him. He kept to the side that faced the bowl. Whenever the cloud thinned and the north-east began to show, a long slope falling away toward the Kuhl cut, he found a ridge of rock to put between it and her.

He did it three times. He did it kindly, without a word, as a man steers a guest past a door he would rather not open.

Somewhere behind him the pencil began to move.

He did not turn at once. He walked another fifty paces, to the next cairn, and let the wind have a say. The blue thread brightened under his boot. She was coming up on the rope behind him, steady, her stride measured, and in the lee of the cairn he stopped, because she had.

Ila sat down on the stones with the book on her knee. She did not look angry. He had seen her angry once, at a clerk, in a letter she had read aloud at the drafting table, and this was another face altogether. It was the face she wore for a slope she was going to cross.

"You've kept the north-east shut behind a rock since the fork," said Ila. "Three times. Once I thought it was wind."

"A rock keeps what it keeps."

"That's a question with the question taken out."

He had no answer to that. He stood with his hands bare at his sides, in the cloud, and the cairn hummed under his shoulder, low and level, as it had hummed all night.

"You're walking me around something," said Ila.

He could have said a hundred things. He could have asked her what she meant, and what she thought it was, and how a surveyor knew where a prince's loyalties lay when she could not see the slope she was being kept from. He could have said *a prince has his reasons* in his uncle's voice, and been believed by anyone but her.

"Yes," said Kunal.

The stones did not change their note. He looked at his fingertips because he could not help it, and they were pink.

Ila nodded. She turned a page, wrote a line, and held the book out to him across the rope's slack.

*K. avoids NE fork,* it said, in her small square hand, with the date beside it and the bearing of the cairn. Under it she had not written a reason. She had left the place for one, ruled, empty.

"You might have put *hides,*" he said.

"You didn't hide it. You said yes." She took the pencil from behind her ear. "If you'd said no I would have written *hides.* I write what's true, Kunal. Even when it's you."

He read it twice. It was accurate. That was the worst of it, and the strangest thing was how little he wished to take it back.

# Chapter 11: Folded Gloves

The storm took the far cairn first, then the near one, then the end of the rope.

Ila watched the rope go. One moment it ran eight spans of pale wool to Kunal's wrist. The next it ran into white and came back out again at his glove. The wind had a voice now, a long flat roar with grit in it, and it came straight over the lip of the bowl.

"Down!" she shouted, and pointed with the axe.

The double cairn stood ten paces back, two heaps of stone with a drift piled in the lee between them. A wall of packed snow, deep as a man was tall. She had noted it an hour ago, the way she noted everything, and never thought she would need it.

"We dig," said Ila. "In. Now."

"With what?"

She held up the axe in one hand and the cooking pot in the other.

He took the pot.

It was ugly work. She cut blocks from the drift with the axe and hauled them clear. Kunal bailed loose snow out behind her with the pot, on his knees, in a rhythm that did not break. Neither of them wasted breath. The snow came sideways. It filled the hole as fast as they emptied it, and she began to count, because counting was what she had. Forty strokes. Sixty. A hand, an arm, a shoulder deep.

At two hundred she could sit up inside. At three hundred she could lie flat. She cut a bench along one wall, shoulder-wide, and a shelf above it for the packs. Then she poked her axe haft up through the roof for air and plugged the door with the biggest pack.

The roar dropped to a hum.

Inside, the world was blue. The light came through the snow from the old cairn above, a sickly shine, enough to see his face by. His brows were white. There was ice in his lashes. He was grinning like a boy.

"Three hundred and forty strokes," said Ila.

"You counted?"

"I always count."

"That's a very comforting thing to say," said Kunal, and she remembered who had said it first, and looked away.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


There could be no fire. The lip had no fuel and the wind would have killed any flame. They ate sattu, the dry flour wetted with snow, scooped in cold lumps from the lid of the pot. It tasted of nothing and sat like clay in the belly. Ila ate hers because it was fuel too, of a kind.

They lay on the bench with the packs behind them, a hand apart, and let the cold settle round the edges. The rope lay in a loop on the floor. It did not hum. Nothing needed to be true yet.

"A name," said Ila, because the rules were the rules. "Tonight's."

Kunal thought. "The two heaps. The double cairn." He turned his head against the snow. "Shepherds on the Kuhl side call them the Two Aunts. They stand together and disagree about everything."

She found the pencil in her cuff and wrote it by the blue light, exactly. *Two Aunts.* "Who's the second aunt?"

"Whichever one you ask."

She nearly laughed and did not. The cold had made her careful with her face.

"My question," said Kunal. He said it softly, as if the walls could be woken. "Not about your orders. Not tonight. I'd rather know why you do it. Why this map, why this much of your life. A woman doesn't walk eleven winters of hills for a salary."

It was a better question than the one she had braced for. She lay and looked at the snow ceiling and felt the answer come up from a place she did not usually go.

"My father copied maps," said Ila.

"Copied?"

"At the Taravan survey office, for thirty years. Other men went out and measured. He sat at a desk and made the fair copy, in ink, with a ruling pen. He was very good. You couldn't tell his hand from the original." Her voice went flat and precise, the voice she used for a bad figure. "Every one of those maps has a surveyor's name in the corner. Seven hundred, perhaps. His name is on none of them."

"Not one?"

"Copyists don't sign. It's a rule." She turned the pencil in her fingers. "He used to say it was an honour to be exact. He said it so often I stopped hearing it. Then he went blind in the left eye at fifty-nine, and the office gave him a pension and a clock."

The blue light lay across Kunal's face. He did not say he was sorry. He said, "So you'll put your name in the corner."

"I'll put it on the whole sheet. Chief Surveyor, and the whole sheet, and nobody's hand underneath mine."

"That's all?"

"That's quite a lot."

"I didn't say it was small." He was quiet, then went on in a different voice. "It's why you wrote *K. avoids NE fork* and not *hides.* Because you know what it is to be copied out of your own work."

She had not thought of it that way. She lay very still. It was right, and nobody had ever put it so.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wind rose and fell over the roof like surf. Hours went by, or what felt like hours. Her toes had gone from pain to a dull, far-off ache she did not trust.

"My turn," said Ila. She had not meant to say it. The words arrived on their own. "You gave me a name. I asked you a question on the cliff and you answered. I'll give you something that isn't in the field book."

"You don't have to."

"I know. That's why."

He turned on his side, so he could see her.

"My grandfather was a warden," she said. "Of the Shilak post. Sixty-one winters ago. Before there was a hall, before the Closing Law. He went out in a night storm to check the cairns for a lost party, and he didn't come back."

"I'm sorry."

"They found him at the thaw. He was on the slope above the third bend, and he hadn't fallen. He wasn't hurt. He was sitting against a flat stone, quite whole. And his gloves were folded on the stone beside him. One on the other." She held up her bare hands and laid the left flat over the right. "Like that. Folded."

"Folded," said Kunal.

"No one in my family has ever known why."

She had told it before, three times in her life. Each time the listener had said *how sad* or *the cold makes men strange* and moved on, and she had let them, because it was easier. She waited for it now. The story was already dull in her mouth, a thing worn smooth.

"How exactly?" said Kunal.

She turned her head.

"The gloves. How were they laid? Left on top, or right? Palms in, or palms out? Were the cuffs to the stone's edge, or the fingers?" He was not smiling. He had propped himself on one elbow, and he looked at her as she looked at a baseline. "Did they lie square? Or like someone who'd set them down quickly?"

Nobody had asked. In sixty winters, not one person had asked.

Her father had not asked, and her brother had asked only once, whether it was true, and their father had poured a drink and not answered. She had a picture in her head and had never had anyone to hand it to. She took a breath and found it unsteady.

"Left on top," said Ila. "Palms in, together. Square to the edge of the stone. The fingers pointed the way he'd been walking." She stopped. "Like a pair set out for the morning. That's what my father said, once, and then wished he hadn't."

"Set out," said Kunal, very quietly.

"Yes."

"That isn't what a frozen man does. A man dying of cold drops things. He doesn't square them."

"No."

"Someone folded those gloves with a steady hand, and knew what he was doing."

She could not speak. Something sat in her throat. It had never, in sixty winters, had a place to go, and now it had one, and it did not know what to do with itself.

Kunal did not say *I understand.* He did not touch her. He lay with his elbow on the bench and let her have the next ten breaths, and then he said, "Thank you for telling me," in the plain voice a man uses for a plain thing.

She thought of Prince Udai, who had walked up the princes' way sixty winters ago with a guest, and come down alone. She thought of the number sixty. She held it in her closed hand like a coin, and told him nothing about it.

It was free. It was a silence, and silence was free. She lay awake and knew exactly how much it cost.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The storm stood on the roof all the next day.

They knew it by the sound and by the dark. Twice Ila dug out the vent and found the world a white wall, the cairn-light a smear in it. The cold crept deeper. Kunal's lips had a blue tinge, and when she looked at her own nails they were the colour of old candles. They ate more sattu. She counted the packets, the one thing she could do, and there were six days in them, and then four if the cloud did not lift.

At dusk, by her watch, he began to shake.

It was a small shake, the kind a man tries to keep in his jaw. She watched it for ten breaths. She was a woman who measured, and she had measured wrong.

"We're both losing it," said Ila. "The heat. The packs are no use, they're as cold as we are." She made herself say the next part clearly. "Two bodies together keep warmer than two bodies apart. It's arithmetic. I'm saying it plainly so you know it's arithmetic."

"Is that an order?"

"It's a proposal."

"I accept." His teeth were chattering, and his voice was dry as dust. "On the terms stated."

They did it carefully. She turned her back to him on the bench, and he put his arms round her and folded the coat over them both, and the whole length of him lay behind her like a wall that was only slowly learning to be a wall. Her shoulder blades found his chest. His breath went into her hair. He was shaking, and then, after a while, a little less, and then, after longer, there was a heat that was no longer hers or his.

"I should say," said Ila, to the snow in front of her, "that this isn't harbouring."

"It isn't?"

"Under the new law. Shared shelter on a licensed crossing is expressly lawful. Clause nine. My brother and the council argued it for a week."

"Ah."

"Under the old law you'd have hanged me."

"Under the old law," said Kunal, behind her ear, "I'd have been the one hanged. For harbouring a crown surveyor. In a snow cave."

"Is that worse?"

"It's worse for the cave."

She began to laugh. It started low in her chest and she tried to stop it, and it only got bigger, and then she felt him laughing too, helplessly, the whole of him, against her back. It shook the packs. A little powder of snow fell from the roof onto the pair of them. She laughed until her eyes ran and the ache went out of her ribs.

"It's worse for the cave," she wheezed. "You said it so seriously."

"It's a very serious cave."

"It's a beautifully measured cave."

He went still. Then, lower, "That's the first time I've heard you laugh like that," said Kunal.

"I do it all the time."

"Not like that."

She thought about it. He was right. She lay in the circle of his arm, with her back warm, and did not move away.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Half of nine," said Kunal.

It was the second night. The wind had dropped a little. She could tell by the voice of it, which had lowered to a groan. They had not moved from the bench, and nothing in her wanted to.

"I'm sorry?"

"You gave me your grandfather. It would be uneven to hold back." His breath moved in her hair. "I was nine. That's the age. I'll give you half."

She said nothing. She had learned, this winter, that saying nothing was sometimes the response.

"My father died at the end of that summer," said Kunal. "He was the kind of man who left a room better lit than he found it. Everyone said so. My uncle was his younger brother, and he came to the funeral, and a month after it he took me up a ridge above the house. To see the valley, he said. To see what would be mine." The breath stopped. It started again. "And I came home."

He did not go on.

She waited. She heard all that was not said. He had stopped on the edge of a slope and was standing on it, not going over, not going back. She did not ask what happened on the ridge. She did not ask what *and I came home* meant, or why a boy of nine would say it like that. She knew a refusal when it was held out to her, like a glove, and it was his to hold.

"Thank you," said Ila. She turned her head and put it on the white wool of his sleeve. It was all the answer she could think of, and she had no way to measure whether it was enough.

He let out a long breath.

"You didn't ask," said Kunal.

"You didn't say I could."

"No." He was quiet. "Most people would."

"Most people aren't good at leaving a hole in a map."

He made a sound that was almost a laugh and almost not. His arm tightened round her, once, then eased.

They lay and listened to the wind. After a long while he said, "My question, then. My real one. I'd rather ask it tonight than in the morning."

She knew, at once, that this was the one. She had known it was coming since the lip, since the sheet, since the line he had read twice in the cloud. She felt it the way she felt a slope under her boot.

"Ask," said Ila.

"Suppose you found a way. A true way, through the hills. One that let my uncle's men into every hamlet of Neshar, down to the last fold, with no road they could be stopped on." He said it level, without heat. "A way nobody had drawn. And you were the one who'd found it. Would you draw it?"

The cave was very quiet. The cairn-light made the snow overhead into a slow blue sky.

She could have said *I don't know.* That would have been free. She could have said *it depends who asks,* and that would have been free too. She lay with her back to his chest and her hands in her lap, and looked at them in the light, and they were pink and warm.

"Yes," said Ila.

Her hands stayed warm. The rope on the floor lay still. It did not whiten her fingertips and it did not hum, and she had never in her life wished so much that a thing had been a lie.

He did not move. His arms stayed round her, and for a moment she thought he had not heard. Then she felt him turn his face away, out of her hair, toward the wall of snow, and stay there.

"A map is true or it's false," she said. She heard how thin it sounded. "What it does after is not the map's business. If I drew less than I found I'd be lying on paper. I won't. Not for the queen. Not for you."

"I know," said Kunal, to the snow.

"I'd tell you first. I'd tell you before I drew it."

"I know."

"That isn't nothing."

"It isn't nothing," he agreed. His voice was perfectly gentle. It was the voice of a man setting something down that he had carried a long way. "It's very much more than a man could ask for. It's only that I'd hoped you'd lie."

She lay quite still with the answer in her mouth. It was honest, and it was hers, and it was warm. Outside, the storm went on talking to the stones.

# Chapter 12: The Widow's Ladder

The fall had stopped in mid-leap.

Ila stood at the rim with the wind gone out of the morning and looked at it. Frozen water hung off the lip in grey ropes and sheets, thick as a man in places, thin as window glass in others. Light came through it green. Below the last of it, cloud lay in the gap like wool in a bin, and she could not see the bottom.

"Under," said Kunal.

"Under what?"

He crouched and showed her. Where the ice met the rock, a slot ran back, one body wide. Behind the curtain, a stair went down. Steps were cut in the wall, each the depth of a boot, each lip glazed clear. They slanted left and out of sight.

"The Widow's Ladder," he said. "The families of Ewe's Rest use it when soldiers come. So do the other hamlets on this side. No one below the Kuhl cut has walked it. No one in Taravan has heard the name."

"Who cut it?"

"Whoever needed to leave in a hurry. The hamlets re-cut it every thaw." He looked up at her. He had not mentioned the cave since they dug out, and neither had she, and the not-mentioning had a shape like a third person walking on the rope. "I'll anchor. You count. It's what we're each for."

She looked at the steps and felt the old, clean pull of a problem she knew how to solve.

"The rope is eight spans," said Ila. "The ice is a hundred feet, near enough. That's four pitches, and a ledge to stand on between each. Is there a ledge?"

"Three."

"Then say so. I'd like my facts in the order I need them."

"Three ledges," said Kunal, gravely. "Possibly four. I was eleven."

She laughed, once, and was glad of it. The morning eased a little.

He set the rope round a horn of rock above the slot and sat behind it with his boots braced and the line across his hip. His hands were steady. She stepped over the lip and into the green.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The first pitch was kind. The steps were deep and the ice outside them was a hand thick, and it hummed faintly in the sun like something alive. She went down facing the wall with the axe in her right hand and counted aloud, the way she always did.

"Eleven. Twelve. Wet on the lip. Thirteen."

"Slow," said Kunal's voice, from above and a long way off. The curtain made it hollow.

She gave the first pitch thirty-six steps and the second thirty-four. At the second ledge she stood on a shelf the width of a door and brought him down. Then she looked over the edge, and the ice changed.

It leaned out. The wall under the fall bellied away from her, and the steps went down it at a slant she did not like, glazed over and weeping where the sun had touched them. The curtain was thin here. Cloud showed through it, and she did not look at it.

"This is the hard pitch," said Ila.

"Yes."

"You said three ledges."

"I said there were ledges. I didn't say they were evenly kind."

She measured it with her eye. The treads were short and close, a hand and a half apiece. Whatever the count, the pitch fell barely forty feet. It sat inside the rope's eight spans with a few paces in hand, and that was the only reason she would take it.

At the back of the ledge stood a squat knuckle of rock, worn glassy by other ropes. Kunal threw a turn round it, sat low on the lee side with the line round his back, and braced. She could hear him breathe.

"Count it for me," he said. "Aloud. I'll feel the rope."

So she did. She set the axe-pick in the ice and gave him every step as she took it, so he would know where her weight was before it came. "Eight. Nine. Lip's rounded, I'll stand on the heel. Ten."

He began to sing.

It was not a song, quite. It was a low count with a lilt in it, the kind of thing a mother hums with a hand on a back. After a moment she understood that it was meant to keep her feet in time. A step to a line, a line to a breath.

*Count to the ledge and the ledge to the wall,*
*lean to the rock and let the water fall.*
*One for the cold hand, two for the warm.*
*The widow was carried down.*

"Thirty-one," said Ila. "Don't make it rhyme with anything. Thirty-two."

"It's a route-song. It has to rhyme or nobody remembers."

"Thirty-three."

The ice leaned. She leaned with it, and the song went round again, and she counted over it. At forty she put her left boot on a glaze she did not trust, and it held, and she let a long breath go through her teeth. At fifty-one the rope hummed against her wrist with the strain of him holding. At fifty-seven her boot struck flat stone and the world stopped sloping.

"Fifty-seven on the pitch," she called. "Ledge. Come down."

She had just time to stand with her back to the rock and her heart going before the line slackened and he began to descend. He came down much faster than she had. He had done it before, she thought, as a boy, with soldiers or without them.

The song was still in her head. *The widow was carried down.*

She turned the word over.

"Carried," said Ila, when he reached the ledge.

"Mm?"

"In the song. What does it mean?"

"What it says." He shook the rope loose from his hip. "She had a bad hip and a sister who walked her. She was carried down the way a child is. By the hand, over the bad places. My grandmother sang it that way. In the hamlets, to carry is to see someone across."

"In the chronicles it means something else."

He looked up.

"I've read every chronicle of the passes the survey office holds." She said it flatly, because that was how it went. "*Carried* is a word for the dead. A man is *carried down at the thaw.* It means they found him and brought him in. It's the only way the clerks use it."

"Same word," said Kunal slowly.

"Same word, two valleys."

"One of us has the wrong end of it."

"Perhaps neither." She was not looking at him. She was looking at the ice, at the clean green edge of it where the sun came through. Her grandfather had been carried down. His entry said so, in the Shilak chronicle's copperplate. She had read it years ago, over her father's shoulder, and thought it only a word.

She put it away, carefully, with the number sixty. The pile was growing. It was not a thing she could set a bearing on.

"Last pitch," she said. "Keep singing."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They reached the foot at dusk, with the cloud tearing off the slope in strips and the last light lying copper on the far rim of the Hollow Moon. There was a niche behind a boulder, lined with flat stones, where some family had sheltered often enough to build a hearth. Under the lowest slab lay a cache of dung cakes, dry as bread. The mountain's one custom, thought Ila, and the only one anyone ever kept.

They made a small fire. They ate, and said little, and he went out to look at the sky.

She sat with the field sheet on her knee.

It was the fair sheet, the one she would carry home. Not the notebook, which was her own, but the paper she had drawn on since Garh Neshar, and which she had sworn at nineteen would show the crown every way. She drew the day on it with the ruling pen. The lip of the Hollow Moon. The Two Aunts, in his words. The left fork, the cairn where she'd sat, the bearing she had taken of a long grey slope she had been steered away from. The rim above the fall.

She drew the rim. She drew the lip of it, and the shape of the ice, and the line of rock. She stopped with the pen over the slot.

It would be a short line. A body wide. She had the count, a hundred and sixty steps all told, fifty-seven of them on the hard pitch, and she knew the slant and the three ledges, or four. She could ink the whole of it in the time it took the fire to settle. It was true. It was a thing she knew.

*If I drew less than I found I'd be lying on paper.*

She had said it herself, in a cave, and meant it.

She lifted the pen. The ink dried on the nib. She drew the rim of the fall as it looked from above, grey, unbroken, with nothing behind it, and went on to the next feature. It was the first line she had ever left out on purpose and meant to say so aloud. The one on the east, in the dark, she had left off in silence. This one she would own.

A sheet that was true and incomplete. She knew the phrase. She'd have called it a dodge in anyone else.

Kunal came back in, ducking under the stone. The cold came in with him. He sat across the fire from her and held out his hands to it, and said nothing, and did not look at the sheet.

"I didn't draw it," said Ila.

He went still.

"The Ladder. The slot. The steps. I didn't draw it." She held out her right hand, palm up, over the fire, so the light fell on it. "I wanted you to hear it from me and not find it out by a gap."

He looked at her hand for a long time. It was pink and ordinary. The nails were clean. The fire lay along the lines of the palm.

"Warm," said Kunal.

"Warm."

"You told me in the cave you would."

"I told you in the cave I would draw a way if I found one. I found one." She put the hand down. "I've left it off. I don't know yet if that's a lie, but it isn't one that costs anything. I've checked."

"Why?"

She had thought about that on the hard pitch, on the ice, with fifty-seven steps going by. The reason would not sound as good aloud as it had in her head.

"Because I sat at Gauri's fire at Ewe's Rest," said Ila. "Because Mini paced her wall for me. Because a girl of six has a place to go when soldiers come, and I would be the one who put it in the crown's hand." She stopped. "It isn't a principle. It's just that I know their names."

"That's quite a lot," said Kunal.

"I didn't say it was small."

He smiled, a small, crooked thing, gone as soon as it came. Then he was quiet. "You know what it costs you. The oath. The sheet is audited at Taravan every spring. If an auditor finds a way you walked and didn't draw—"

"Then I'm a surveyor who forgot a way."

"You, forgot."

"I'd have to say it was an error," said Ila, "and it wouldn't be. That's the part I haven't solved. I'll have to find the sentence that's true, and I haven't." Her voice went thin. "Chief Surveyor is a name on a sheet. I've worked eleven winters. I could lose it over a staircase."

"Then take it back." He was turning the loose end of the rope in his fingers, slowly, as he spoke. "Draw it. I'll not ask you to carry my people's ways at the cost of your own."
