It was handsomely said. It was courtesy, and she watched him pay for it. His thumb worked the wool until the strands creaked, and his eyes stayed on the fire and did not once come to the sheet. He had it in his hands to be generous, and he could not hold it. She saw the exact moment it gave out.

"I won't," said Ila.

"Ila."

"I've decided." It came out sharper than she'd meant. She made herself breathe. "I've said I'd tell you before I drew a way, and I'm telling you. I didn't draw it. That's all. Let me have the whole of it before you say anything kind."

Kunal let the rope go. He laid it down between them in the dust and sat with his hands open to the fire. When he spoke it was low, and the courtesy was gone from it.

"Promise me you never will."

She looked at the rope.

It was a fair request, the only one he could have made. His bare hands lay open and steady on his knees, as if he had wanted very much not to make it.

"I can't," said Ila.

His face did not change, but the fire in his eyes stopped moving.

"A promise made on this rope, if I broke it, strikes me as a great lie," she said. "You know that. I'd lose the hand, or worse. And I'd be promising against my own oath. The crown commissioned me to draw every way I find, and I swore it. If the crown asks for the sheet, the oath says draw. I won't promise to break that before I know what I'd do."

"You've just not drawn it."

"That's one staircase, on one evening. A promise is for all of them." She heard how reasonable it sounded. She hated it. "I won't make you a promise I might not keep. It's the one thing I can give you that isn't free to lie about."

He took that in.

"And besides," said Ila, "it isn't properly measured yet."

The words lay in the firelight. The rope did not hum. It stayed quiet and level as it always did for a true thing. She had spoken a fact. She had taken the bearing of the head of the Ladder only by eye, and the whole slant wanted a second reading, and she did not yet know the length of it to within a span.

And she had said it so that he would hear a way out. A *not yet.* An *it may be.*

He heard both. She watched him do it. His eyes went to her hands, and they were warm, and his mouth moved a little at the corner, the way it did when a sum came out and he did not like the answer.

"That's true," said Kunal.

"Yes."

"And it's a dodge."

"Yes."

"You might have said so."

"You'd have heard it anyway."

"I did." He let out a breath that fogged and thinned. "A map can be true and still leave a thing out. You'll tell me that."

"I'll tell you a blank isn't false."

"No," said Kunal. "A blank isn't false. It's only a decision."

She opened her mouth to answer and found there was nothing in it. She closed it. It was the sentence she'd have used on any man who tried it on her, and he had used it gently, as one hands back a dropped pencil.

She did not concede it. She did not argue it. She sat with the dried ink going stiff on the pen and let it stand.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He rose a little later and went out to the edge of the shelf. After a time she put the sheet aside and followed, with the rope paying out between them, and stopped at his shoulder.

The cloud had gone. Stars lay thick and hard in a black sky, and the cold came up off the ground as it does after a storm, with a clean smell like iron and snow.

"The fall has a name," said Kunal, without turning. "The families don't give it to people from below. They call it the Long Braid."

She stood very still.

He had not asked her anything. Every night since Ewe's Rest he had set a question beside a name. Tonight he set none. It was a name, held out, with nothing on the other side of the scale.

"That's your name for tonight," said Ila.

"It's just a name." He was still looking out. "I'd have given it you anyway."

"It'll go on a line."

"I know what happens to a true answer on this rope." His voice was almost gentle. "I know what you do with them. I've watched you. You write what's true, even when it's me." He looked at her then. "You may write it."

She took out the notebook, the one that was her own. She wrote it by starlight, exactly. *Long Braid.* The pencil scratched. Her hand was steady, and it was warm.

When she looked up, he had turned back to the edge.

"There," said Kunal.

Below the shelf the ground fell away into a vast pale floor, ringed by black mountains. Three long shapes lay on it, one after another, lighter than the snow round them, as if someone had set down three mirrors and walked off. Starlight lay in each, flat and perfect. Far out, from somewhere in the middle one, came a sound like a great drum struck once, deep and slow. It rolled along the basin and came back off the cliffs.

"The Glass Lakes," said Kunal.

He didn't say anything more. He didn't give her a bearing. He stood with his hands bare in the cold and let her look.

Her left hand went to her coat, to the lining over her ribs, and lay flat on the paper sewn there.

*Open when you stand at the Glass Lakes.*

She was not standing at them yet. They were a night's walk off and a long way down. But she could see them, and the ice rang again in the dark, and she did not take her hand away.

# Chapter 13: The Ringing Stone

They came down onto the basin floor at first light, after a night of walking by blue cairn-light with the rope between them. Kunal knew the storm would reach the water before they did.

He saw it as the last scree gave out underfoot. A long grey wall rolled down the far peaks like a lid sliding shut. Under it the three lakes lay pale and flat and shining. They would not shine long.

"Three hours," said Ila.

"Four. Perhaps five."

"Three." She had her watch open on her palm and her eyes on a spur across the basin. "Its shadow crossed that spur between my first count and my second. That's not a cloud drifting. It's a cloud arriving." She snapped the watch shut. "Walk."

They walked.

The floor was scree and old drift, hard under the crust. The rope ran eight spans of pale wool, taut and then slack and then taut again as their strides found each other. The wind was at their backs, which he did not like. A wind that pushes you is a wind that wants you somewhere.

Ila did not look at the sky again. She worked. He had watched her for more than two weeks now and it had not once grown ordinary. She took a bearing off a crag without breaking stride, her elbow tucked, her lips moving on the count. When her pencil lead snapped she held out her left hand, palm up, and did not turn her head. He put a fresh one in it, warm from inside his coat. She wrote. She held out the hand again.

"The shore cairn," he said, to keep his voice steady. "It's close. A mile along, no more. We'll shelter there if the front comes early."

"A mile."

"About."

"Kunal." He heard the delight start in her voice before she turned. "I paced a baseline at the foot of the Ladder and took two bearings on that cairn off the Raven Spur. I've carried the figure down the basin all night. From where we stand, right now, it is nine hundred and ten paces, give or take a dozen. A third of a mile, with a following wind."

"It looks a mile."

"It looks a mile because you hill people measure distance by how tired you'll be." She was grinning. "I have never once been wrong about a pace."

"You were wrong about the Ladder's third ledge."

"I was told there were three. Possibly four."

"Fair."

Then she laughed. She did it the way she always did, with no warning, her head going back and the sound going out across the basin so loud that two ravens broke off the scree and flapped away. It rang on the cliffs. It came back thin.

He had been set down in front of that laugh perhaps six times now, and each time he had thought he was ready. He was not. His boot went wrong on a loose slab and he caught himself on the rope, his count lost entirely. He had spent years teaching himself not to want a thing where others could see him wanting it. Now he stood on a frozen shore, caught wanting this, openly, to be proved wrong by her again.

"Walk," said Ila, still smiling, and he did.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cairn stood where she had said it would, within a dozen paces of her figure.

It was a jogini cairn, old, chest-high, built of flat grey slabs the lake wind had polished. It stood on a shelf above the shore where the scree gave out. The storm had pulled the morning down to dusk, and the blue light in the stones showed even by day. When they came up under it the wind went through the gaps and made its low note, level as a bowl rim before a finger touches it.

The Mirror lay below them. It was the first lake and the largest, a flat white plate a mile across with no mark on it. Beyond it the second and third were smaller shapes, sunk in shadow. Kunal had never come this way in full light. He had come at night, by the ridge, twice, and had not looked down.

Ila stood on the lip of the shelf to take her bearing of the far shore. Her left hand went flat against her coat, over the lining at her ribs, and stayed there. It stayed through the sighting. It stayed while her right hand wrote, and she held the book against her hip and wrote one-handed, rather than take it away. She did not say why. He had seen her do it once the night before, at the edge of the shelf above the lakes, and had not asked. He did not ask now. Whatever lay under that wool, she kept her palm on it the way a person guards a sleeping child.

Behind her, the storm stood nearer than it had any right to. The grey wall had crossed the second spur since the scree, and its lit edge had gone the colour of old pewter. Frost had caught in the loose hair at her temple, and she had not noticed.

"It's gained," she said. "Faster than I put it. Twenty minutes to be on the ice before it. After that, none."

"Then we have twenty minutes."

"That's what I said."

"I know." He was standing too close, and he knew it, and he did not step back. "You make a deadline sound like a dare."

She turned.

She had not meant to, he thought. She had turned to say something sharp, and then the sharp thing was not there. They stood a pace apart under the stones with the wind going through them. Her face was bare and wind-scoured, her lashes white. Her eyes moved, once, from his eyes to his mouth. He saw her see him see it.

Neither of them stepped away.

"You've been watching me since the scree," said Ila. Her voice was lower than he had heard it.

"I've been watching you work."

"Is there a difference?"

*Is there?* The words were already in his mouth. It was his oldest habit, a question handed back like a coin. But she had spent two weeks learning his habits. She would count it a refusal, and she would be right. He opened his mouth to say *yes*, which was true.

She spoke first. She glanced down at the field book in her fist and up again. "You've watched me draw all winter. Does it matter to you what goes on it?"

It was the question the whole rope had been walking toward. Thirty years of dinners answered for him. The smooth, warm reply that soothes a guest and costs the host nothing came out before he had weighed a word of it.

"It doesn't matter to me what you d—"

The stones rang.

It was not loud. The low note in the cairn lifted, the way a bowl's song climbs when a wet finger presses harder on the rim. It went up past the wind. The rope gave a hard cold pull at his wrist, and his fingertips went numb in his glove.

He stopped. His mouth shut on its own.

Ila had heard it. She did not flinch or step back. She went very still, her head a little to one side, the way she listened to ice.

"Finish it," she said.

"No."

"Finish the sentence, Kunal."

"I won't."

"Why not?" Her voice was perfectly even. "If it's true, it costs nothing. If it isn't, you've lost a few fingertips and I've lost an afternoon. Say it."

He looked at her, and at the stones behind her. He thought of his uncle, who had taught him the autumn he was nine, over supper, in the pleasant voice he used for terrible things. *Whoever knows the way owns the people, Kunal. Remember it. Everything else is manners.* It had sat in his mouth ever since, a stone he could neither swallow nor spit. And what had just left his mouth was manners, the cheapest kind, pressed on the one person who had asked him a plain thing plainly. If he said what was true now, she would hold it in her hand, written down, with his name beside it.

But she had refused him a promise at the Ladder's foot because she would not give one she might break. She had still left the staircase off her sheet and told him, plainly, why. She had paid for his people's ways with her own good name. She was owed something.

"It matters more than anything," said Kunal.

The stones dropped. The note slid down through the wind and settled, low and level, as it had been when they arrived.

"Your hand," said Ila.

He pulled the glove. Five nails the colour of candle wax, the white climbing a knuckle's depth. The stones had gone quiet, but the cost had not. A lie once begun did not unbegin because a true word came after it.

"An hour," he said. "It's small."

"I can see how small." She did not look away from the hand. "More than anything?"

"More than my seat on the council. More than my uncle's head on a plate. More than Neshar, on some mornings, and I'm not proud of that." It came out of him in a rush, rough. "The first thing was manners. It was out before I knew it, the way I've answered every question since I was nine. The mountain weighed it for me. So you'll have to have the truth."

"I can have it?"

"You've had it all winter. You'll do what you'll do." His throat hurt. "You'd write it down if I asked you not to."

"Yes."

"Then write it."

She did not reach for the notebook. She lifted her bare hand, slowly, as if there were a long way to bring it, and laid two fingers against the front of his coat. They rested over his heart for the length of one breath. They were warm.

"I'm not writing that one," said Ila.

It was not a kiss, and it left him weaker than any kiss would have.

The wind changed on the stones. It went from a hum to a groan.

"Kunal," said Ila.

"I know."

"The front."

He looked past her.

The grey wall had stopped being a wall. It had become the whole north, and it moved faster than any cloud had a right to. Its edge was a white line eating the far shore, and under the line the lakes were going dark.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They ran the last of the shore with the snow coming in sideways. It was not yet a storm. It was a warning of one, hard small pellets that stung the face and hissed on the crust. By the time they came down to the lip of the Mirror, he could not see the third lake at all.

The cairn-light stood on the ice. He had expected it. Faint blue shapes ran out from the shore in a line, lamp to lamp, dead straight across the plate toward the far side. The winter line. Two bound people could walk it to the other bank.

Ila stopped at the edge and went down on one knee.

"Wait," she said. "I want to sound it."

"There's no time."

"Ten minutes. I'll tap the ice with the haft every ten paces out and listen." She had the axe off her pack already. "Clear lake ice rings. Rotten ice goes dull. If there's a pressure line, a spring, a seam, I'll hear it. I won't walk onto a plate I haven't read."

"The line is lit."

"The line is lit for people. It isn't lit for the weather."

"Ila." He took her arm, his bare hand on her sleeve, the nails still white. She looked at the hand, and then up at him, snow packed on her eyebrows, the axe haft in her fist and the hunger to be exact all over her face. He wanted to give her the ten minutes. He wanted to stand on that shore and let her read the whole lake like a page. "Look behind you."

She looked.

The north had gone out. There was no far shore any more, no cliffs, only a brown-white wall a few hundred paces off, and the sound that came out of it was long and flat and full of grit. Three minutes, he thought. Perhaps two.

"If we're on the shore when that hits," said Kunal, "we'll be in the open with no cave and no wall and the whole sky coming down on us. If we're on the line, we'll be moving. We'll be across in a quarter hour."

"On ice I haven't sounded."

"On the one road the mountain lit for us."

He watched her weigh it. It took her about as long as a breath. She had been wrong about a Ladder and could not stand to be wrong about a lake, and he saw her hate the answer and take it.

"Then I tap as we go," said Ila. "Every step. You don't talk."

"I won't."

"You walk where I walk. You walk on my left foot's track and nowhere else."

"Yes."

"And if I say stop—"

"I stop."

She nodded once and stood. She stepped onto the Mirror with the axe haft low in her right hand, and the wood went tick on the ice, tick, and the sound ran out ahead of her, bright and clean.

He pulled the glove back on over the white nails, and went after her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wind took them at the third lamp. It came on all at once, a hand between the shoulders and a roar in both ears, and the world went to white and rope. He could see her back, a dark shape, three paces off. He could see the faint blue gleam on the ice at his feet. He could see nothing else.

Tick, went the axe, and tick. He put his boots where hers had been. His fingertips lay cold and dead inside the wool, and would for the best part of the hour. He did not think of the lie. He thought of her two fingers laid against his coat, and the warmth of them. He had never been so glad of a wind in his life, because it let him pretend the shake in his breath was the cold.

*It matters more than anything.*

He had said it aloud, to a surveyor, on a shelf of open rock, and the stones had sung it low and true. It could not be called back. It was a line already, whether she drew it or not, and anyone who knew how to read could take a bearing off that sentence and find where he was soft. His uncle would have found it in a breath.

Had he given her a gift, or a handle?

He did not know. The doubt came up cold through the soles of his boots, and he walked on it. He had taught himself for twenty years that a truth was a weapon you either held or were cut by. Now he had set one down in her hand, hilt first, and he did not know which of them he had trusted. And what did she keep against her ribs, with her palm flat over it, that she would not name?

Tick. Tick. The lamps went by, blue and dim. By his count they had gone halfway, perhaps a little more. By hers, he was sure, it would be exact.

The wind dropped for a breath. In the lull he heard the ice.

It was a low note first, far down, as if the lake were clearing its throat. Then it rose through the soles of his feet and the bones of his legs, and the whole white plate under them boomed, one great struck-drum note that rolled out to every shore and came back doubled.

Ila stopped dead.

She did not turn. She did not look at him. She stood with the axe haft lifted off the ice, her head tipped, her whole body gone to listening, from her boots to the snow on her hood.

"Don't move," she said.

# Chapter 14: Orders

Ila did not move, and behind her Kunal did not either. The rope went slack and stayed slack, which told her he had stopped dead on her left foot's track, as promised.

The boom rolled away to the shores and came back doubled. Then she heard the other sound under it.

It was a thin, rising crackle, like a long strip of cloth being torn very slowly. It came from ahead and a little left, and it ran. She turned her head to follow it. It slid across the plate in front of them from left to right, and it did not fade the way an echo fades. It crossed the winter line a dozen paces on.

"Pressure line," said Ila.

"What?"

"The ice is pushing against itself. The cold has locked the plate and the wind is working it, so it cracks along a seam." She found she was whispering. "The boom is the lake letting go. That's what we heard. The crackle is where."

"The lamps run straight through it."

"I know."

The lit line was a good road. It had been laid for the mountain's purposes, which were to keep two bound people off the cliffs and out of the whiteout. The mountain had never promised them dry feet. She tapped the haft against the ice, and it rang clear. She took two steps forward and tapped again, and the note went flat and dull, like a spoon on a sack.

"Stay here," she said, and went on to the end of her track with the rope paying out behind her. Tick. Dull. Tick. Dull. At four paces the dullness lifted, and the note went bright again, and she was a long way past what she'd have called safe. Slush and old fracture ran under there. She could hear it in the wood.

She worked it as she would have worked a river ford. The seam was a ridge of broken ice that had heaved and frozen again. It was thin at its crown, where the lamps crossed it. The plate was thicker to either side, because the heave had stacked the old floes there. A thick edge, then. Somewhere along it the sound would turn clean.

She went left. Forty paces, tapping. The wind tore at her hood. Behind her the rope hummed along the ice as Kunal walked her track, and she did not look round, because she did not need to. He did exactly what she had said. It was a very odd thing to be trusted so entirely.

"Here," said Ila.

The note rang. She tapped again a pace to the right, and a pace further, and each answer came back bright and long, the sound of ice you could build a house on. She laid the haft down flat across the line of the crack, and it spanned the seam with a hand's breadth to spare on either side.

"Narrow," said Kunal.

"A step and a half, and a ridge in the middle. Put your feet where mine go. Don't look down. There's nothing to see, only a bit of grey."

"I'm not looking down."

"You're looking at me."

"Yes."

She went over. The ridge under her boots groaned and settled, and the far side took her weight with a sound like a door closing. She turned, and he was a dark shape in the white, with his hand out for the rope. He crossed in one long stride, his boots exactly in her marks. On the other side he stood a moment with his chest going.

"The lamps are over there," he said. "Forty paces."

"We'll go back to them past the seam. We've no use for them where they cross it."

"You tapped the whole way."

"I told you I would."

He laughed. It was short and surprised, and the wind took it. Then the storm came down in earnest, and there was nothing to do but walk.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They crossed the rest of the Mirror in a white roar, with the cairn-lights guttering blue at their feet. When the far shore came up, it was a darker grey in the grey, a slope of scree with a black bulk above it. Kunal said something she could not hear. He took her elbow and turned her left, and a few hundred paces on a wall stood in the snow, low, square, and roofed with slabs.

"Dhanu's," he shouted.

It was a stone hut with a door of split deodar and a smoke hole in the roof. Inside, it was black and bitterly still, and the silence rang after the wind. Kunal found a flint at the back of a shelf and struck it. The first spark showed her a chulha built of river stones, its mouth scraped clean, and beside it a pile of stunted juniper and dung cakes laid up to the rafters. There was one sleeping shelf, a plank wide enough for two if they were careful, with a folded blanket on it. A line of jars stood above the hearth. She read them by the lamp as it caught: barley meal, salt, dried apricots, a wrapped brick of tea.

"Dhanu stocked it," said Kunal. "He came the short way, with Gauri and the child. He'd want the pot used."

"He's thought of everything."

"He's thought of what he'd want, which is the same." He was already kneeling at the hearth, laying kindling with those scraped, careful hands. She saw him flex his fingers. The wax was gone from the nails. It was an hour and more since the stones had rung for him.

Ila stood in the door and let the storm go on without her.

The lakes were out there. She could not see them. She could hear the third one, far off, booming through the snow, a deep note that shook the doorframe in her hand. Three frozen tarns. She had measured the first by pacing and two bearings off the Raven Spur, and now she stood in the middle of them, exactly where the letter said.

*Open when you stand at the Glass Lakes. Not before. S.*

Her hand had gone to her ribs without asking. The paper was a flat dry square against her side, its edges plain through the wool. She had carried it for more than two weeks. She knew every thread of the stitching that held it.

It was a queen's letter, and the queen had named this place. She had not named it to Kunal, or to the council, or in the commission Ila had signed. She had named it only to her, in a fold of paper, with a thread of blood in the wax.

Open it. She was standing at the Glass Lakes. The condition had been met.

And she knew, with a clean cold certainty that she did not like, that the first thing the letter would do was tell her what she had been sent to find. Then she could not un-know it. Then it would be a line on a sheet before her pencil moved.

Kunal had walked her down the Stair and the Ladder and the Hollow Moon, one way at a time. He had given her names and bearings and a staircase of ice she had not drawn. He had said *it matters more than anything* with his fingertips going white. Where did his road go next? She wanted to see it. She wanted to walk it with her own eyes before the queen's hand came between them.

It was not a good reason, and she knew it. She shut the door on the wind.

"Soup," said Kunal. "If you'll hold the pot."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They ate in the red light of the chulha, sitting on the floor with their backs against the shelf. The barley was thin and salty, and she thought it the best thing she had ever put in her mouth. Kunal had spread the wet wool to steam. His hair was dark with snow-melt, and he had a smear of ash on his cheekbone that she did not tell him about.

He gave her the night's name first. That was the order of it. He gave it with his eyes on the fire.

"The seam you stopped for. The families call it the Sleeper's Spine. When it cracks, they say the sleeper's turning over. They don't cross it with a laden mule." He glanced at her. "You'd have known it for what it was. Nobody told me you read ice."

"Nobody told me you'd walk on it with your eyes shut."

"I had them open."

"You said you weren't looking down."

"I wasn't." His mouth moved. "I'd have told you if I was frightened. I'm not so good at lying. I've learned that this week."

She wrote it down. *Sleeper's Spine.* The pencil lead was one of his, still warm from his coat, and the line came out clear and black. Her hand was steady and pink in the firelight, and she looked at it for a moment, and then at the page.

"Your question," said Ila.

It was the rule. One name from him, one answer from her, for anything she knew, or a refusal that cost nothing. She had never once refused. Nothing he had asked had been worth the price of the silence.

He turned the bowl in his hands. He did not look at her. She saw him decide.

"Do your orders name these lakes?"

It came very evenly, and she heard everything under it.

She had known he would ask. She had known it since the Ladder, if she was honest, and in the cave before that. It was the question his whole winter had been walking toward, and it was a fair one. He had given her the Glass Lakes freely. She had done nothing to earn it except not drawing a staircase. He wanted to know what he had bought.

The letter named them. *Open when you stand at the Glass Lakes.* The words were in a woman's hand, folded against her ribs. She had only to say so.

And he would stop.

She saw it plainly, as she might have seen a road in a survey. She would say *yes*, and his face would close. He would be courteous. He would be so courteous. He had said he'd not ask her to carry his people's ways at the cost of her own, and he meant it, and that was why he would turn them round. He would take her back across the Mirror to Ewe's Rest in the morning, and the lakes would be a sound she'd heard once through a storm. And the letter would stay sealed in her coat, a thing she carried and could not read.

Refusal was free. *I'll not answer that.* But he would read it, because he was Kunal, and a refusal from her on this was an answer.

"No," said Ila.

It came out level. It was a small word. She had said it a hundred times in her life, to clerks and cooks and men who wanted her pencil.

For a moment nothing happened at all.

Kunal's shoulders came down. She watched them do it. He let out a long breath through his teeth and shut his eyes, and he laughed, softly, as at a joke on himself.

"Good," he said. "I'd hoped."

"You'd hoped?"

"All winter. I'd stopped believing it." He opened his eyes, bright and a little wet in the fire. "I thought, however I led you here, I'd be leading you with a knife at my back. Your orders were the first thing I asked you. They're the last thing I've needed to hear. And you said no." He shook his head. "It's very small to be so large."

She said, "Yes."

That was true. It was the only true thing she had said in a minute, and she held to it.

He reached over and took the empty bowl from her lap, gently, as he'd have taken a dropped pencil, and set it by the hearth. His fingers brushed her wrist. They were warm. She wanted to say something, and her mouth would not make the shape.

Then the cold came.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It began in her fingertips, as it always began. She had read about it. She had stood in the hall at Garh Neshar while Meera, who had lost two fingers to one lie, told it flatly: the hands first, then the feet and ears. Ila had thought she understood. She had measured it as a quantity, a size of lie against a size of loss.

It came up under the nails like a needle drawn through, and then like a hundred. She looked at her right hand, resting on her knee, and it looked the same. She flexed it, and it did not feel like hers.

It was not the cold of the hut. The chulha was roaring now, and the stones were heating, and her cheeks stung with warmth. This cold sat inside her fingers like a thing set there. It ate its way up from the tips. Her ears began to burn, and then to prickle, and then, worse, to go quiet, and she put a hand up and found the rim of one numb as wax.

*Middling*, she thought. It was a clerk's word. A middling lie. Frostnip, and a night of it. She had seen her own face in the rule and had not liked the mirror.

She shifted closer to the fire. It did nothing. She held both hands out to the coals until her knuckles ached with the heat, and the cold stayed, bright and exact, under the skin. A shudder took her at the shoulders. She clenched her teeth to hold it, and it went down her back instead.

"Ila."

"I'm all right."

"You're shaking."

"It's the storm. I've been wet since the scree." She heard her own teeth. "I'm cold, Kunal. That's all. The fire hasn't got into the wool yet."

The cold answered before he did. It slid up her fingers like water up a wick, past the first knuckle, and stopped there with a hard small ache. She had paid for that, and she knew exactly what coin. She had bought nothing with it. She had only told another lie on top of the first and made it larger.

*A map is true or false*, she had said, at nineteen and at thirty. She had said it to her father and to the clerks and, once, to him, across a fire. Here was a line she had put on a sheet. It was not a mistake. She had done it with her eyes open, and she could not rub it out.

She pulled the pattu round her shoulders and held it shut with both fists. Her knuckles had gone from pink to a dead cream. She tucked the fists in against her ribs, where the letter lay, and bent over them.

Kunal had gone very quiet.

He was sitting with the pot in his hands. It was the iron pot, still half full, the last of the soup he'd put by for the morning. He had been about to set it on the hearth. He did not move. She heard the fire ticking in the dung cakes, and the third lake booming far away, and her own breath, ragged and loud.

"Ila," he said again.

"It's nothing."

The white climbed again, a slow cold step, to the second knuckle. She felt it go. She bit down on a sound and did not let it out.

"Show me your hands."

"I said—"

"You said you were cold." He said it gently. "Then it'll cost you nothing to show me."

She could have refused. It was free. She knew it, and she knew what the refusal would say, and she was too tired and too shaken to build the sentence that would make it say something else. Slowly she uncurled her fists and held them out in the firelight, palms down, as she had held them out above the fire at the foot of the Ladder.

They were white. It had climbed. The white went past the tips and past the first knuckle, up to the second, a clean hard line like the edge of a map, and below the line her skin was livid. The fire lay along it and did nothing at all. She watched his face as he looked. It did not change, and that was the worst of it. He looked the way a man looks at an old, true thing he has been reading for some time and has only now seen the date on.

He set down the pot.

# Chapter 15: Taking the Cold

Kunal took her hands.

He did it before she could close them. He had set the pot down so he would have both hands free, and he did not spend a breath on whether he should. He folded her fists open and wrapped them in his, palm to palm.

"Don't," said Ila. "Kunal, don't. You'll know."

"I know where to find the answer. Hold still."

He had learned the loophole at six, the way every hill child did, from a grandmother who told it as a warning and a lullaby together. *The one who was lied to can take the cold. Skin to skin. And then they know.* Children played at it in the lanes with snow and a ring of stones. Nobody had told him what it would be to do it with a woman who had just said *no* in a level voice and gone white to the knuckle.

For the last minute he had been holding a small, shabby doubt. She had said *the storm*. She had said *I've been wet since the scree*. A frostnipped hand looks much like a lie-cold hand, if a man wants it to. He had wanted it to. He had sat there with her hands on display and let the fire's failure to warm them mean nothing at all.

Touching her would end that. He knew it, and he held on anyway.

The cold came up his arms like the Mirror in spring, when you go through to the elbow.

It was not hers any more. It was his. It went into his fingertips first and hit the bone, and then it climbed. With it came the knowing, plain as a name read off a stone. A lie. Not small. Middling, the weight that costs a night. And it had been told tonight, to him, in answer to the only thing he had asked her.

*Do your orders name these lakes?*

*No.*

The fire cracked in the dung cakes. Kunal sat very still with her hands going warm between his, pink coming back under the nails, and felt his own go dead.

He did not learn the truth. That was the cruelty of it, and the mercy. He knew only the shape of what she had hidden, like the hollow in snow where something has lain. He could not tell what her orders said, or why she had put a lie over them. He knew he had been lied to, and how much it had mattered, and that was all.

"You've gone grey," said Ila. Her voice cracked on it. She stared at their hands. Hers were pink and shaking and plainly her own. His were the colour of tallow. "Let go. You can't take it all. It's too much, you'll—"

"It's a night's shaking. Nimmi would call it a nuisance."

"That's not what Meera said about—"

"Meera told you about a great lie." He held on. "This isn't one."

"It's still mine."

"It was yours until I asked." He heard how hoarse he'd gone. "I could have let you keep it. I'd have known you lied from your hands, as I did. The doubt would have stayed clean. I could have gone to sleep tonight not knowing what you'd said to me." He tried to smile. "I seem to like my ignorance less than I thought."

"Kunal."

The first shudder took him at the shoulders and ran down his spine. His teeth went. He tried to say something dry about it, and his tongue would not shape the words.

Ila said something. He did not catch it. Then there was wool around him, the grey pattu, and her arms, and he was pulled in against her side with his head on her shoulder. She was so warm. It was absurd. It was the plain warmth of a body, the heat that had left her an hour before and had come back, and it was lent to him now with both arms and no argument.

"Don't talk," said Ila into his hair.

"I want to say—"

"Not now."

"I'd rather say it—"

"Morning." Her arms tightened. "If I tell you now it'll be to stop you shaking. I won't do that to the truth. Tell me you can wait till it's light."

He lay in the circle of her arms, and the cold went through him in long waves. In the gaps between them he understood what he had done, as a man understands a fall on the way down. He had taken the lie into his own flesh and, with it, the knowledge of it. He now knew that his bound partner had lied about her orders, and he had chosen that. Nobody had made him. A smaller man would have stood at the edge of the doubt and warmed his hands at it. His uncle would have.

*Whoever knows the way owns the people.* And what did knowing her cost? He could not say. He had never bought anything so dear on purpose.

"I can wait," said Kunal, and the shaking took him.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He did not sleep, or not for long. The shudders came in rounds, each worse than the last, and between them the world went small and bright: the red mouth of the chulha, the smoke finding its hole, a seam of cold air under the door. Ila fed the fire without letting go of him. She had found a way to stretch and reach the dung cakes with one arm while the other kept him. Twice she wedged more of Dhanu's blanket round his feet. Once he woke with his face in her neck and her bare hand flat against his chest.

"Your heart's going like a goat's," she said. She sounded wrung dry.

"Yours?"

"Steady. I counted it."

"Of course you did."

She laughed, once, a cracked sound with no joy in it. Her breath shook. He had never heard her laugh so low. After that she said nothing for a long time, and he lay in her arms with his teeth going like dice and knew, quite clearly, that she was crying, and that she did not want him to say so.

He did not.

By the time the hut's seams went grey, the shaking had thinned to a tremor. His hands were stiff and prickling, flesh again to the last knuckle, though the tips stayed wax. The wind had gone. In its place was a vast listening quiet, and under it, once, far off, the long boom of the third lake letting go of the night.

Ila sat up slowly. She wrapped the pattu round them both and sat cross-legged in front of him, hands on her knees, palms up, in the dim.

"Watch my hands," she said.

It was a thing hill people said. He had said it himself. He looked, and they lay open and pink in the first light, the nails clean, the knuckles easy.

"The letter names the Glass Lakes," said Ila.

There were no stones here to sing. Her hands did not change.

"My commission says only 'every way.' That's all of it. But there is a letter, in the queen's own hand, sealed. It's sewn in my coat, here." Her hand went flat to her ribs. "The fold says to open it when I stand at the Glass Lakes. Not before. I took the letter for part of my orders, so when you asked, I said no, and it wasn't true. I don't know why she wants the lakes. I've carried it more than two weeks and haven't opened it."

Kunal looked at her hands. He looked a long while.

They were warm.

"Why," he said. He had meant to say it as a man would say a thing he'd rehearsed. It came out as a breath. "Why did you say no?"

"Because I was afraid you'd turn back." She did not look away. "You said yourself you would. If my orders named your lakes, you'd have walked me to Ewe's Rest at dawn and shut the Glass Lakes like a door. And I wanted to see where your way goes." Her mouth shook. "I wanted to walk it. Before the queen's hand came between us and told me what it was for. That's all. It's no better a reason than it sounds."

*I wanted to see where your way goes.* It was a surveyor's reason. It was also, he thought, the plainest thing anyone had said to him in thirty years.

Her hands stayed warm.

"You could have refused," said Kunal. "It's free."

"You'd have read it. A refusal on that question was an answer."

"Yes." He thought about it. "I would have."

"I know." She let out a breath. "I did the sum, and I did it wrong. I thought it was the lesser cost."

"It was." He turned his own hands over in his lap. The tips were still the colour of tallow, and he was glad to see them. "It cost you a night of this, and me one. And I've the truth now, which I'd choose over the quiet."

"Do you want the letter?" She said it very low. "I'll hand it to you sealed. I don't know what's in it. It may be a warrant. It may be a map. It may say that my commission is to hold your people's ways, and then I'll have to choose in front of you."

He might have said *give it to me*. Pritam would have said it, softly, with his hand already out.

"It's yours," said Kunal. "The queen wrote it to you."

"And if you're in it?"

"Then I'd like to be told in daylight." He tried a smile and found one. "With my teeth in."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The storm had spent itself in the night. When they came out, the sky over the lakes was scoured and high, the cold so clean it rang in the nose. Snow lay in long ribbed drifts along the shore, and the ice beyond was blown bare, a dark floor with the wind-curl still frozen into it.

They stood on the scree with their breath smoking. Ila had her field book out and was not writing in it.

"The second lake," she said. "What do people call it?"

He knew what she was doing. She was handing him the night's name early, on credit, because she could not yet stand to open anything else. He took it.

"The Drum."

"Because it booms."

"Because it's the loudest. My father said it was the heartbeat of the valley." He heard how it sounded. "My father said many foolish things at the Fair. That was one of the better."

She wrote it down in her small hard hand. *The Drum.* Then she shut the book and slung her pack.

"The winter line crosses it," she said. "I'd like to know what it crosses before we trust it. That's not a delay. It's what I'd do on any day of my life."

"Then do it."

The Drum lay a long half hour beyond the Mirror's far shore, over a lip of rock and down a shelf of drift. It was smaller than the first lake, a dark oval bounded by black cliff on one side, and its ice was not smooth. Seams cut across it in great pale lines, shore to shore, where the plate had heaved in the night.

"Boom lines," said Ila, kneeling at the edge. "Every one is where the lake let go. It lets go where it's weakest." She slid an iron spike out of her pack, as long as her forearm, with a ring welded in the head, the kind a surveyor drives to hold a chain. "Give me your knife."

He gave it. She laid the spike against the ice and set the point of his knife in the ring like a lever, and began to chip. It was slow work, the spike turning and the blade biting, and chips of white flew up and stung his cheek. In a quarter hour she had a hole as wide as her fist, and black water welled to the lip.

She dropped a length of cord with a knot in it down the hole. It sank, and caught under the ice, and she drew it up until the knot snagged. Then she held the spike beside it and counted along its length, her lips moving.

"Eleven fingers."

"Is that good?"

"It's a number." She wrote it. "A pair with packs wants eight. Three wants twelve. That's what I've always used on rivers, and it's never lied to me yet." She sat back on her heels. "Eleven holds the two of us with packs. It won't hold a third."

"We're only two."

"Raghu's men are two pairs. If they cross close, they're four, and whatever is behind them." She had her pencil out already. "I'd like to know where they'd go through."

He wanted to say that nobody was following them. He did not say it. There had been a spyglass on the Hollow Moon. He knelt beside her and took the spike when she held out her hand for it, and he drove it where she pointed, at the next seam, forty paces along, while she worked the knife.

It went like that all morning. She never hurried. At each boom line they cut a hole, and she dropped the cord, and she counted and wrote. *Nine. Ten. Fourteen on the thick side, under the cliff. Seven, here, where the black water showed through, and a cross marked over it.* He watched the numbers go down the page in her handwriting, small and square and sure. After the first hour her shoulders came down from her ears. After the second, she was humming under her breath, off key, a counting tune.

Whatever was in her coat, she had left it there. She had a lake to read, and the lake was honest.

*A pair but not three.* He found himself marking the places with her, a little behind, in the margin of his own head. A pair could cross where nine fingers lay. A pair could not cross where seven did. Where fourteen lay, you could march a column. His uncle's men would have given a year of their lives to buy such knowledge.

And she was writing it down in front of him, with her hand open on the page, and he had not once asked her to stop.

Perhaps that was what the night had bought him. He had taken her cold and paid it, and he had not turned into a man who guarded what he'd paid for. He turned the thought over carefully, as a man handles a bone he thinks has healed. It held.

At noon the sun came over the cliff and lit the Drum from end to end, a great dark oval stitched with white seams and Ila's small black holes. She stood at its centre with the spike in her hand and the last number in her book, steady. He could see it in the line of her back.

Then she turned, and the steadiness went. Her hand rose, as if of itself, to her coat.

"I've sounded what I can sound," said Ila. "I haven't another reason."

She said it to the ice. He walked across the plate to her, on her tracks, and stopped with the length of the rope between them slack.

"Open it," said Kunal.

She drew a long breath. She unpicked the stitches at her ribs with the point of his knife and drew out the folded square, thin and cream and stiff with wax. The seal was red, and when the light struck it a thread of darker red ran through it like a vein.

She looked at him once. Then she put her thumb under the wax and broke the seal.

# Chapter 16: The Grave Map

She did not unfold it.

The seal lay in two halves in her palm, red wax with its thread of darker red. The paper sat in her other hand, still folded in three. Ila looked down the length of the Drum at her own small black holes. Eleven fingers. Nine. Seven, with a cross. A lake was a place where numbers held. She did not think a letter would.

"Not out here," she said.

"No," said Kunal.

They walked back along her tracks, hole to hole, and she tapped the haft at every step although she had sounded it all morning. The rope lay slack between them. It was eight of her arm-spans, and he kept to the near end of it, close enough that she could hear his breathing. At the lip of rock above the shore she found a flat shelf, dry and out of the wind. She sat with her back to the cliff and the letter on her knee.

He did not sit. He stood two paces off with his hands hanging, as a man stands in a doorway when he has not been told to come in. His fingertips were still the colour of tallow. They had been that colour since dawn, because of her.

Ila was thirty-one years old. She had measured four mountains, two rivers and one man's grandfather's grave-field, and she had never once been at a loss for what to do next. She was at a loss now. So she did the thing in front of her.

"Give me your hands."

He gave them. She wrapped them in her own, and they were cold as river stones. She had thought she knew that cold. She had held it for an hour at nineteen fingers' depth, in a manner of speaking. This was only what was left.

"I'm going to kiss you," said Ila. "I'm saying it first so it's on the record. It's thanks. Some of it isn't thanks."

Something went across his face, quick and young. "Which part is which?"

"I'll let you work it out."

She kissed him. It was not graceful. She had to rise on her knees, and the shelf was narrow, and his hands were still folded in hers, so for a moment they were two people holding a bundle between them. His mouth was cold and then it was not. He made a small sound, low in the chest, as if he had been handed something he had stopped expecting. When she let go of his hands to take his collar, they came up and held her face, so carefully that she wanted to laugh. The laugh came out against his mouth and he felt it, and she felt him smile.

She drew back an inch. His eyes were open. He had snow-melt in his lashes.

"I promise nothing," said Ila. She said it plainly, because it was a rule of the place. "Not one thing. If that paper says what I'm afraid it says, I may stand up and draw a line you'd hate. I want you to know it before this is the last thing you remember."

"I wouldn't take a promise today. The rope would hold you to it."

"I know."

"Then why say it?"

"Because I'd rather you were angry at me honestly."

He was quiet. His thumbs moved once on her cheekbones. He was not looking at her hands or his own. Ila marked that. A week ago he would have glanced down at his fingertips for the colour.

"I don't know what you'll do with any of it," said Kunal. "The lakes. The numbers. Me. And I'd show you anyway."

It was not a speech. It came out the way her laugh did, before anyone had checked it. She looked, because she could not help looking, at his hands against her jaw. The nails were wax-pale from last night. They had not gone one shade whiter.

"That's true," said Ila.

"I know. I could feel it."

She sat back on her heels, and he let her go. She picked up the paper.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


*Ila,*

*If you are reading this you are at the Glass Lakes, and you have walked there beside him. I hoped it. I will not pretend I was sure.*

*Sixty-one winters ago the pass began to close. The archive holds the reason in one line, and I will give it to you plain, because nobody gave it to me plain. The price is a life, paid beneath the Shilak Cairn.*

*It was paid. Warden Harish of the Shilak post went down to the Cairn in the dead of that winter. Prince Udai of Neshar guided him, by the way that begins at these lakes. Prince Udai came home. Warden Harish did not.*

*He was your grandfather.*

*The Closing Law was written the next spring, so that no one would walk that way in winter and ask what it cost. I have kept that drawer thirty winters. I will not be the last to keep it unless you help me.*

*Draw the way and read the price before the fourth winter. Tell no one Neshar's part until it is found.*

*Sarala*

She read it once. Then she read it again, slowly, with her finger under the lines, as she read a bearing she meant to trust her life to.

The words did not go in at first. The paper was good, and the queen's hand was small and upright and steady. *Harish* was written with a long first stroke, the way a clerk lengthens a name he has written ten thousand times in other men's ledgers. She saw these things the way you see a stone in your boot on the way to a cliff.

*He was your grandfather.*

She had known the name. She had known it as she knew the name of the first king of Hemkot. It lay in her father's chronicle, in her father's careful clerk's hand, with a date. *Carried down at the thaw.* Her father had said it over his shoulder with his good eye on the page, and she had been a girl, and she had thought, what a tidy word.

*Carried.*

In the hamlets it meant seen across by the hand. In the post's chronicle it meant dead. He had been carried, then. Carried to where?

She remembered the gloves.

Her father had told it once, late, with a cup in him, and never again. Her grandfather had been found at the thaw, sitting whole against a flat stone on the slope above the third bend. His gloves lay on top of it, palms together, set square to the edge, the fingers pointing the way he had been walking. *Like a pair set out for the morning,* her father had said, and shaken his head, and let it go.

She had always thought it a sad picture. A neat man making a neat death. She had even liked it, a little.

It was not neat. It was a receipt.

A man who had been told where he was going, and what it cost, and who had gone anyway. He had taken off his gloves, because he would not need them, and laid them out where someone would find them. Where Rohan would one day find them, in a drunken story, and never work out why.

"Ila."

She had stopped breathing, or near it. She started again.

She hunted for the right words and found none that was not a measurement. Sixty-one winters. The Closing Law, written the next spring. Every smuggler hanged since for crossing a pass in the cold. Meera's mother. The ropes. Ranu's graves, thirty-two of them, in rows on a terraced slope. The whole law had been a lid, and she had spent her life drawing lines on the ground beneath it, and calling that neutral.

*A map is true or false.* She had said it at nineteen and at thirty. And here was the truest map she had ever been handed, and it was a grave map. Her own family's. She could not draw one line of it as a thing with no owner.

Then the other half struck her, and she looked up at him.

*Prince Udai of Neshar guided him.*

Kunal was watching her. He had not moved. His face wore the careful, courteous quiet of a man who has made himself wait. Behind him the Drum lay dark and white and open, and she thought of what he had said at the broken door cairn on the Hollow Moon, as lightly as he might say the time. *Sixty winters ago, in the old women's reckoning. My grandfather walked the princes' way in winter with a guest.* An unnamed guest. The year before the Closing Law.

*I'm counting,* she had told him. He had taken it for arithmetic.

It had been arithmetic. It had come out at a man's name.

She went through it as she would have gone through a field sheet, point by point, and every point turned over under her hand and showed its other face. His family's secret ways, never written, never said to anyone below the passes. *Whoever knows the way owns the people.* The hill council's route-keepers, burned out of their huts. Raghu's men digging nine holes in the snow round a toppled cairn, hunting a carved marker that had never existed. Kunal's own voice, with his fingertips going white at the Mirror: *it matters more than anything.*

She had thought he was guarding his people's road. He was, but he was guarding something else with it. Perhaps he did not know it himself. The way began at these lakes and ended beneath a cairn, and at the end of it was a man's bones, or a debt, or a door. The secrecy that kept his people free also kept a grave unmapped.

Her neutral map was a family grave map. His closed hand was a hand over a grave.

And neither of them knew, except the one holding the letter.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She folded it along its creases. It took three tries. Her hands were pink and steady, and she looked at them, because that was a thing she did now.

She weighed it. She sat on the shelf with the cliff at her back, and she weighed it in the open, as he had taught her to weigh a name.

She could tell him. *Your grandfather walked mine to his death.* She could put the letter in his hands, sealed or unsealed, and watch his face. She had sworn the archive oath to take her commission, on a long gold-edged page in a hall in Taravan, and it bound her to silence about whatever the crown's papers held. Taking it had not cost her a night's sleep. Now the oath had a shape. The queen had added a line of her own on top of it: *Tell no one Neshar's part.* She did not owe that line her obedience. She owed it, she thought, a hearing.

And she could not tell Kunal what his own grandfather had done, until she knew what it was. A man could walk another man down a mountain and come home. It might have been a crime. It might have been a mercy. The letter had no word for which. Udai had been one of two men on a mountain sixty-one winters ago, and only one had come back to say.

She would be accusing him before she had any right to. She would be turning a prince's grandfather into a murderer on a queen's say-so, in front of a man who had, an hour ago, put his open hands on her face.

Silence was free. She turned that over too, as coldly as she could. She knew how it would look from the other side, how it would read, since she had taught herself last night to read it. A refusal on this question was an answer, and the answer was *yes, there is something.* But *something* was true. She could not make it smaller. The rope would not make her pay for it.

Under the rope, silence was free. She hated that it was.

"Ila," said Kunal again, very quietly. "What does it say?"

He did not move closer. He had settled on his heels at the rim of the shelf, and the rope lay slack across the snow between them.

She said, "It says that I'm to find where your way goes. And read what's written there." She watched her own hands. They stayed pink. "That's the most of it. The rest I can't tell you yet."

It was true. It went through the cold air as a plain thing, and the stones above the shore did not sing. She kept her eyes on his face, because she owed him that.

He looked at her hands. He did it openly, and it cost him to do it openly, and she saw the cost. It went across him in a long breath. He had taken her cold last night. He knew precisely how a lie looked on her skin. He knew, now, how a true refusal looked, and it looked like nothing at all. It looked exactly like a closed door.

*Can't tell you yet.* He turned the words over, the way she did with a bearing. She could almost see him count the ways it might go. A warrant. A garrison. A line across the Under-whatever his grandmothers had never named. Pritam would have asked again, softly, and kept asking. Kunal's hand moved to his knee and stayed there.

"You said 'yet,'" he said.

"I did."

"That's a thing you'd say if you meant it."

"I mean it."

"Yes." He let out a breath. "Your hands say so."

She had thought he might argue, or be angry, or turn courteous again. He did none of those things. He sat on his heels at the edge of the shelf, with the Drum behind him and the whole white morning on his shoulders, and set something down. She watched him do it. It was the size of a mountain. He set it down quietly, as a man puts a sleeping child on a bed.

He had been born to the principle that a secret kept was a life kept. He had been shown, with her hands held out in the firelight, what it was to be on the other side of one. He was choosing, anyway. It was not trust, exactly, and not simple belief. It was something that had to be built again each time, with a hole in the middle where the answer should have been.

It was the most generous thing she had ever watched anyone do.

And she was sitting on the answer.

*He guided him.* The sentence stood in her mind with its long clerk's stroke. *Prince Udai of Neshar guided him, by the way that begins at these lakes.* The trouble was not whether Kunal's grandfather had done a terrible thing. It was that Kunal did not know there was a thing to do. All his life he had been told the princes' way was a gift to the valley, a road for the hill people when the crown closed the passes. Not once had anyone told him it was a road a man walked another man along, to a stone, and left him.

She held the letter flat against her coat. It was a very small piece of paper.

"Kunal," she said. "Whatever's there, I'll tell you the first moment I can. I'm not making that a promise. The rope would hold me to it, and I don't know that I can keep it."

"I know."

"I'd rather you were angry at me honestly."

"You said that." The corner of his mouth moved. "Do you think I'll be angry?"

"I think you'll be right to be."

He got up. He did it slowly, and offered her his hand, and she took it, and he drew her to her feet on the narrow shelf. They stood very close. The wind had dropped, and the cliff stood over them with its black seams. She looked at his face and saw that it was perfectly steady. His eyes were bright, and not with anger.

"The way begins at the head of these lakes," said Kunal. "It's mine to show. It was my grandfather's, and he'd have said it wasn't his to show either." His hand tightened once on hers and let go. "I won't make you swear to what you'll do with it. I haven't the right, and you've said you'd not give it. But you came here to find where it goes. So."

He turned to the east, where the third lake lay hidden behind its spur, booming softly, a deep note that came up through the rock into her boots.

"Then I'll take you."

She said nothing. She could not say anything that was both kind and true, and she had learned this week what the second kind cost.

She knew what his family had done, and he did not. And he had just offered, with his open hands and his steady face, to walk her down the road where his grandfather had walked hers.

She folded the letter into her coat and tied the stitches shut with a shaking hand, and went after him.

# Chapter 17: The Shape of a Silence

Ila had not looked him in the eye since the shelf above the Drum.

She looked at everything else. She looked at the roof poles of Dhanu's upper hut, the smoke hole, the juniper stack, the grain of the split-deodar door with the wind leaning on it. When Kunal fed the chulha, she looked at his hands. When he passed her the tea, she took the cup by its rim, so their fingers never met, and watched his hands go away again.

Her own stayed where he could see them. They lay open on her knees, or flat on the blanket, or spread on the sleeping shelf like a woman showing a clerk she carried nothing.

The storm had caught them an hour past the lip, a white wall coming over the spurs faster than her pocket watch allowed. They had made the hut with the rope hauled taut between them and the third lake still unseen. That was a day and a half ago. The wind had not stopped since, and the stones outside sang in it, a low note with no meaning in it.

Kunal had learned her silence the way he learned a new road. It had edges. It began exactly where the kiss ended. She talked well enough about the weather, the ice, the thickness of the hut walls, which she had paced twice and found a hand narrower than the north side. She answered whatever he asked her. But when he asked anything that came near the shelf, her eyes dropped to his hands and stayed there.

By the second morning he had a theory, and he disliked it.

She was ashamed of the kiss. She had been cold and grateful and wrung out after a night in which he had taken her lie onto his skin, and she had paid him the only coin she had. Now the coin sat between them, and she was too honest to take it back and too kind to say so.

He thought about it while he did the one useful thing the storm allowed, which was the petition.

It was four sheets, folded in his coat, for the thaw council. He wrote it on his knee with one of her pencil leads, warmed against his chest and held in his fist like a coal. The hill council wanted a seat at Taravan, and the keeping of the high ways under the new law, and no crown garrison in any hill village. Every clause was a thing he had said aloud at a long table. None had cost him anything to write until this week.

He wrote *the high ways* and stopped. He knew what the clause meant. He had been looking at three days of Ila's field book, and for the first time in his life he understood that "ways" was a word you could hand someone, and that they could draw it.

He finished the line. Then he put the lead down and said, to the roof poles, "If it was the cold talking—"

"It wasn't."

She said it before he reached the verb. She was crouched at the door, chipping ice into the water pot with the back of her axe, and her head did not turn. But her hands had stopped.

He looked, because he could not help it. They were pink. The nails were clean, and the knuckles were easy, and they were hers.

It was true, then. And it left him with less than he had come in with, because the theory had been tidy and the truth was not. If she did not regret it, and was not cold, and was not ashamed, then there was a wall, and he could see it plainly. He could not name a single stone.

"Good," said Kunal. He found a smile and put it on. "I'd hate to be the sort of man who was kissed by weather."

She laughed. It was the short, loud one, cut off at once, and it startled the water pot. Then her gaze went to his hands again, and the quiet came back down.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wind dropped a little after dark, enough that they could hear the lakes through it. Far off the Drum boomed, one slow note, and then another answered it from somewhere under the cliff.

They had the sleeping shelf to share and one blanket's worth of room on it. The first night, they had lain like two planks. Tonight Kunal banked the fire and came back along the rope, and Ila was sitting up against the wall with her knees drawn in.

"Your grandfather," she said. "Prince Udai." She said it carefully, like a woman setting a foot on rotten ice. "You said he walked the way in winter. What was he like?"

It was the first thing she had asked him in a day that was not about a measurement. He sat down at the shelf's end, a hand's breadth from her boots.

"I don't know. He died when I was four." He thought about it. "I have a beard that smelled of apricot smoke, and being lifted onto a wall to watch the Fair. That's all of him I own. The rest is my uncle's."

"What did your uncle say?"

"He said it at supper, the autumn I was nine. My father was a week in the ground." Kunal turned his hands over. "Pritam likes a lesson to arrive with a meal. He said my grandfather had shown the way once, to one guest, in winter, and was not the same man after. Then he put down his knife. *The way kills those who share it,* he said. 'Remember that, boy.'" He heard how light his voice had gone. "That was the whole story. The lesson's on the end like a rope on a post."

"And the rule?"

"My grandfather's rule was only for winter. My uncle made this one that night, over the fish. No prince shows the way to wife or son or friend. It stays in the head of one man at a time." He tried the smile again. "It's a very good rule. Between them, the two have kept the crown out of Neshar's hills for sixty winters."

Ila had gone still. It was a particular stillness, the kind that came over her at a bearing that would not close. Her eyes were on his hands.

"You've gone quiet," said Kunal. "It's a tale for frightening boys. I've shown you three ways in twenty days, and you're alive."

"Yes," said Ila. "I am."

It was not an answer. He put it with the others.

"The night's name," he said. "You measured the long seam at the cliff foot, where the plate rings. Hill people call it Where the Drum Answers."

She had her field book out already. "Because it echoes?"

"Because when it answers, you lie still till morning. You never cross on a night the Drum answers." He watched her write it, small and hard. "My nurse used to say it to me. *Where the Drum answers, lie still till morning.* It was how she got me to sleep."

Her pencil stopped on the last word. Then it finished, neatly, and she shut the book.

"Your question," said Kunal. "I've a name given, so I've a question owed. Or you may refuse."

"I know the terms."

"I've been choosing it all day."

She did not move. He thought of the other questions he could have asked. *What does the letter say. What did you mean by yet. Why can't you look at me.* Any of them was fair. Any might have been the one that mattered. But each, he had found, could be answered with a shrug and a word like *warrant*, and he would never know whether he had been told the truth or only not been lied to.

So he asked the narrowest thing he could find.

"Is my family in your letter?"

The fire ticked. In the stones outside the wind rose, and sank.

Ila's eyes stayed on his hands. Her own lay open on her knees, and he watched them as he had watched them for a week, from the angle where he could see all ten nails. They were pink. They stayed pink.

"I'm not going to answer that," said Ila.

It was free. He knew the exact weight of it. He had read her refusal on the Drum, and had told her himself that a refusal on a close question was an answer. He had said it with his own mouth and thought himself clever.

It was an answer. It said that there was something in the letter that touched his family and that she would not say. It could mean a hundred things. It could mean Pritam, and a warrant, and a garrison on the road to Garh. It could mean his father's debts, or his mother's name, or a line in a ledger from before his birth.

He sat with it. The word *family* had Pritam's face on it, because everything did.

"Then I'll take it as it stands," said Kunal. "Not as a yes."

Her head came up, a little. Not to his eyes. To his collar.

"I can't help what I infer," he said. "I'm not a stone. But I'll put it down and not build on it. If I find out otherwise, I'll find out in daylight, from you." He heard the dryness in it and let it stay. "I'd like it noted that this is a considerable feat for a man raised by my uncle."

"Noted," said Ila. Her voice had thinned. "Kunal, it's more than I've earned."

"Probably." He looked at her open hands. "But it's cheaper than the alternative, and I've lately found I'm thrifty."

She made a sound that was nearly a laugh, and nearly something else. She slid down along the wall and lay on her side with her back to him, and the length of the rope lay between them across the blanket. After a while, without turning, she put one hand out behind her, palm up, on the boards.

It asked for nothing. She was only leaving it where he could see it.

He lay down beside it and did not touch it. He looked at it in the red light until the fire went to coals. It was warm and steady, and it was hers, and it did not tell him one thing he wanted to know.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The third day brought nothing but the count.

He took the petition out again at first light and ruled the margin with the edge of the pencil case, a line for each day. He had started it the night of the binding, thirty-five days to the Meadow Cairn's meltwater in a soft year, with Shilak's thaw council somewhere after, when the crown's men and the hill's would sit down at one table and read what had been written. He had thought of it as a distance. Now he laid it out in strokes and counted them.

Twenty. Twenty strokes, and fifteen empty places.

He had gone into the winter wanting two things, and each had a number on it. The first was that thaw council. The second was the unmapped hills, still his, when it came. He was beginning to understand that the two might not live in the same house.

When he looked up, Ila was at the door with her eye to the crack, one hand flat on the wood. She had been there some time.

"It's easing," she said. "The pitch has changed."

She opened it a hand's width. Light came in white and hard, and the cold with it, and the singing in the stones thinned to a thread and stopped. Beyond the scree the Mirror lay blown bare and shining. The sky had been wiped.

They stood in the door together with their breath going up. Neither spoke, and he found that he did not want to, because he knew what the next hour was for.

In an hour they would walk east along the shore, past the Drum, to the head of the third lake. She would unfold her field sheet and ask him for a bearing, and he would have to say what he had never said aloud to anyone.

He had decided it in the night, somewhere between the hand on the boards and the grey light. It was not a trade, and she had not asked. That was why.

"The way goes in at the head of the third lake," said Kunal.

She turned her head. Her eyes came to his face for the first time in two days and stopped at his chin.

"There's a cleft in the cliff where the ice meets the rock. Hill people call the lake the Eye. It's the last, and it's the deepest, and the cleft is the pupil." He kept his voice level. "That's where the princes' way enters the mountain. I've never been inside it past the first turn."

"Never?"

"My uncle took me to the first turn when I was twelve. He stood me against the rock and turned me round himself, and walked me out." He made himself say the rest. "He said the remainder was for a prince who had earned it. I took that to mean him. I've never been told what it meant."

The cold came in at the door. She said nothing. The pencil was in her fist, and she had not opened her field book.

"I'm telling you because we are going there, and I'd rather you walked in knowing how little I know than how much I'd like you to think I do." He found he had put his hands out, palms up, in the light. He had not meant to. "Past the first turn I've no more than you. It's all true. You can look."

She looked.

He saw her do it. Her eyes went to his hands and then to the sky behind him, and then, at last, to his eyes, full on, and it was like a door opening onto a room with no floor. There was grief in it. He could see that much and no more. He could not tell whose it was, or what for.

"Thank you," said Ila. She said it very low.

She did not write the Eye down. He noticed, and did not know what to make of it. She stood in the white doorway with her pencil shut in her hand, and her eyes went east along the shore to where the third lake waited behind its spur, and then, quickly, back to his hands.

# Chapter 18: The Eye

The ice on the Eye rang clear, and Ila trusted it about as far as she could throw it.

She tapped her axe haft along the shore for a hundred paces, then knelt and chipped a sounding hole with the survey spike and Kunal's knife. Fourteen fingers. Fifty paces on, sixteen. Near the middle, where the lake was deepest, the ice ran so clear that her hole went down like a blue throat.

"Well?" said Kunal.

"It'll carry us. It won't carry us kindly."

The storm had wiped the sky, and the morning was so still that she could hear her own pulse in her ears. The third lake lay long and dark between steep walls. The Drum was behind them, and the Mirror behind that. At the head of it, where the ice met a black cliff, was the cleft.

It was a slot as wide as a man's shoulders and twice his height. A curtain of blue ice hung from its lintel, half drawn, like a lid over an eye. Somewhere deep inside, water ticked on stone, slow and patient, like a clock in another house.

She stopped fifty paces short and let the rope go slack.

Low in the dark, a long way in, there was light. It was faint and blue, and steady as a lamp left burning in a far room. She had seen it on the Stair and on the Hollow Moon, strung out along the winter line. Cairn-light. It did not flicker. It simply was, and she understood that the mountain had put it there to be seen.

"You see it," she said.

"Yes." Kunal's voice came from behind her shoulder. He had stopped a pace short of where she stood. "I was twelve when he brought me here. There was no light. There was a hole in a cliff and my uncle's hand on the back of my neck."

"You weren't bound."

"No."

"So it shows itself to the bound." She said it flatly, to the ice. "The mountain marks it. It wants us to find it."

He did not answer. She looked round at last. He stood with his arms away from his body and his hands open, the way he had stood in the doorway of the hut. His feet had not moved since the last sounding. She knew that stance. It was a boy's, when a hand had been laid on the nape of his neck.

He had walked her to the one place in the world he was afraid of. He had done it on a morning when he had every reason to think she would draw it and sell it.

Ila sat down on a flat tongue of rock at the shore and took out her field book.

She drew the lakes first, because they were the part she knew. The Mirror, the Drum and the Eye lay one below the other like three beads on a string, and every number she had paid for in holes went down in her small hard figures. Then she took her bearings on the cleft from two points along the shore. She paced the ice to the lip. Eighty-eight paces. She wrote *88* and underlined it.

Then she said, without turning, "I'm drawing the mouth."

The wind had no voice. Kunal's boots shifted on the snow.

"Where it is, how wide, how high, and the light inside. All of it, to the pace. I'm telling you because the book goes where my commission sends it, unless I decide otherwise. I haven't decided. I won't promise you that I will."

"I know."

"I can draw it less true." She made herself turn and look at him, full in the face. "Move it a hundred paces. Leave off the light. People do it. Maps lie by being kind." Her pencil pointed at the slot. "Somebody will come here in a storm with a sheet in his hand, and he'll believe it. A sheet that's wrong is a grave with a tidy lid."

He thought it through while she watched. His hands stayed open. Under the courtesy he wore was the whole of what he had been taught. *Whoever knows the way owns the people.* A line on a crown sheet was an owner, and a line left off was a door shut.

Both values showed in his face at once, and neither was foolish.

"Draw it true," said Kunal.

It came out rough. He cleared his throat and said it again, quieter. "Draw it true, Ila. I'd rather a hundred men knew where it is than one went in the wrong place."

She did not say *thank you*, because it was not a gift. She turned back and wrote the bearing down, and her hand was steadier than her breath.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


To take the width of the mouth, she had to stand inside the lip.

She sounded the first pace of the shelf under the ice curtain, and the haft rang clean. She sounded the second and it rang clean. She stood with the rope slack behind her and weighed it, as she weighed everything.

"One in eight," said Ila.

"One in eight what?"

"That it's hollow under there. I can hear meltwater, and meltwater undercuts." She tugged the rope twice. "I'll go three paces. I'll take the width off the chain and come out."

"One in eight is a great deal of mountain."

"It's an eighth. It was one in five on the Hollow Moon and you didn't say a word."

"I said a word. You didn't hear it." He moved up behind her and took the rope in both hands. She saw the wrappings he had made, torn from his shirt, dark where the blisters had broken. "If you go through, I'll hold you."

"Hold. Don't haul." She looked at him over her shoulder. "If I drop, don't pull until I say. A rope dragged over a lip cuts. Let me climb."

"You want me to stand there while you hang."

"I want you to stand there while I climb."

He looked at her a long moment. Then he set his heels in the snow. "Go."

Under the blue curtain, the cold closed round her like a wet cloth. The ticking was suddenly louder, and the cairn-light lay ahead, calm and low. One pace. Two. Her axe haft rang, then rang again.

On the third pace it did not ring.

It gave a short dull note, like a spoon on a pillow. She knew what it meant, and her weight had already gone onto the foot.
