The shelf went with a sound like a dropped plate. She dropped through to the armpits, and her chin cracked on the edge. The rope snapped taut across her ribs and wrenched the knot on her wrist. Under her boots there was nothing at all, only a cold breath coming up from far below, smelling of stone and water.

The survey spike slid out of her belt. She heard it fall. One, two, three. Then a small bright sound, very far away.

"Hold," said Ila.

"Holding."

His voice was ten paces behind her and perfectly level. The rope did not move. It might have been tied to the cliff.

She swung her axe with her right arm and sank the pick into the ice wall at the back of the mouth. It bit. She tried it with her weight, and it held. She kicked, found a crack with the toe of her boot, and pushed.

"Don't pull."

"I'm not."

What it cost him was plain in the sound of him. His breath came harsh through his nose. Every muscle in him must have been screaming to haul her out like a sack. He did not. He stood in the snow and let her climb on her own axe, a hand's breadth at a time, as she had asked.

Then she was over the lip on her belly, lying with her cheek against the ice and her heart going like a mallet. Below her the ticking went on, as calm as ever.

"Ila."

"I'm out." Her voice had a crack in it. "I'm out. Let go."

The rope went slack. She crawled the last three paces on her knees and her good hand, and sat down in the snow. Then she looked round.

He was on one knee, with the rope wound twice round his hip and still locked in both fists. The wrappings had come off. Red had run into the wool.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They sat on the stone tongue out of the wind, with the sun on their backs and the Eye shining below them. She had the chain in her lap and her field book safe in her coat. She had not drawn the width. She would pace it from outside.

"Give me your hands," said Ila.

She heard it as she said it. It was the same thing she had said on the shelf above the Drum, and it went through her like a draught.

He gave them. She laid them across her knees, palms up.

They were worse than she had thought. The fingertips were swollen and shining, with blisters at each pad. Four had burst on the rope and wept pink into the creases. She had known what the cold had cost him. She had not looked at it this closely, and she made herself look now.

*Because of me,* she thought. Then she put the thought down, because it did not help, and reached into her coat.

The jar was small and brown, with a cork bound on by a thong. He frowned at it.

"That's Dhanu's. From the hut."

"The second morning." She worked the cork out with her teeth. "It was on the shelf by the barley. I took it and said nothing."

"You wouldn't look at me for two days."

"I was looking at your hands." She warmed a thumbnail of the fat in her fist until it softened. "I was also not looking at you. Both are true."

He made a short sound. She did not look up. She cleaned the broken blisters first, with snow-melt from the flask. He flinched, once, and held still. Then she smoothed the fat over each pad in turn, as lightly as she could, and wrapped the fingers one by one in strips torn from her own shirt hem. She was a measurer. She was good with small, exact work.

"It's not cold you can warm," she said. "Fire doesn't touch it. But the skin heals."

"You've been carrying it for two days."

"I've been carrying a good many things for two days." That was true, and she heard it as she said it. She kept her eyes on the knot she was tying. "Does it hurt less?"

"It does." He turned his hand over in hers, very slightly. "That's the first time anyone has dressed my hands since I was nine."

She did not know what to do with that. So she did the next thing, which was to talk, and she reached for the safest ground she had.

"Rohan sends me numbers," said Ila.

"Numbers."

"Every summer. A packet from the Shilak post, tied with the post's grey cord. Snow depth at the door by the week. Thaw dates at the cairn. How many crossers turned back, how many sacks the wagons carried in the autumn." She pulled the last knot snug. "Eleven winters of columns. There's never a word of his own. Once, in a margin, he wrote *ice late*."

"That's all?"

"They're love letters." She heard how it sounded and let it stand. "That's what he can do. He sends me his sums, and I send him mine, and neither of us has to say a thing. I have the whole post's weather in a trunk. I read it when I'm homesick."

Kunal began to laugh.

It came out of him by surprise, and it was not the courtier's laugh she had learned in the first weeks. It was lower and rougher, and it shook his shoulders, so that she had to hold his bandaged hand still. "The most feared captain in Hemkot," he said, "sends you his thaw dates."

"With the sacks."

"Tied in grey cord."

"He'll be insufferable once he's married. Meera counts things aloud." She was laughing too, the loud sudden one, and two ravens went up off the scree. The sound rang off the cliff and came back to her from the cleft, thin and strange, and she stopped.

In the dark, the ticking never changed.

For a long time she looked at the mouth. It was no longer a place she was afraid of. It was a place she had drawn, which was worse.

The next year lay as clear in her mind as a survey sheet. Her bearings, copied fair at Taravan, in a drawer with a key. A clerk would hand them up to an engineer. An engineer would lay the sheet on a table and put a thumb on the eighty-eight paces. He'd say, *Twenty men on the shelf. A watch-fire at the tongue. A chain of sentries along the shore, so that nothing goes in or comes out.* All of it would be accurate. Every line would be true. They would drive a garrison into the Eye as neatly as a peg into a post.

A true line had always been a lamp to her. You set it down, and people could see by it. Now she sat with the pencil still in her fist and understood that it was also a blade, and that the line did not decide which way it cut. Whoever picked it up decided.

*A true line in her hand.* It felt heavy, and edged. For the first time it felt like something she could aim.

She did not know where. She knew only that she would have to.

The field book went deep into her coat, beside the letter and flat against her ribs. Kunal had gone quiet beside her. He did not ask what she was thinking, and she was grateful, and she hated that she was.

"I'll pace the width from outside," she said. "I don't need to go back in."

"I should hope not."

"You held."

"You asked me to." He lifted the wrapped hand a little and looked at it. "I find I'm bad at it. Not pulling."

"You were very good at it."

He smiled at her, plainly, without having first checked it, and she had to look away over the lake.

It was then, past the pale curve of the Eye, that she saw the smoke.

It stood up from the far end of the chain, a grey thread against the rock, where the lower huts hid in the fold of the shore. It rose straight and thin. Someone was burning dung and juniper, and had been for an hour.

Kunal had seen it too. She felt it in the rope.

"That's the lower hut," he said. "Nobody's lived there since autumn."

"Raghu's men?"

"Raghu's men burn pitch. They'd have no reason to hide the smell." His eyes narrowed, and the tension went out of his shoulders. "That's a chulha that draws well. That's Dhanu's. Dhanu and Gauri have come."

She stood up. The sun was bright on the ice, and the ticking went on behind her in the mountain.

Dhanu would not have climbed this far up in the snow for nothing. Whatever he carried, it was news. She turned toward the smoke and tried to count the paces to it, because counting was safe.

# Chapter 19: Knucklebones

"No news until you've eaten," said Gauri, and shut Ila's fingers over a fistful of dried apricots as if she were closing a gate.

Dhanu stood behind his wife in the door of the lower hut with his mouth open on whatever he had climbed here to say. He had blown the goat-horn whistle three times along the shore, two long and one short, *come on, it's clear*, and he had not stopped limping toward them since. Right foot planted, left swung out and round. He looked like a man who had run a long way with a full bucket and been told to put it down.

"Gauri—"

"Eat. Both of you." She took Kunal's wrapped hands in her broad brown ones, turned them over and sniffed the goat fat. "Who tied these?"

"I did," said Ila.

"Flat knots." Gauri gave the hands back as though they had been inspected and passed. "Good."

Ila had been checked by clerks and engineers for twelve years. She had never once felt it go all the way down.

The hut was one room, warm and low, smelling of dung smoke and wet wool. On the sleeping shelf under a pony blanket lay a small shape with one sock off. Mini. Her fist was against her mouth and her breath whistled faintly.

Ila ate an apricot. It was leathery and sour and sweet, and she had not known she was starving. She ate a second. By the sixth she had started counting, so she ate a seventh to spite herself.

"Now," said Dhanu, "the bones."

"News," said Gauri.

"After the apricots, you said. The bones keep the hands busy while the mouth works." He was already digging in his coat. He came out with a leather twist, and from it spilled five knucklebones, sheep's, polished by years of thumbs. One was stained red. "A woman who can't throw knucklebones can't be trusted with a pass. Sit."

Ila sat on the hearth edge. Kunal folded himself against the wall with his bandages in his lap and the rope slack across the floor. He had the look of a man who meant to enjoy being useless.

The rules were simple, which made them cruel. You scattered four bones on the flat stone by the chulha. You tossed the fifth into the air, swept one bone off the stone with the same hand, and caught the fifth before it fell. Then two at a time. Then three and one. Then all four.

She lost the first game badly. The bones skittered where they liked. Her hand, which could hold a chain steady in a gale, made a fist of itself and dropped the red one in the ash.

"Ha," said Dhanu, with deep satisfaction.

"She's learning," said Kunal, from the wall.

"She's *losing*."

"She's gathering data."

Ila looked at him. He kept his face grave, but one corner of it had gone.

She gathered data. The hearthstone was a river slab laid by someone in a hurry. The side by the door sat a finger low. The third corner rocked, very slightly, under the heel of her hand. She tapped it twice and listened. When she threw the bones again, she did not throw them anywhere. She let them fall from a hand's height on the one spot where the slope walked them together, two and two, with the red one nosing neatly out on its own.

Then she swept, and caught, and did it again with three.

By the fourth round Dhanu had stopped saying *ha*.

"Do that once more," he said.

She did it once more. The bones went where she had meant them to go. They always did. He stared at the stone, then at her, then at the stone.

"Those aren't throws," said Dhanu. "Those are *sums*." His voice cracked upward. "You've surveyed my hearth!"

"Only the one slab."

"She's surveyed my hearth, Gauri!"

"It's been crooked since the day you laid it," said Gauri, without turning from the pot.

"A finger low on the door side," said Ila, "and the third corner rocks. You'd have a draught under that slab in winter."

"I do have a draught!" He slapped his knee and roared. The bundle on the shelf stirred and muttered, and he clapped a hand over his own mouth and shook with it, the tears running down his face. When he could speak he said, "Ten winters I've lost to my own hearth. Never a soul told me why."

Kunal was laughing against the wall, without sound, with his shoulders. It was the rough laugh from the lakeshore, and she did not look at it for long.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The soup came after. Gauri said "Now," in the tone of a woman shutting a book, and Dhanu put the bones away.

He told it plainly, with his hands on his knees. His boy had run in two nights ago from the upper folds, and what he had seen was this.

Raghu was in the Hollow Moon with eight pairs.

"Eight," said Kunal.

"Sixteen men, each pair bound at a cairn. They came up by the Kuhl cut in twos and threes, so the watchers wouldn't count them." Dhanu rubbed his bad leg. "They've dug the bowl like a field. Nine holes round the door cairn, and more along the east wall. The boy says they sit round the fire at night and don't talk."

"They took Hari's goats," said Gauri.

"Nine of them. Drove them up the Moon road in a string behind two of Raghu's men." His voice dropped. "Hari wasn't with them. Nobody's seen him since the hut burned."

Ila remembered the hut. She remembered the black ring of it, and the chalk on the marker stone with its second line smeared out. She put her spoon down.

"They haven't found the lakes," said Kunal. It was not quite a question.

"Not the basin. They have the Stair and the Moon." Dhanu looked at him. "They want the princes' way, and they have it half. The cairn told them nothing. They'll try the north-east shelf, then the Kuhl side. It's one slow mile at a time, but they've sixteen men to walk it with."

Nobody spoke. The chulha ticked.

Ila did the sum, because she could not help it. Sixteen men on a rim shelf. Four days to the Kuhl cut, two more along the shoulder. A shoulder that overlooked the head of the lakes. Then there was the blue light low in the cleft, shown only to the bound. Sixteen men were bound.

She did not say it. She picked the spoon up again, and found she could not taste anything in it.

"Mini," said Dhanu, after a while, in a different voice. He was looking at the shelf. "She's asked after you. All the way up. 'Is the lady with the string still counting?'"

It went into Ila before she had thought. It slid under all the sums.

"The star I named for her," she said. "I wrote it down."

Dhanu's head came round.

"The small yellow one over Ewe's Rest, the night we came in. It had no name in any book I own, and it was bright enough to shout." She kept her eyes on the pot, because it was easier. "Kunal said it wouldn't go on the map. It won't. It's in the margin of my field book, in pencil, which isn't the map. But it's hers. It has her name against it, with the bearing."

Dhanu said nothing at all.

When she looked up, his eyes were full. The tears stood in them, bright, and did not fall. He was a big man, and he had laughed himself weak over a crooked hearth an hour ago, and he sat with his hands open on his knees and could not say a word.

Gauri put her palm flat between his shoulders and left it there.

"She'll want it on the map," he said at last, thickly. "She'll make you swear it."

"I'd rather swear to that than most things," said Ila.

He scrubbed his face with the heel of his hand. Then he did what she had come to expect of him, and reached for a laugh to stand on.

"Look at him." He jerked a thumb at Kunal. "Sitting there like a lamb in a blanket. Do you know how long it took me to teach *him* the bones? Seven winters. And he sulked when a goatherd's child beat him. He's been like that since he was a boy, our Kunal, stubborn as a stone, and you'd never believe the half of what's behind it. Ask him what his uncle did on the ridge when he was—"

"Dhanu."

It was said flat. It was not loud. It dropped on the room like a lid.

Dhanu stopped with his mouth still shaped for the next word. His hand came down off his knee. In the stillness Ila heard the pot tick, and Mini's whistling breath, and the wind going over the roof.

She looked at Kunal.

He had not moved from the wall. His face was composed. His hands lay in his lap in their wrappings, and he was looking at them with great attention, as if he meant to count the knots. But the colour had gone out from under his skin. His mouth had the set of a door pulled to from the inside.

She knew that stance. She had seen it at the Eye, with his arms held a little away from his body. A hand laid on the nape of a neck.

*And I came home,* he had said, in the snow cave on the Hollow Moon, and put the rest where she could not reach.

It was shame. She had measured enough of other people's silences to know the shape of it. It was not a secret kept for use. He had his hand over it as you cover a wound.

A question was ready. It had come up whole, the way they did, with its bearing already taken. *What did your uncle do?* It was fair. She had a right to ask it, with the rope round her wrist and the hill's half-spoken story lying open in the room.

She thought of the letter flat against her ribs. Then the question he had put to her on the shelf, the narrowest one, and how he had taken her refusal and set it down without building on it. *I'll find out in daylight, from you.*

She did not ask.

"My throw?" said Ila.

Dhanu let out a breath that went on a good while. "Your throw," he said, small and shamefaced, and he took the bones out again, and put them in her hand.

She did not look at Kunal. She threw. They fell exactly as she meant them to, and for once she took no pleasure in it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dhanu had put them in the second hut, the smaller one, where the chulha had been lit that morning. It was a plank shelf and a stack of dung cakes and one blanket. They had done this nine times now, and neither of them mentioned it.

Kunal had said almost nothing since the bones. He had eaten, and thanked Gauri, and let Dhanu clasp his shoulder at the door and say, "I'm a fool, brother," and answered, "Yes," quite gently. Ila had taken her boots off and lain on the shelf with her face to the wall and the rope across the blanket. She listened to him not sleeping.

The Drum boomed, far off through the cliff. It was a slow single note, and then nothing.

"You didn't ask," said Kunal.

She did not pretend to be asleep. "No."

"Why?"

She thought about it, because he deserved a true answer and because it cost nothing to give one. "You said *and I came home*, in the Moon, and then you put the rest somewhere I couldn't reach. I thought it was yours to bring out. Or not."

"Dhanu would have told you. If you'd asked him tomorrow, when I was out of the room." His voice was very even. "Half the hill knows a piece of it. Dhanu has the best piece. It's the one with the joke in it."

"I wasn't going to ask Dhanu."

"I know." The blanket shifted. "That's why I'm not going to let him."

She turned over. He was sitting up against the wall with the bandaged hands on his knees. In the red light of the coals his face had no courtesy on it at all.

"I'll tell you myself," said Kunal. "Not from Dhanu. From me." He stopped. She watched him make himself go on. "I don't know what it's worth. I don't know what you'll do with it. I've been trying to work it out since the bones, and I can't, and I think that's the point of it."

Ila sat up. Her heart was going hard, and she was careful to keep her voice level.

"All right."

"Not here." He looked at the door, and past it, to the dark and the lake and the long ringing ice. "There's a place on the shore where you can hear the Drum. Where it booms. I'd like to be standing when I say it."

"Where the Drum answers," said Ila, "you lie still till morning."

"I know what it says." One side of his mouth moved, not quite a smile. "I've never in my life done what it says."

She put her feet over the edge of the shelf and found her boots. The rope went with her, and with it the whole length of him, so there was no question of going alone. She tied the laces by feel and did not hurry them.

When she stood, he held out one wrapped hand, palm up, the way she had once left hers on the boards. She laid her own in it. The bandage was rough, and the hand beneath it was warm.

He pulled the door open on the cold and the stars. Far down the shore the Drum spoke again, one slow note under the ice, and the whole dark answered it.

# Chapter 20: The Ridge

The shore was a shelf of black stone with the Drum under it, and the ice there was alive.

Kunal stood where it boomed. Out on the lake a long seam let go, and the note came up through his boots and into his teeth. The cold was clean and the sky was thick with stars. Behind him Ila had stopped at the end of the rope's slack, a pace and a half away, with her hands in her sleeves.

She did not say anything. She had not said anything since the hut. In twenty days he had learned this about her: she waited like a woman holding a level, to see which way the bubble went.

He had rehearsed it on the walk down, in the courtier's order. *First the fact, then the frame, then what it means.* He had a version that began *You should understand that my uncle* and ended with Ila nodding. He knew exactly what that version bought. It bought a woman who thought she had been told everything.

"I was nine," said Kunal.

His voice came out thin. He started again.

"My father died at the end of summer, of a lung fever. It was quick. A month after the funeral, in autumn, my uncle came to the nursery and said he would take me to see what was mine." The ice boomed. He waited it out. "We went up the Kuhl ridge. It was a long day. He bought me honey cake at the last farm, and he carried my coat when I got hot."

He stopped, because the next part had a shape he had never said aloud, and his mouth would not make it.

"At the top he gave me a knife and a heel of bread. He said, 'Find your way down.' I said, 'Which way?' and he said, 'That is the whole lesson.' Then he walked off along the ridge, and I watched him get small."

"Kunal," said Ila.

"Let me." He held up a bandaged hand. "I'll lose it if I stop."

She said nothing more.

"He said one more thing before he went. He didn't shout it. He said it like a man reading out a price. *Whoever knows the way owns the people.*" Kunal looked at the dark lake. "I had no route. I'd never walked that side. It came on to snow at dusk, and I sat under a rock and held the knife, and I was not brave about it."

"How did you get down?"

It was a surveyor's question, practical and low. It steadied him as nothing else could have.

"Goats." He half laughed. "In the morning I heard a bell. A nanny and eleven others, going down to the winter fold, and she knew the way in her feet. I followed her for two days. I ate snow and the bread, and on the second night one of the herd boys gave me a cheese rind and didn't ask my name. I walked into Garh Neshar at noon on the third day. My uncle was at the table. He said, 'Well?' and I said, 'I came home.'"

The wind moved over the ice, and the seam boomed again, farther out.

"I kept every way after that. Whatever's in your head can't be taken. A map can be burned. A guide can be bought. A route-keeper can be hung. But I carry the Stair and the Moon and the Ladder, and the Eye, where he took me at twelve and turned me round. I carry them and I give them to nobody. It's why I'm alive." He breathed out. "It's why the hill council has followed me for eight winters, and why none of them has the whole of it. I tell them to be open, Ila. I stand up and tell them. And I've never once opened my own hand."

She said, very quietly, "You think you're him."

"I think the one thing I took from that day was his lesson, and I kept it too well." His voice cracked on it. He let it. "I'm afraid I'm his best pupil. I'm afraid that if I say a way aloud to you, I'll start counting what it buys. I counted at the Eye. I counted at the Stair. I'm counting now, and I can't find the sum."

He had never said any of it. It lay on the stone between them, and nothing he could think of would pick it up again.

"I don't know what telling you gets me," said Kunal. "That's why I'm telling you."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


For a long moment she did not move.

Then she crossed the pace and a half. She did not touch him. She stood at his shoulder and looked out where he looked, at the pale lake and the cliff beyond.

"You found the way," said Ila.

He turned his head.

"A boy of nine. No map, no guide, a knife and a heel of bread. You found it by listening for a bell, and you were home in three days." She said it in the flat, exact voice she used for distances, and he understood that she was giving him a measurement. "He walked off along a ridge and left you the whole mountain to cross. He owned nothing, Kunal. He owned a table and a chair, and a nine-year-old who got home without him."

He had no answer. His throat had closed.

"The goats owned more of that road than he did," she went on. "That's what I'd put on a sheet, if I drew it. And the thing you're afraid of." She paused, choosing the words one at a time. "He never gave a way to anyone. He kept the grain and the bell and the roads. You paid nineteen silvers with the corners clipped, so that the medicine in Ranu could not be traced to your hand. A man who does that in secret for two winters, with no one to thank him, is not Pritam. Pritam would have put his name on the sack."

The Drum boomed. Kunal was shaking, finely, in the shoulders, as a horse does after a fall.

"I didn't know you'd worked that out," he said.

"I'm a surveyor. I check my closures." She turned to face him, and her face in the starlight was grave and open. "Nobody has told you that, have they?"

"No."

"Then I'm the first, and I've said it. It's measured." She took her hands out of her sleeves.

She held them up between them, bare, palms toward him. The fingers were slightly curled. She was not holding them out to be taken. She was showing him.

They had been in the cold air for half an hour. In the starlight the tips were pink, the colour of blood close under skin.

"I want you," said Ila.

He heard it, and then he looked at her hands, as a bound man does. Warm. Not a shade paler. They stayed warm while he looked.

"I'd like you to know I said it with these showing," she said. Her voice was not entirely steady. "I'm not very good at this. I'd rather be checked."

"Ila."

"I'm thirty-one," she said. "I know my own mind. I've known it since the lake. Possibly before, on the Stair, but I'd have had to work out the bearing, and I hadn't the numbers." One corner of her mouth went up. "Say something, please, because my hands are going to freeze."

He laughed. It cracked in the middle and became something else. He took her hands in his bandaged ones and held them, and they were warm, and he had never held a better piece of evidence.

"Kunal," she said.

Not *Highness*. Not *the prince*, with the small, dry edge she kept on it. His own name, plain, in her brisk voice. It went in under his ribs and stayed there.

"Say it again," said Kunal.

"Kunal." She stepped closer. "I'd like to go back to the hut, if you want that too. If you don't, I'll sit here with you till the Drum stops, and that's true as well."

"I want it," he said. "I'm thirty-three, and I've never wanted anything so plainly."

The rope slid between their boots as they turned. Neither of them reached for it. It was eight spans of undyed wool on a stone shore, and it had never been the thing that held them.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The herders' hut stood on the scree above the Drum, a steep climb up from the shore. Dhanu's people used it in summer for the high grazing, and it was cold. Kunal knelt at the chulha and coaxed a coal while Ila stood in the middle of the floor. Her coat was off. She unpicked three stitches of the lining, drew out a folded square of cream paper, and slipped it into the hem of her pattu. He saw it, and she did not look at him while she did it. Then she untied the rope from both their wrists and laid it on the floor, because the knots were for the stone, and the stone was not what she was thinking of.

The fire caught. He sat back on his heels and found her watching him.

"I should say the practical thing," she said.

"Please."

"I want it, and I'm saying so, and you can say no at any point and I'll stop. I'd like the same." She blew out her breath. "I find it easier to put it as a rule."

"Then it's a rule." He stood. "No at any point. A word, and we stop, and I'll warm tea."

"You'll warm tea," said Ila. She was smiling. "You'd do it, too."

She came the last step. He kissed her, and the rest of him went quiet. The cold had been in his hands for two days, and he had stopped noticing it. Now he noticed everything: the dry warmth of her mouth, the cold edge of her nose, the way her fingers closed in the front of his coat and pulled. She kissed as she measured, with her whole attention. When he lifted his head she followed him a little way, and said, "Again," and he did.

His bandaged hands were clumsy on the toggles of her coat. She laughed against his jaw and undid them herself, and then undid his, one by one, with the small exact movements he had watched on a chain a hundred times. The coats fell. She took the strips off his fingers, kissed each pad where the skin had mended pink, and set the wrappings on the shelf in a neat row.

"Oh," said Kunal, to the roof.

"You're hurt."

"I'm not. That's the trouble."

Her pattu came off her shoulders. Beneath it she wore the thick grey wool of a woman who had crossed three lakes, and she was warm to touch and smelled of snow and goat fat and chulha smoke. He found the laces at her collar. He looked up for leave, and she nodded once, and he undid them. She did the same for him, with her mouth set seriously, and put her palm flat over his heart and held it there.

"That's quick," said Ila.

"Yes."

"Good." She said it fiercely. "Mine is too."

They went down onto the shelf and the single blanket together. The coals lit her from the side: the long line of her shoulder, the freckles below her collarbone, the white scar along one rib where a chain had once whipped back. He kissed it. She said "Ow," and "Don't stop," in the same breath, and he did not.

In a hundred cold rooms he had thought desire was a thing you managed. A debt, a trade. Not this. Her hands knew where to go as though she had measured him. She slid them down his back and he arched, and she said, shaky and delighted, "There?" and he said "There," and meant it with everything he had.

When she drew him over her she held his face between her hands and stopped.

"Ila?"

"Say it with me. Yes?"

"Yes," said Kunal. "Yes. You?"

"Yes." Her mouth shook. "Come here."

He came. It was slow, because she wanted it slow, and he found that he did, too. The coals cracked. She breathed his name once into his neck and again against his mouth, and her heels pressed the backs of his thighs and drew him in. He had no courtesy left in him, nor any sharpness. He had only her pulse against his chest and the long climbing heat of it, and her hand finding his and bringing it up beside her head, fingers laced in his.

Somewhere in it she stopped counting.

He knew the moment. She had been breathing in sets, a habit he had seen on every climb, in threes and fours. Then it broke, and she gasped, and laughed in surprise at herself, and held on. After that there was nothing to measure. She went over first with his name in her throat, and her whole body shut around him like a hand. He followed, with his face in her hair and her heart going under his, and the whole mountain gone still.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The coals had burned low when she shivered.

She had gone to the end of the shelf for water and come back with her feet bare. She tucked them under the blanket, and they met his shin like two stones taken out of a river.

"Ila. You're frozen."

"They're always cold. It's nothing."

"Is that a small lie?" He looked at her hands. Pink. "No. It's true. Come here."

She let him pull her feet into his lap. He wrapped his hands round the left, then the right, and rubbed the arches slowly with his thumbs. They were long, narrow, slightly callused at the heel, and so cold they made his palms ache. She went rigid for a breath. Then she let her head fall back against the wall and made a sound he would not forget.

"No one has done that," said Ila, in a different voice.

"Never?"

"My mother, when I was small." She laughed, shakily, with her eyes shut. "I keep them under me in the field. I put them in a bag on the Stair. I don't show them. It's absurd. They're feet."

"They're feet," he agreed. "And it's you telling me where you're soft. I'll take it as given." He drew his thumbs up under the ball of her foot, and she shivered all through.

He did not stop. The blanket was warm, the hut was still, and her small cold feet were slowly turning to flesh in his hands.

"Ila."

"Mm."

"I'll give you a name a night," said Kunal. "From now on. One of ours, one of the hill's, true, as I know it. We've traded so far. A name for a question. I'd like to stop that." He kept his eyes on her foot. "I'll ask nothing back. Not an answer, not a line. You can draw it or burn it, and I'll know I gave it freely."

She sat up. Her feet stayed in his lap, but her face had changed. It was the one she wore over a closure that would not come out.

"Then hear the price first," she said. "Out loud, so neither of us can say we didn't. You say that across the rope and it's a promise. A promise made while bound and broken before the thaw strikes you like a great lie. Your hands first, then the rest." She held up a second finger. "And every name you give me, I have drawn for the crown for eleven years. I haven't decided what I'll do with them. Each one could be a line on the queen's map by spring, with your hill's name on it, and you'd have put it there."

"I know both," said Kunal.

"Say you do."

"I know both. I'd rather lose the fingers than keep counting." He heard how that sounded, and let it stand. "Tie it on, and I'll say it again."

She looked at him a long moment. Then she reached for the rope on the floor. She knotted the end round his left wrist, flat and snug, and he tied hers, and the wool warmed between them.

"I'll ask nothing back," said Kunal.

The rope hummed against his wrist, low and level, with no climbing in it.

Her hand came down and lay over his on her ankle. "Which is tonight's?"

He knew which. It was the one he had held back longest, and it surprised him how little it weighed.

"The black crag you named for the healer." She went still. "We call it the Widow's Knuckle. For the hand a widow laid on the rock while she waited for her husband. It's older than the Stair. Write it down however you like."

"Kunal," said Ila.

"It's yours."

She did not answer. She slid down against him and put her face in his neck, and her cold feet against his calves. He pulled the blanket up over both of them and held her, and for the first time in twenty-four years, he did not count.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He woke before dawn, with the hut dark and grey at the edges, and the coals a thin red line.

She was awake. She lay on her side with her head on her arm, in the pattu, watching him. How long, he could not tell. Her eyes were very steady.

"You were speaking," said Ila.

"What did I say?"

"Names. In hill speech. I didn't write any of them."

He smiled and reached out. She let him touch her cheek, and it was warm. The pattu had slipped where its hem hung over the shelf's edge, and a corner of cream paper showed through a loosened stitch. She had sewn it in last night with her face turned from him. Now it was no longer wholly hidden. Her hand lay over it, flat and still.

She saw him see it. She did not move her hand.

Whatever it was, she had carried it three weeks. He remembered the shelf, and *I'll find out in daylight, from you.* He had set that down on purpose.

"Ila," said Kunal.

"Yes."

The question was a plain one, and it was already in his mouth. *What does it say?* He drew breath for it, and the cold arrived at once. It came into the tips of his fingers like the first bite of a long night on the ice, and the rope at his wrist went tight and still. He had said *nothing back*. He had said it with the knot on.

He closed his hand on the blanket and swallowed the question whole.

"You're awake early," he said instead. The chill drew off his fingers slowly, as a tide goes out.

She had seen his fist close. She looked at it, and then at his face, and something crossed hers that he could not read.

"I was thinking." Her voice was calm. "About a number. It won't come out."

She lay watching him in the grey, and her hand did not leave her hem.

# Chapter 21: A Promise with a Date

Gauri knocked with the flat of her hand, twice, as if the door might be asleep.

Ila had been up for an hour. She sat on the floor of the herders' hut with a field sheet pinned under her knee and a pencil lead warming in her fist, and the rope lay slack across the boards to the shelf where Kunal slept with one arm flung over his eyes. She had not slept much. She had spent the grey hours counting the roof slates, and the count had come out the same every time.

"Open," said Gauri through the planks. "I've bread, and I've news, and I'm not carrying either back down."

Kunal was awake before the bolt was drawn. He sat up with the blanket at his waist and his face already shut, the courtier's face, and Ila watched him put it on like a coat.

Gauri came in smelling of cold. She set the bread on the shelf, looked once at the two of them and once at the rope, and said nothing about any of it. It was a kind of courtesy Ila was beginning to value above speeches.

"Dhanu's boy was out on the north shore at first light," she said. "Two pairs. Four men. They're on the far side of the basin, past the Eye."

"Raghu's?" said Kunal.

"Grey wool, no blue cord. The boy says they walk like men who've been told to be quiet." She drew a line in the air from right to left. "Tracks along the west side of the Eye, close under the cliff. Fresh. They've been looking at the water."

Ila's pencil had stopped. "How far from the cleft?"

"A morning's walk. Less, if they stop looking at the water."

*Sixteen men bound,* Ila had thought last night, over the soup, and had not said it. Here was the first four, small as a pencil mark on a sheet.

"They'd have come down the Kuhl shoulder," she said. "That's the only way onto the west shore without crossing the ice." She heard herself, brisk and exact, and found it a comfort. "If they hold the shoulder, they can see the whole basin. The Ladder's foot, the path to the lake huts, and the slope under this hut."

"Dhanu says the same," said Gauri, with a short nod, as if Ila had passed another inspection. "He wants you both told before the sun's up the cliff. After that he'll ask what you mean to do."

She went. The door banged. The bread steamed on the shelf, and neither of them touched it.

"Tonight," said Kunal. "We go into the Eye tonight. The cleft is the one place four men with spyglasses can't follow unless they're bound and know where it opens." He rubbed his face with a bandaged hand. "Draw me what's behind us. Dhanu will need to know which ways to shut."

It was the sort of request she could do in her sleep. She had walked every one of those ways with a chain.

"I'll draw it," said Ila.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She drew the exits.

The Widow's Ladder came first, because it was the biggest and the easiest, with its four pitches and its hundred and sixty steps. Then the shore path along the Mirror, with the lakeside cairn at nine hundred and ten paces from the scree edge. Then the foot trail from the lower huts. Then the scree slope where they sat now, steep and loose, with a sheep track going up it that no one had ever bothered to name. Last came the cleft at the head of the Eye, as far as she had seen it, which was as far as a dropped spike would fall in a count of three.

She did it true. She could not do it any other way. The line was what her feet had made it, and the numbers sat beside it in her small square hand, bearings and paces and the fall of the ground.

Halfway through the Kuhl shoulder she saw what she was making.

Every line on it was a door. Every bearing was a key. Raghu's four men would pay dearly for this sheet. The hills would pay to keep it from them, and the crown might do anything with a copy. It was also an excellent piece of work. The closures were tight. There was not one wrong figure on it.

*A map is only true or false.* She had said it to herself for eleven years, like a woman checking a latch. She looked at the shore path, sharp as a hair on the paper, and for the first time the sentence did not close.

"You've stopped," said Kunal.

He had come to sit against the wall opposite, with his bandaged hands round a bowl of tea. He had eaten the bread. He watched her draw, as he always did, with the open and slightly stunned look of a man watching someone do a thing he can't.

"I'm checking a closure," said Ila.

"Does it close?"

"Yes." She turned the sheet toward him. "That's the trouble."

He looked. He did not reach for it. His eyes went along the Ladder and the shore and the cleft, and she saw him name each place in his head, in the old speech. He did not say any of them.

"It's very good," he said.

"It's the best sheet I've made in eleven years." She tried a laugh, and it came out short. "And if it goes the wrong way, it opens the whole basin like a purse."

"So don't let it go the wrong way."

"Which way is that?" said Ila.

He opened his mouth and shut it. She watched him decide not to say the neat thing.

She took her pattu from the peg and turned the hem out. The corner of cream paper still showed through the loosened stitch. She had meant to mend that before he woke. She sat with needle and thread and the sheet folded small, and unpicked the lining along the side seam, above the hem, where it would not show. Then she laid the sheet against the wool with the three older ones from the Stair and the Moon, and began to sew them in. The queen's letter slept in the hem below.

Kunal said nothing. He watched her stitches go in, small and flat and close.

He had asked her for the exits. Not the sheets. It would have been simpler to give him the paper. She did not.

"Ila," said Kunal.

She knew from the weight in his voice what was coming. It had been in the room since dawn, with the hem and her hand over it.

"I promised you I'd ask nothing back," he said. "I'm going to break that, and I want you to hear why first." He set the bowl down. "Raghu's four are under the cliff. Your letter has been sewn into you since the Fair, and it came from the crown's drawer. If it names the cleft, then the crown's friends know the Eye better than I do, and I'd be leading you in blind. I won't do that. Not to you. Not tonight."

He held out both hands, fingers spread, in the light from the door. They were bandaged to the knuckle, and the tips were pink.

"So I'll ask where you can see it."

"Kunal. You promised."

"I know what I promised."

"Then you know what it does to your hands." Her voice had gone hard, and she let it. "You've set a price on the table so I have to look at it. If I refuse, I watch you pay. If I answer, you've bought it with your fingers. That's pressure, whatever you meant by it."

"I know." He did not look away. "I'd rather you were angry than that I walked you blind into the cleft."

"What did the letter say?"

The cold came at once. She saw it arrive. The pink in his right fingertips went waxy, then grey, as if a hand had wiped the blood out of them. The rope at his wrist drew taut and still. His breath went out in a short, hard push, and under the bandages the skin lifted in blisters, fat and clear, and the grey beneath them did not change.

He did not drop his hands. He held them where she could see, shaking a little, and kept his eyes on her face.

"I'll pay it," said Kunal.

She was on her knees before she knew she had moved, her hand out to take his. He drew them back against his chest.

"Don't. It's paid." His voice was rough. "Take it onto yourself and you'll be climbing with them as well. I need one of us whole on stone tonight."

She stopped with her hand in the air between them. The sheep track ran up the scree on her sheet, in pencil, in her own hand. A broken promise struck like a great lie, and a great lie killed flesh. Those tips were gone, and nothing she did now would bring them back.

She obeyed. She drew her hand in and set it on her knee, and it was the hardest thing she had done since the Ladder.

She had known the question was coming since the Mirror. She had had the answer ready since the Drum, folded small. *It says what the crown wants of this mountain, and what the crown owes it, and a name I know.* All of it was true. She could have said all of it, and it would not have given him back his fingers, and then she would have told him her grandfather's gloves lay palms together on a stone.

"I'm not going to tell you," said Ila. Then, because he had a reason and deserved an answer to it: "Raghu's four don't need my letter to find a cleft. They've seen its shadow from the shoulder."

"No," said Kunal slowly. "They don't."

The hum did not rise. Her own fingertips stayed pink.

His face did what she had been afraid of. It did not go cold or courtly. It went quite still, and then it opened, a little, and what showed under it was a boy of nine sitting under a rock with a knife, who had asked which way and been told that was the whole lesson.

"Right," he said. "That's yours to refuse."

"It's not about you."

"I know."

"It's not." She heard her voice go up. "It's mine. And an oath I swore before this crossing. I took a commission from the archive, and it binds my silence on what their papers hold. If I break it, I'm a clerk who sold the crown's drawer." She pressed the needle into the wool. "That's *one* reason."

"What's the other?"

"I don't know yet." It was the truth, and it came out of her like something torn. "I don't know what it does to you."

He did not push. That was the worst of it. He nodded slowly and laid his ruined hands on his knees, where she could still see them. He had swallowed the question once before, in the grey light, and now he had asked it and paid and was swallowing the answer, with nothing at all in his face.

She could not bear it.

He had held her weight at the Eye, and his fingertips had burst on the rope. She had said *I want you* on the Drum shore, with her hands out. She had not known you could lie warm in a man's arms all night and still keep a locked room in the same house.

"When we're through the mountain," said Ila.

He lifted his head.

"When we're through." She held the sheet against her ribs, and the words were out before she had checked them against anything. "Through the cleft, and the Eye, and whatever's under it. I'll tell you everything in it. The whole of it, and the oath be damned."

The rope hummed.

She felt it through the bone of her wrist, low and level, steady as a held note. It did not climb. It was the sound of a thing meant, and she had made it, and it would not be unmade.

Kunal had gone white about the mouth. "Ila. A promise, with the bond on. You know what a miss would do."

"I know."

"You don't have to—"

"I'd like to," said Ila, and found it was true, which was the most alarming part.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She told him the rest because it was the same sum she did over any survey, and he deserved to see it written.

"There's a date on it," she said. "I haven't chosen it. The bond ends when meltwater runs at the cairn where we tied it, and that's the Meadow Cairn. South-facing shoulder. Soft weather." She tapped her field book. "I have eleven winters of its thaw dates. They spread wide. A hard year gives me weeks. A soft year gives me fewer, and nobody on this hill can say which one this is."

"And if it runs before we're through?"

"Then the blue light goes out of the cleft, and the path under the Eye closes to us like any lone crosser's. The promise lapses the same hour. Not kept. Only ended, with the words still in my mouth and the oath still on me." She made herself say the last of it. "You'd be standing in the dark at the head of the Eye, with five dead fingertips, having been given a refusal and a debt I couldn't pay."

It was the first time in her life she had made a promise with a date on it. She sewed the last sheet in. Her hands were steady, and it was a very long way down.

Kunal had not moved. He was looking at his fingers, turning them in the light from the door. The grey had not crept anywhere. It had simply stayed, and the blisters had settled over it like glass. He closed his hand slowly, and it closed, but the last joints went down a beat behind the rest.

"The cleft is stone and ice," he said, to the hand. "I'll need those on it tonight."

"Then we'll rope the hard moves," said Ila. "I'll lead them. You'll hold with the palm."

"Yes." He flexed it again. Then he looked up, and the courtier was nowhere in his face. "Dhanu wanted till dusk to see what Raghu's four did. I'm not waiting for that."

"Kunal—"

"I was going tonight for Raghu. To get ahead of him." He shook his head. "It's your date now. Hard year or soft, I'd rather be through the mountain with a week to spare than be right about the weather. We go tonight, and we go for yours."

It was a choice, not a promise. She heard the difference, and so did the rope, which stayed quiet.

"You didn't have to give me a date," he said, lower.

"It has one whether I name it or not," said Ila.

He laughed, once, without sound. Then he held out his right hand, palm up, the fingers curled away from her, and she put the pattu aside and took it by the palm. It was warm. The dead tips lay against her wrist like cold pebbles, and she held the living part of him and let them lie. The sun came up the cliff and laid a bar of cold gold across the floor.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dhanu came at midmorning, limping, with a whistle round his neck and a waxed cloth full of cheese.

He stopped at the door and looked at Kunal, who stood at the lip of the scree with Gauri, pointing at the far shore, and then at the rope. It ran from Kunal's wrist back across the stones to the hut, eight spans exactly. The whole length was the width of a courtyard.

"He's at the end of his string," said Dhanu.

"He's at the end of his string," Ila agreed.

"Then he can't hear me, can he, if I speak low."

She understood. She stood, and walked the pattu to the doorframe, and let the rope go taut behind her in a long straight line. Kunal looked round once. She nodded. He turned back to the shore, and the wind took what was left.

Dhanu set the cheese on the sill. He did not sit. The limp had worn the heel of his right boot to a slant.

"My boy says the four on the west shore aren't looking at the lake any more," he said. "They're looking at the cliff. They've seen the cleft's shadow from the shoulder." He rubbed his leg. "It'll be tonight, then, as the prince says."

"Yes."

"And when you're through, and you've seen what he'll show you, and you go back down with your lead and your string." He said it carefully, as a man sets down a full bucket. "What will you do with the map?"

Ila looked at the pattu on its peg. She could feel the sheets in it, like a second spine.

"Draw it true," she said.

It was the answer she had given the survey office, and the archive, and herself in the dark for eleven years. It came easily. It was solid as a surveyor's stone.

Dhanu waited a moment. He was not unkind about it.

"True for who?" he said.

The wind came over the scree. Out on the lake the ice spoke once, a long note, and the whole basin answered it.

She opened her mouth, and the word she wanted was not there. There was only the lined shore, and a small yellow star in a margin, and a woman at a table in Taravan with a key she had never been given.

Ila said nothing. She laid her hand flat on the hem of the pattu, over the stitches and the paper under them, and the ice sang again across the dark water.

# Chapter 22: Walnuts

Dhanu put a walnut in Kunal's palm and folded the dead fingers over it himself, one at a time, like a man closing a purse.

"Feel that?"

"No," said Kunal.

"Then look at it."

Kunal looked. The shell was dark and ordinary, with a red thread wound twice round the seam and knotted off. Dhanu laid a second one beside it. The juniper fire popped between them, and the smoke went up straight and sweet into a sky full of stars.

"My word without a walnut isn't from me," said Dhanu. "You know the rule."

"I made half the council swear to it."

"You taught it to them. My people made it, ten generations back. Different thing." He lowered himself onto the log with the stiff right foot first, the left swinging after. "A boy comes with a walnut, it's mine. A boy comes without, I never said it, I was asleep, I'm a lame man with a bad leg. Even if it's my own runner. Even if it's you at my door, doing my voice."

"Why two?"

"One for when you're stupid." Dhanu's face did not move. "I brought a second because I've known you thirty years."

Kunal laughed, and it hurt somewhere under the ribs. He shut the dead hand on the walnut. Through the open door behind him, the herders' hut was a square of dark. Ila slept on the shelf in her boots, because they went into the Eye at moondown. The rope lay across the threshold in the ash-grey light, untied, a long pale line from his place at the fire to her wrist. Eight spans. He had paced it twice without meaning to.

"Send one, and I come," said Dhanu. "I don't ask why. Not till after."

"Thank you."

"Don't. It's a bad bargain. Half the time you'll be wrong." He held his hands to the fire. "Now say it."

"Say what?"

"What you've decided. You've been decided since noon. You sat on that scree with your jaw set and your mouth shut tight as a fist."

Kunal looked at the flames. Juniper burned with a clean, short heat, and the resin spat. He thought of the sheet Ila had drawn that morning, with the shore path on it, sharp as a hair.

"I'm taking her through the princes' way," he said. "All of it. Every turn I know."

Dhanu said nothing for a time.

"No," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"I thought you'd say that," said Kunal.

"Then you thought right, and you can stop there." Dhanu did not raise his voice. He never did. He leaned in, and the firelight caught the grey in his beard. "Listen to me, because I'll say it once and then I'll say it again. She's the crown's surveyor. Her commission has the queen's own words in it. *The crown may hold every way.* She read it to you herself."

"She said she doesn't know what holding means."

"I know what it means. A garrison means it. A garrison is a house with soldiers in it, and the house goes where the way goes." He tapped the log with one finger. "You give her the princes' way, and she draws it, and she's honest, so it's true. Then it sits in a drawer in Taravan, in a hand you'll never see, with a key you'll never be given. And one winter a captain opens that drawer and sends two hundred men through the mountain in a night. Do you think the villages can shut a door against that?"

It was a fair argument. It was better than the one Kunal had made himself on the long walk from the lake, and he took his time answering it.

"The hills have kept that way sixty winters, and more," said Dhanu. "Your grandfather kept it. Your father kept it. They didn't keep it because they were mean, Kunal. They kept it because it's the one door we have."

"And my uncle kept it for the same reason, he'd say."

"Your uncle kept it for a throne."

"He kept it exactly as you describe. In one man's head, with nobody to check him." Kunal turned the walnut over. "That's the part I can't get round, Dhanu. A door only one person can open isn't ours. It's his."

"That's a speech."

"Then here's a fact." He made himself say it plainly, as Ila would. "The crown's cover sheet to the council names a way beneath the Shilak Cairn. I read it. I'd never heard of any such way. Raghu's men have dug nine holes round the door cairn on the Hollow Moon, looking for a carved marker that was never there. They're hunting the princes' way with picks. And the queen put a letter in Ila's hem that she won't show me, and she wouldn't have sewn it in so carefully if it didn't matter." He opened his hand. "It's already out, old friend. Somebody in Taravan knows it exists. Somebody in the hills is paid to dig. All that's left to argue about is whether the one person on this mountain who lied to my face once and owned it by dawn gets the true line, or whether Raghu draws a bad one and sells it."

"Secrecy's failed, you're saying."

"It failed before I was born. I only noticed."

The Drum boomed on the lake, a long note that rolled up the scree and through the planks of the hut. Neither of them looked round.

"It's a good case," said Dhanu at last. "I still say no."

"I know you do. I'm glad. If you didn't, I'd be doing this because it was easy."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dhanu fed a stick to the fire. When he spoke again, he had changed the ground, which he did when a quarrel was done and the work was not.

"Tell me the petition."

So Kunal told it. He had rehearsed it on the scree, with the wind taking half of it, and it came out smoother than he liked. "When the thaw council sits at the Shilak Cairn, I stand up and ask two things of the crown. First, no garrisons in the hill villages. Not on the Stair, not on the Moon, not in any fold the council names. The hills answer to the hills. If a warden wants a hearing, he asks the council, and the council answers."

"And second?"

"A council seat at Taravan. One voice of ours in the queen's own hall, with a right to speak before any order touches the passes." He heard how it sounded in the open air, and it still sounded good. "Neshar a self-governing valley under the crown. Not outside it. I don't want a war, and she can't afford one. This gives her the passes safely open, and it gives us a door she can't close from the capital without our hearing."

"It's a fine petition."

"But."

"But they'll ask." Dhanu did not look away. "Old Bhim will stand up first, because he always does. And he won't ask whether it's fair. He'll ask what you gave the crown for it. A queen doesn't hand over a seat in her hall for a speech."

Kunal had known that question was waiting, as one knows the next stair in the dark. He had not wanted to walk onto it.

"I'll tell them the truth," he said.

"Which is?"

"That I showed a crown surveyor the way. That it was mine to show." He took a breath. "That I gave it before I knew what she'd do, and that the council can hold me to it."

"They'll hold you to it, all right." Dhanu said it without heat. "Some will say you sold the hills. Some will say you were a fool in love, and that'll be the kinder half." He stirred the coals with a stick. "Let me tell you what none of them will say, because none of them have watched you as long as I have."

"Go on."

"You have never given anyone anything you couldn't take back."

It dropped into the fire like a split deodar round, and the sparks went up.

"That's not true," said Kunal. "The silver—"

"Clipped at the corner so no one could trace it to you. Nineteen bits, and you never once said your own name to the woman who ate because of them. The names you give the council, you give in pieces, so none of them holds the whole. The bones." Dhanu lifted the sheep-knuckles from his belt and let them lie in his palm. "Seven winters I taught you. You played me every game and never once let me win, nor lost on purpose. You played so it couldn't cost you."

"I broke a promise to her this morning. I paid for it."

"You broke it so you could be safe in the cleft. And you paid in coin, which is fine and clean and over." Dhanu closed his hand. "This one's different. You put a way in her head, there's no clipping it back. She can't forget it. You can't have it back. If she's the crown's, it's the crown's, and the hills pay. That's what I mean by a thing you can't take back."

Kunal sat very still. The fire hissed.

"I know it," he said.

"Do you?"

"I've known it since the Eye, when I was twelve and a man turned me round by the shoulders." He looked at his bandaged hands. "That's why I want to. If I can't take it back, then I've finally given it. Not lent it. Not parcelled it, or priced it. I want to find out what I am without the lock on."

Dhanu looked at him for a long time. The limp was still in the way he sat, the right foot planted and the left held out, as though even at rest he were walking round something.

"The council will want your head," he said.

"They can have a hearing."

"And if she draws it and takes it down the mountain and hands it over?"

"Then I was wrong, and the hills will say so, and I'll stand in the hall and hear it." He said it slowly. "I'd rather be wrong that way than right in the way I've been."

Dhanu grunted. It was the sound he made when a thing was settled and he did not intend to praise it. He heaved himself up, and the left foot swung out and round.

"Ask her what she'll do with it," he said. "Not before. After. That's all I'll say, and I'll say it without a walnut, so you needn't take it from me."

"I'll take it anyway."

"You're stupid enough already." He went off down the scree with the whistle swinging on its thong, and the dark folded behind him.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Kunal stayed by the fire until the sound of him was gone.

Then he opened his hand and looked at the second walnut.

There was a third in his coat. He had carried it since the day the council fixed the thaw date, in the lining over his ribs, wrapped in a scrap of grey wool. It was a plain walnut, whole and dry, with no thread on it. He took it out and held it in his palm, without asking himself why he had brought it.

Mini had asked for one. That was all. Six years old on the pony in front of her mother, with her fist in the horse's mane, she had said, *Uncle Kunal, can I have a walnut with a string, like the runners? I want my own.* And he had said yes and, by the stones, had said *When I'm back.* It had been a light thing to say. He had said it as men say it to children, and he had not thought of it until the Drum froze under him last night, and he had lain with Ila's cold feet against his shin and not counted.

He took a hank of red thread from his pouch. It was Gauri's, a gift from the winter before. He pulled out a length with his teeth, because the fingers would not pinch it, and laid it round the seam.

The tips of his right hand lay against the shell like pebbles. He could see them move. He could not feel what they held. The thread slid, caught, and slid out again, and the first knot came out as a lump the size of a seed.

He undid it with his teeth, and began again.

It was a simple knot. A runner's turn, a loop, a tuck. He had tied it ten thousand times, in the dark, in a gale, with half a hand frozen. The second try came out lopsided, the thread riding up off the seam and leaving the walnut naked on one side. He pulled it loose and sat a moment with the thread dangling.

*The last joints lag.* He had said it to the hand that morning, flat, as a fact.

He thought of Pinku, in the walnut grove at Ranu at moonrise, last winter, with a clay lamp too big for his fist. He had tied that one himself, by the light of a lantern, in a hurry, with all ten fingers. It had been the best knot he knew. He had sent the boy home with it, and never learned if anyone had asked where it came from.

The third try he did slowly. He held the walnut against his knee, trapped the thread under the heel of his hand, and made each turn with the whole arm instead of the fingers. The knot came out round and fat and slightly off true, with an ugly tail he could not cut straight. It held. He tugged it, and it did not slip.

It was a bad knot. It was the worst he had tied since he was eight.

Kunal sat with it a while on his knee, and found that he was smiling, stupidly, at a nut.

"Mini will like it," said Ila, from the doorway.

He had not heard her wake. She stood on the threshold in her pattu, with her hair flat on one side and her boots laced, and the rope slack across the planks at her feet. It had stayed within its eight spans the whole night. She looked at the walnut, and then at his hand, and did not say anything about the fingers, which was her own kind of manners.

"It's bad," said Kunal.

"It's very bad. It's lopsided." She crossed and sat on the log where Dhanu had been, close enough that the fire lit one side of her face. "She'll show it to everyone."

"That's the idea."

"Is it from you?"

"It's from me." He turned it so she could see the tail. "No one else could make it so ugly."

She laughed, short and sudden and loud, and it rang across the scree like a dropped pan. Out on the lake the ice answered. He put the walnut in his coat, over the heart, in its wool.

The fire had burned to a red bed. The moon was low behind the cliff, and above the Eye the stars stood thick and cold. Raghu's four were somewhere on the far shore, with their spyglasses and their patience. In an hour the line would show, blue, to the two of them and no one else.

Kunal looked at the woman beside him. She had a pencil lead in her sleeve and a letter in her hem and eleven years of true sheets in her head. She would not tell him what she meant to do with the way. She had said *when we're through*, and a rope had hummed under it. He did not know her answer. He had not asked for it, and he did not mean to wait for it.

"Ila."

"Yes."

"I'm taking you into the cleft tonight." He made himself say it all, with his hands open on his knees and nothing kept back. "The whole way I know, every turn, in order. And then past it. Past the place where my uncle turned me round. I've never been there. We'll find out together what's on the far side of it."

She did not answer. The wind came down off the shoulder and moved the smoke. He watched her face go still, the way it did over a closure that would not come out. The pencil lead in her fingers had stopped mid-roll, and under her breath, so low he almost missed it, she began to count paces.

# Chapter 23: Seven Breaths

The cleft breathed on her before she had the lamp lit.

A slow, even draught, cold enough to ache in the teeth, came out of the dark at the head of the Eye as if the mountain had turned over in its sleep. Ila shuttered the lamp to a slit. Behind her the lake lay black and still. On the far shore, four men with spyglasses were watching a cliff.

"Blue," said Kunal.

She had been looking too high. She looked low. Along the foot of the left wall, where the ice met the stone, small stacked stones ran into the cleft, one every few paces, each with a faint blue glow. The glow was no brighter than breath on glass.

"I see it."

"I didn't. Last time." He stood with the rope slack between them and his bandaged hands held a little away from his coat. "I stood here at twelve and there was nothing. Only a man behind me saying it was dark and I'd been brave."

She did not say anything kind. He would have heard the effort in it.

"Walk," said Ila. "I'll count."

They went in.

The walls were ice over stone, and the lamp lit them from inside, so that they walked down a throat of blue glass. Water ticked somewhere ahead. Each drop came alone, like a clerk tapping a pen. She counted her paces under her breath, because counting was what her feet did when her head wanted somewhere to stand. Eighty. Eighty-one.

The wall on the left came in. The passage doubled back on itself like a hairpin, the stone slick and black at the bend, and Kunal stopped dead with the rope drawn out behind him.

"Here," he said.

He was not asking. She waited. His breath smoked in the lamp slit.

"He turned me by the shoulders. Like this." He did not show her. "He said, 'The rest is for a prince who's earned it.' Then he walked me out. I counted his steps all the way to the shore, so I'd know them."

"Kunal."

"There's a song." He said it to the hairpin, not to her. "He taught it to me that day, so I'd have it when I'd earned it. I have sung it to myself for twenty-one winters and never once walked it." He drew a breath, let it go, and sang very low, in a child's flat tune.

*First the turn that turns you home,*
*then seven breaths and none to keep,*
*then the stone that weeps and weeps,*
*step on the wet where the water sleeps.*

"It ends there," he said. "Past that, nothing. He never gave me the rest."

Ila looked at the hairpin. She had walked into it already with her eyes. "That's not an order," she said.

"What?"

"*The turn that turns you home.* You've heard it all your life as 'go home.'" She put her gloved hand on the bend. "It's a bearing. The passage turns you round, and you face the way you came. It doubles back, so you're facing home and walking away from it."

He stared at the stone. Then he laughed, very short and not happy. "All my life."

"Twenty-one years. You were twelve." She held out the lamp. "Go on. You're six steps from your furthest point, and I'd like you to be the one who takes the first."

He took it. The rope paid out behind him around the corner. She followed, and the blue stones went with them.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The slot began forty paces on, a crack in the wall the width of a man's shoulders sideways and no more. It ran away into a dark the lamp could not enter. Cold air came out of it. Kunal put his face to it and took it in like a man smelling bread.

"Seven breaths," he said. "That's what the song means. Long enough that you'd take seven, and you can't. The rock's too tight. Breathe in and your chest swells and you stick." He turned to her. "Let it all the way out first. Then don't take another until you're through."

"How long is it?"

"I don't know." He said it plainly, and she respected him for it.

She did the sum anyway. A man's slow walking breath, seven times. The slot was long. She put the lamp on a ledge and tied her pencil case flat inside her coat, where her ribs would shield it. "If you stick," said Ila, "don't fight. Breathe out more, and I'll pull." She went on, because he was not saying it. "If I stick, you do the same."

"I'd have done it anyway."

"I know. I wanted it on the record."

He went in first. The rope slid after him in short, hard jerks. She counted them. At seven spans it went slack and then taut again, and one long pull came down it. *Come.*

She breathed out until her ribs met each other. Then she turned her shoulder to the stone and went in.

It was worse than the Ladder. The rock took her front and back, cold through her pattu, and the white scar along her rib dragged over a knob of stone and woke. *One.* The lamp slit went out behind her and she was alone in the dark with her heartbeat. *Two. Three.* Her lungs began to talk. *Four.* The rope at her wrist pulled steadily, and she let it. *Five.* A black pressure came behind her eyes. *Six.*

Then the stone let go of her chest, and she fell out of the crack onto her hands and knees and breathed in. The air tasted of iron and snow. She had never tasted better.

Kunal had her by the collar. He had a hand in her pattu and the heel of the other under her arm, and she saw that the dead fingers of the right one would not close.

"Seven," she said, with a gasp that turned into a laugh. "By my count it was seven. I'd like to write to your uncle. His song's exact."

"Don't." He was laughing as well, helplessly, with his forehead against the wall. "Don't, he'd be insufferable."

She went back for the lamp, which took the rope, and came out of the crack again with the flame cupped in her hands. They stood a moment in a space like the inside of a bell. Somewhere ahead the water ticked faster.

The weeping stone was twenty paces on.

A ledge of black rock lay glazed with ice, and a skin of meltwater slid over it in a thin bright sheet and fell into a runnel below. The runnel went down into a dark with no bottom. A bound pair's winter line crossed the stone, one blue cairn at each end, and the light on the far one was as faint as a candle through a sleeve.

"Step on the wet," said Kunal, "where the water sleeps." He looked at it. "The dry ice is the glaze. The wet is the stone under it."

"One in five," said Ila.

"What is?"

"That the glaze takes a boot." She dealt in odds that way. Comfort it was not, and he did not take it for any. "I'd put it lower on the wet. I'd put it higher if you kept looking at your feet."

"I won't."

He sang as he went, to keep his eyes up. The tune was the same flat one, a little steadier. His boot came down in the bright skin of water, and it held, and the next. On the third step the dead hand slid off the wall. He did not drop. He put the palm flat to the stone and leaned his weight into it, and she saw his jaw go hard.

He reached the far cairn. The blue light climbed on his face.

She crossed on the wet, with his song in her ears. Her boot held. She came off the ledge into a long dry passage of loose stone, and the blue stones ran away ahead in a faint line, down into the mountain.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Stop," said Kunal. "I want you to write this."

They had gone on perhaps three hundred paces. She had counted every one, and the passage was wide enough now to walk upright, with a floor of dry gravel that ticked under their boots. Far ahead the meltwater ran in the dark, and the cairn-light made a path of small blue seeds.

"Write what?"

"All of it." He sat down on the gravel with his back to the wall and held his hands open on his knees. "Get the sheet out."

She did, and the pencil lead warmed in her fist. He had given her those one at a time all week from inside his coat. He had not been able to pinch one tonight, and she had reached into his coat for them herself without a word.

"From the cleft mouth," said Kunal. "Eighty-one paces to the turn. It doubles back on the left. Forty to the slot, and the slot is seven breaths, out first. Twenty to the weeping stone, and you step on the wet, the third step is the bad one. Then three hundred and four to here." He did not look at his own face for her sake. "Ila, he gave me four lines of a song. I've had it all my life. Everything past the stone, I'm making up as we walk it. I count with you. If I'm out, correct me."

"You're not out." Her pencil went on. "You're not."

"Then it's yours. All of it."

He had not asked what she meant to do with it. She waited for the question, with her hand over the page. A man asks that to buy a thing, and he did not ask it.

"You haven't said what you want for it," said Ila.

"No."

"Why?"

He turned his face up to the lamp. That was not the courtier's face. She had stopped being afraid of that one. This was the other, which looked younger than he was and a good deal more tired.

"Because I want to know what you do with it more than I want to own it," said Kunal.

The cairn hummed. The small stone beside her knee, no higher than her boot, held the note low and level, and her own fingertips were warm when she looked at them.

"That's a bad trade," she said, and her voice came out too light.

"It isn't a trade."

That was the worst of it. She wrote the next figure down and the line shook. She had traded her whole life, a name for a measure, a clause for a clause, and she knew where she stood on every ledger. This was a gift with nothing on the other side of it. She had no column for it.

"I'll draw it," she said.

"I know."

They went on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The way ended in water.

No lake lay there. The stone fell away into a hollow wide as a hall, with a floor that sloped to a black edge. Beyond the edge the dark drank the lamp. The blue stones went down the slope in a neat line and into the water, and she could see them under it, small and steady, a long way down.

The sound came up cold. The ticking was gone. A slow, great pulse had replaced it, as if the water were breathing.

"It goes under," said Kunal.

"It goes down." She walked to the edge and put the lamp low. The cairns went on beneath the surface, a lit stair into black. "I can't say how far. If the water's still, it could be a pool. If it's moving, it's a river, and we'd need rope longer than twelve spans, and the spirits won't take that."

"Dive it?"

"With nothing but a lamp and a bound partner?" She tried to say it lightly, and managed it. "One in two we'd drown, and I'd rather have worse odds on something I could see."

He nodded slowly. "Then we need stores."

"Bread for a night, and oil for less." She shook the lamp and heard it slosh, a third gone. "And a line of days, which we don't have. If we turn now, we're out by dawn. If we push on with half a lamp, we're not out at all."

That was the sum of it. She did it twice. The Meadow Cairn faced south, and the thaw was out there somewhere, soft or hard, and a day turned back was a day gone from the weeks she owed him. *If it runs before we're through.* The sentence stood in her head like a stone.

"We come back," said Kunal.

"We come back."

He sat on the slope with his back to a boulder. He had not let his shoulders down since the cleft, and now he did. She sat opposite, took out the long sheet, and laid it on her knee under the lamp.

She drew.

The line went down thin and black on pale paper, with every count in its small square figure. The hairpin that turned you home. The slot, with *7 br.* beside it and a mark where the stone dragged her rib. The weeping stone. The gravel passage. The blue cairns marked as a dotted line, and the black hollow at its end, where the dots ran under the water and off the edge of the paper.

She lifted the pencil. Every figure closed. It was the best sheet she had ever made, and she knew by now what that was worth.

Kunal had not moved. He was watching her hand, as he always did, with that slightly stunned look.

"Dhanu said to ask," he said. "After. Not before." He kept his eyes on the sheet. "I'm asking."

"What will I do with it."

"Yes."

She looked at the line. She thought of the survey office, and a key she had never been given. She thought of a small yellow star in a margin, and a man's gloves laid palms together on a stone.

"I drew it," said Ila. "I don't know yet whose it is."

The cairn beside her gave its note, steady and unbroken. Her fingertips stayed pink. The answer was true all the way down, which was the only comfort in it.

He breathed out slowly. Not relief, not disappointment: the sound of a man who had expected a lock and been handed a window.

"That's the first true answer anyone's given me about a map," said Kunal.

"It's the only one I have."

She should tell him. The thought came in whole, with the lamp on his face. *There's a letter in my hem, and it says your grandfather walked this way with a warden, and the warden did not come out.* She had the first word of it in her mouth. Her hand lay flat over the stitches of the pattu.

*Through the cleft, and the Eye, and whatever's under it.*

That was what she had said. She had said it with the rope humming, and she had meant every word. And they were not through. There was water under that dark and a stair going down into it. If she told him here, now, with the sheet still wet on her knee, she would be paying a gift with a handful of change. The trade would be tidy and early and cheap.

He had said it wasn't one.

She shut her mouth. Silence was free, and she hated what it cost.

"What?" said Kunal.

"Nothing yet," said Ila. "I'll tell you when I can pay it properly."

He looked at her a long moment. He had paid for his own question with his fingers that morning, and had not asked her twice.

"All right," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They came out at dawn.
