The cleft let them go as quietly as it had taken them, into a grey light that turned the lake to a sheet of pewter. Ila stood on the ice lip with the sheet inside her coat and her legs shaking. She breathed the cold air and tasted nothing in it but morning. The rope hung slack and warm between her wrist and his.

Nobody had come for them. The far shore was bare. The cliff stood where it stood. Raghu's four had gone, or hidden, or were watching something else.

"Gauri will have bread," said Kunal. He sounded very tired. "She'll say nothing about it. She'll hand it over and say nothing."

"I'd marry her for that alone."

He laughed. Then he turned toward the lower end of the lake, where the shore folded in below the Drum and the lodging huts sat in their shelf of ground, and the laugh stopped.

Ila looked where he looked.

Every morning of her time on this lake she had seen a thread of smoke over the huts, grey and straight in the cold, where Gauri banked the fire. It had been there at moonrise yesterday, from the scree. It had stood over those roofs the whole of the week.

The huts stood in their fold of shore, small and dark and square.

There was no smoke.

# Chapter 24: Proof of Life

Kunal was running before he knew it, and the rope took him by the wrist and hauled him round.

"Eight spans," said Ila, behind him, hoarse. "Kunal. I'm coming. Don't pull."

He had pulled. He stood on the shore path with the frozen stones turning under his boots, and the huts were still a hundred paces off, small and dark in their fold below the Drum. No smoke. No goat bell. No Gauri at the door with her sleeves rolled and a rag in her hand, ready to say nothing and hand over bread.

"Walk fast," said Ila. "Don't run. If it's a trap, I'd like to see it before it sees us."

He walked fast. It was the longest hundred paces of his life, and she kept every one of them at his shoulder, her breath loud, the rope between them swinging low.

The door of the second hut hung from a single leather hinge. It had been split down the middle by something heavy, and the wood was pale and raw at the break. Inside, the blanket lay on the floor. The plank shelf where he and Ila had slept was empty and cold.

"Dhanu," he said. Not loudly. Then, loudly: "Dhanu!"

The lake answered with a low note and nothing else.

Dhanu's own hut stood a little apart, on the better ground. Its door was shut. Kunal went to it with the dead hand out in front of him like a man feeling for a wall in the dark, and then he stopped, because he had seen the snow.

It lay trampled in a wide ring before the sill. Many boots had been through it, and it had frozen again in ridges. In one place the ridges were dark. The stain was the width of a spread hand, the colour of old wine, crusted at the rim. Beside it, a sack lay slit and spilled. Dried apricots had been ground into the ice under heels, orange and brown, flat as dropped coins.

Gauri's apricots. She had sent them to the huts by the pony load each autumn from her sister's orchard, and she handed them out like favours. He had eaten them off her palm at twelve.

"Don't touch it," said Ila quietly.

"I wasn't going to."

"You were." She did not say it unkindly. She crouched, rope trailing, and put a bare finger near the stain without quite touching it. "Not much. A cut, not a wound. A man could lose this and walk." She looked up. "A woman could."

He heard what she was doing. She was giving him a measure to stand on. He took it, and hated that he needed it.

There was more. Two grooves ran from the doorway through the apricots and away over the snow, side by side and a boot's width apart. Between them, at long intervals, lay the scuff of a heel that had dug in and not held. Kunal followed the grooves with his eyes to the end of the shore, where the stones gave way to the white of the lake.

They ran straight down to the Drum.

"They didn't carry him," said Ila. "Or her. Not all the way."

"They dragged them to the ice."

"Yes."

"Mini," he said.

Ila was already at Dhanu's door. She put her shoulder to it, looked in, and came back. "The shelf's bare. Her blanket's gone, and her boots." She pointed past the goat pen, where a pony's tracks, older and half filled with blown snow, went away down the valley path and not toward the ice. "Somebody put her on the pony before the boots came. One small rider."

Kunal's breath went out of him. Gauri. Gauri would have heard them on the shore and sent the child down to her sister's orchard before she thought of anything else.

He could not look at the lake. He looked at the door of Dhanu's hut instead, and that was when he saw the paper.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It was pinned to the lintel with an iron nail, driven in at an angle, hard. A scrap of pale hide, the kind herders use for tally marks. Under the nail, looped over its head by its thong, hung a small curved thing the length of a finger.

Dhanu's whistle.

Kunal had heard it on four hundred mornings. He knew how it sat in a man's palm and the smell of it, goat and smoke and Dhanu's skin. It was polished dark where a chest had worn it. Two long notes and one short: *come on, it's clear.* Three short: *turn back.* It had hung against Dhanu's breastbone since before Kunal could walk the ridge.

A man did not take that off over his head and leave it. It had been cut or torn away. The thong was frayed at one end.

He took it down with the good hand. He could not work the nail with the other, so Ila did, wordlessly, and drew out the hide and flattened it against the door with her palm.

The hill script was square and plain, in charcoal. It was not Raghu's hand. It was a clerk's, or a man who wished to look like one.

*The surveyor's sheets, carried by the prince, to the Drum fold by moonrise tomorrow, or the two go under the ice.*

That was all.

He read it three times. By the third reading it had stopped being words and become a shape, and he could see the whole of it at once, like a ridge from the shoulder. *The two.* Not *the lame man.* Not *his wife.* Whoever wrote it had not wasted ink on names. They did not need to. They had left the whistle.

"They're alive," he said.

"The note says they are."

"The whistle says it." He closed his hand on the horn. His voice was steady, which surprised him. "Look at it. They took the one thing he'd never give. They can't show me a finger, so they showed me this. It's proof of life. If they were dead there'd be nothing to trade, and no note."

"That's an inference."

"It's a good one."

"I didn't say it was bad." Ila stood very straight, her hand still flat on the hide. She was looking at the bottom of the scrap, below the last word, where a clean strip of leather lay bare. Her eyes moved along it and back, as they did along a chain. "Kunal. There's no walnut."

He had known it. He had known it from the first reading, in the part of him that heard the marsh-note under a stone before the foot came down. He had put the knowledge aside as one puts aside a bill.

"No," he said.

"You told me the rule. Your own, or his. A walnut with red thread, or the word isn't his." She said it gently, the way she would tell a man his sum was out. "This isn't his word. It was never meant to be. It's theirs."

"I know whose it is."

"Then you know that if Dhanu had time, he'd have sent one." She turned from the door. "He had one on him. You saw him put it in his pouch. He made it, and he'd have used it to say *it's a lie, don't come.* They didn't give him time."

*They didn't give him time.*

She had meant it as a fact, small and dry, to be laid on the table with the others. It went into him like the nail into the door.

He saw it. Dhanu in the doorway with the lamp behind him, the lame foot planted, hearing boots on the shingle. Reaching for the pouch. Not reaching quickly enough.

"No time to send it," said Kunal. "That doesn't make him dead."

"No."

"It makes him alive and taken."

"Yes," said Ila, after a moment. "That's what I said."

It was not what she had said. He let her have it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He walked down to the Drum alone as far as the rope allowed, which was not far, and she came with him, giving him the dignity of silence. The grooves ended at the lake's rim. The ice there was bare, wind-scoured and grey, and it took no print past the first yard. Still, the stones at the edge were scuffed, and a strip of blue wool lay caught between two of them. Gauri's. Or a stranger's.

Out on the Drum a long note rolled up from the depths and passed under his feet.

Where the Drum answers, lie still till morning.

He stood in the wind with the whistle in his fist and did the sum, because that was what she would have done, and because his own head would not do anything else.

Moonrise tomorrow. A day and a night and part of another. The fold lay at the far end of the lake, under the cliff, and a man could reach it in the time it took to eat. He would be there with her sheets, or without them. If without, they would be in the water.

He could not fight them. Raghu had eight bound pairs in the Hollow Moon, sixteen men, and one of them wrote in a good clerk's hand. He could not send for the council. Bhim would debate it. Dhanu's runner boy was a child, and every other boy was a day's walk off. He had no one. He had his dead fingertips and a surveyor who had lied to him once, and told him so by dawn.

And he had what she carried. Five sheets, sewn in a lining, with every turn of the hills drawn true. The Stair. The Moon. The way into the Eye, past the weeping stone, down into the dark. He had put it into her hands himself last night, with his palms open. *Then it's yours. All of it.*

Last night he had meant it as the first free thing he had ever done.

Now it was a price.

He thought of it without flinching, which was the horror of it. It came to him whole, neat, and almost easy, as a clean line comes to a draughtsman. They wanted the sheets. He would give them the sheets. The princes' way for Dhanu. The hills' one door for one lame man with a bad foot who had taught him to throw knucklebones and let him lose.

Every argument Dhanu had made at the fire before the Eye stood up in him and went on standing. *A garrison is a house with soldiers in it, and the house goes where the way goes.* It was all true. He would still sell it.

His uncle's voice came back to him over a supper table, mild and reasonable, with a boy of nine on the bench beside him and the funeral meats still on the cloth. *Whoever knows the way owns the people, Kunal. Remember it. Everything else is manners.*

Twenty-four winters he had spent hating that sentence. He had built a council on its opposite. He had stood before the hills and said that no door belonged to one man.

And here he stood, with a whistle in his hand, ready to be the man who owned the way. Not to rule with it. Worse. To spend it. To count it out coin by coin on a frozen shore, as Pritam would have, and call it love.

Whoever knows the way owns the people, and whoever owns the people may sell them, one at a time, to buy back the one he cannot do without.

*I'd rather be wrong that way than right in the way I've been.* He had said that at the fire before the Eye, with his hand on a walnut. He had not known that being wrong might come with a price in blood.

It did not feel like a defeat. That was what frightened him. It felt like clarity, like walking out of a dark room into a lit one, and every creed he owned lay behind him on the floor.

He pulled the thong over his head. The whistle settled against his chest where Dhanu's had sat, and the cold of it went through his shirt like iron.

"Kunal," said Ila.

"I need you to listen to me."

"I've been listening for a quarter hour. You haven't said anything."

"I know." He turned. The rope swung between them. She stood three paces off with her arms wrapped across her coat, her hair crusted white at one temple where the lake had breathed on it. She looked small, and she looked like the person on the whole mountain who understood best what he was about to do. "I'll say it plainly, because you taught me to. They want your sheets, carried by me. They'll let them go if they get them. I believe that. I have to believe it. I'll go to the fold tomorrow at moonrise and give them what they ask."

She did not move. A stillness came over her face, the one that came over it when a closure would not come out.

"You'd give them the princes' way."

"I'd give them every turn I know. And every turn you drew." It came out level, and he was proud of that, and ashamed of the pride. "Last night I said it was yours. It is. I'm not taking it. I'm asking for it."

"Asking."

"For one night. For one man." He heard how the words sounded on the cold air. "Ila, I made the council swear to Dhanu's walnut rule, and I carry the knots in my teeth because of what I've held back from everyone for twenty years. I'm done holding. Dhanu told me I'd never given anything I couldn't take back. I'm giving this, and I can't take it back, and it's for him. I'm sorry. I know what it costs the hills. I know what it costs you."

"You don't."

"Then tell me."

"Not yet." She stood with her hand pressed flat to the hem of her pattu, where the letter lay, as she had at the cairn. He had asked once, and paid for it, and not asked again. "Kunal, what happens to the route-keepers on the Stair, and the Moon, after Raghu has the sheets?"

"I don't know."

"You do. You've known since I said it. A man who draws a bearing for a captain isn't a witness he leaves standing. Dhanu's the one who knows where the lines run. If they have the sheets, they don't need Dhanu."

It was a fair argument, and the kind he would have made himself, an hour earlier, from the other side. He found he had no place to put it.

"If I go with nothing," he said, "they die for certain. If I go with the sheets, there's a chance. A bad one, I know. I've done the odds." He swallowed. "I'd say one in three that they walk off that ice. Maybe less. It's all I have to buy."

"One in three isn't what I'd have said."

"What would you have said?"

She did not answer. She looked at the whistle on his chest, then at his face, and her mouth set in a line he had last seen when she measured the pen wall at Ewe's Rest and told Gauri it was a hand short.

The wind turned. Under the ice the Drum spoke again, a slow, deep stroke, and every stone on the shore rang with it. The rope stirred between them. He did not know what she would say. He knew only that every answer she had would be true.

Once, in a cleft, he had asked her to hand him a way. She had said *I drew it, I don't know yet whose it is.* It was the only honest answer he had ever heard about a map.

Now he needed another.

"Where are the sheets?" said Kunal.

Ila opened her mouth.

# Chapter 25: Burned

Ila could have said she did not know. It was a small lie, and her fingertips would have told him so in the time it took to blink.

She told him a truth instead, because truth was what she had.

"The Drum fold sits under a cornice," she said. "North shore, forty paces off the ice. The ledge hangs over the shingle like a lip. A man up there sees the whole fold, the lake, and both ways in. Two more rises look down on it from east and west."

Kunal said nothing. The whistle hung on his chest.

"I measured it from the mouth of the Eye, on the first morning, with a sight bar. The figures closed." She made her voice flat, the way she would for a clerk. "Three sightlines, Kunal. Nobody walks into that fold unseen, and nobody walks out unless the men on the ledge allow it. You'll hand them the sheets, and they'll kill you."

"That's not an inference."

"It is. It's a good one." She held his eyes. "You know the way in your head. A man who knows the way can share it. Your uncle taught you what to do with that sort of man. Raghu learned the same lesson at the same table."

He looked at the lake. A long note rolled up through the ice and went on under their boots.

"Then I won't give them the true set," said Kunal slowly. His voice had changed. He sounded like a man picking up a tool he had sworn never to touch. "Draw them another. You can, in an hour. A wrong turn at the slot. A bad count at the weeping stone. Let them walk it for days."

"No."

"Ila."

"They'll test it." She said it quickly, to get the first part out before the second. "Raghu isn't a fool. He'll put Dhanu on the front of the rope and walk him down your wrong turn, and Dhanu will find out what a false line is in the dark, with his bad foot. That's the first reason."

"And the second?"

"I don't draw false lines." It sounded smaller aloud than it had in her head. "I never have. Not for the crown. Not for a bribe. Not once in eleven winters. A line is true or it isn't, and if I make one that isn't, then I don't know what I am. I'd sooner lose the sheets to a fire."

She heard what she had said and wished the word back.

He took it badly, and then he took it well. She watched both happen. His mouth twisted. Then his shoulders came down and he nodded, as though she had named a bearing and he had checked it.

"You'd say that if it were your sister on the ice."

"I'd say it and hate myself."

"Yes," said Kunal. "I know." He rubbed his face with the heel of the dead hand. "Then it's the true ones. I'll carry them. I asked you for them a quarter hour ago and I'm asking again. Hand them over, and I'll be at the fold by moonrise."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wind dropped. The ice went quiet.

Five sheets lay sewn into her pattu, with a queen's letter below them in the hem. Last night he had put the princes' way into her palms and asked for nothing. This morning he asked for it back, and he was right to ask. She would have asked herself.

She did the sums the way she did all sums, in a straight line.

If she handed them over, Raghu had the Stair, the Moon, the Eye, and a cairn-lit road down into the mountain. Hari's goats would go on being driven up roads that had been drawn for someone else. Every route-keeper who had ever scratched a warning on a stone would be a witness Raghu didn't need standing. And Kunal would walk onto the shingle under the cornice and be neatly, quietly done with.

If she said no, he would ask again. He would ask a third time at dusk. At the fold, in front of Raghu, his eyes would slide to her coat, as a man's eyes do toward whatever he is trying not to look at. Raghu would see it. A man who has the way in his head is worth keeping alive, if you can't have the paper. A man who has the paper in his friend's lining is a different sum.

But a man who thought the sheets were ash would not look at her coat.

They might strip her anyway. Raghu would search a surveyor on principle, and a woman with sewn seams was a risk. Still, Kunal's eyes would not send them there, and that was a risk she could carry for his life.

The thought settled in her like a plumb bob coming to rest. It was also what she had refused, a minute ago, on principle. She had said she would not draw a false line. She had not said a word about her tongue.

Why not burn them, then, and make it true? She had offered fire herself. But the sheets were the only true record of these hills she would ever have: Hari's scratched warnings copied stone by stone, every turn Kunal had given her, and the map she meant to share with both courts at the same hour. Burn them and the hills went back to whoever held the ways in his head. And to burn them here, in front of him, would be one more thing he stood and watched go.

She thought of Meera at the drafting table in Garh Neshar, laying a left glove on the cloth with a thumb and two fingers and a gap where the ring and the little finger had been. *A great lie kills flesh,* Meera had said, quite cheerfully, while Rohan stared at his plate. *You'll know it's great when it's about something that matters.* Kunal had sat silent through two of those sessions. He had watched that glove. He knew exactly what one touch of her hand would teach him.

He waited with his hands open.

"They're gone," said Ila.

She said it too softly, so she said it again. "I burned them. This morning, when we came in. There were coals banked under ash. They're gone."

The cold took her hands first. It went in under the gloves like a blade laid flat, and her fingertips were gone before she finished the sentence. She folded her arms across her ribs and tucked both hands into her armpits as though she were only hugging herself against the wind.

Kunal did not move.

"You burned them," he said.

"Yes."

"The Stair. The Moon. The Eye."

"All of them."

He looked at her as if she had spoken in a language he had once known. His face went through it. Disbelief came first, then something nearly like relief, the feeling of a man told that the weight he must lift was a trick of the light. Last came the plain horror of the sum.

"You drew the Eye last night," said Kunal. "You had it at dawn. I watched you put it in your coat."

"I know what you watched."

"Then—"

"The coals were alive under the ash when we came in. You were shouting for Dhanu and your back was to me." She heard herself and thought, with the cold part of her that kept on measuring, that it was a thin story. She said nothing more. Silence was free, and every added word was one more she would pay for.

"There was no smoke," said Kunal.

"Banked coals don't give any."

He looked at her a moment longer. She saw him weigh it, find it light, and decide, with a small movement of his jaw, not to say so. He let it go. He chose it, and she saw him choose.

He stepped toward her. For one long moment she thought he would put out the good hand and take hers. She knew exactly what he would learn.

He stopped a pace off. He put his hands in his coat.

"Why?" he said.

"Don't ask me why."

"I'm asking."

"Then I'm refusing," said Ila. "For free."

The word hung over the ice. He heard it and did not know what to do with it. It was not a lie, which was her whole hope. It was a locked door, and he was a man who had been raised among locked doors.

"All right," said Kunal, very quietly. "I'll look."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The second hut still smelled of Gauri's tea. Ila sat on the plank shelf and pulled her boots off.

The cold had reached her feet while she walked up the shore path, an inch at a time. First came a numbness like the end of a bad winter day, then a wooden feeling, then nothing. She unlaced the left one and looked at the sock. The skin of her toes looked ordinary. That was the worst of it. She had expected it to look like what it was.

She pulled the boots on again, as tight as the laces would go, and tied them off in a double hitch. Anything that made the foot feel held would help. If she could not feel the ground, she would at least not lose the boot.

Behind her Kunal knelt at the hearth. He took a poker from the hook and stirred the ash, and grey dust rose and settled in the thin light from the door. He lifted a clot of cinder in his dead fingers and crumbled it. He held a black curl to the light.

"Bark," he said. "Or paper."

"Could be either."

"It's thin." He turned it. "It's thin enough." He put it down and turned over more, lips moving. He was counting. She knew the shape of that count, because it was hers. He was checking whether the ash was enough ash for five sheets, and it was not a question the ash could answer.

She kept her hands in her sleeves and her knees together. When he looked round, she was lacing the right boot, bent over it, so that her hair hid her face.

"Your hands," he said.

Her stomach dropped. "What about them?"

"They're cold. You came out of the Eye with them cold. You've been wrapping them in your coat since the shore." He said it mildly, a man noting a measurement. "You should put them near the stove."

"There's no fire."

"No." He looked at the grey hearth. "No, there isn't."

He did not take her hands. He did not say *show me*. He sat back on his heels, and she watched him look at the ash and then at her bowed head. He had spent twenty years reading rooms, and he had put all of it down. He wanted to believe her more than he wanted to know. She would remember that whenever it hurt, and it was going to hurt.

"I'm sorry," said Kunal, and it startled her. "That I asked you to hand them over. That I asked twice. I said it was yours, last night. I meant it as a gift, not a trade." He rubbed his thumb along the poker's iron. "I was going to spend it at the first price anyone named. Maybe it's as well you got there before I did."

"Don't," said Ila.

"It's true."

"I know it's true." She tied the knot. Her fingers did not work on it, so she did it with her wrists. "That's why I'd rather you didn't."

He laughed once, wretchedly, and stood up and hung the poker on its hook.

"Then there's nothing to give them," he said.

"No."

"They'll want the sheets. Raghu will want to know where they are, and I'll tell him the truth and he won't believe it." He stood in the doorway with the light behind him, and she could not see his face. "Moonrise isn't the hour any more. They'll have a man at the shingle by then, with a spyglass. If I go tonight, at dusk, I'm one man with a rope and a prince's name and nothing in my hands. He can't search a thing that isn't there. And if Dhanu's been waiting a day and a night on the ice, I don't want him to wait another."

"Kunal."

"If I'm all there is," said Kunal, "I'll be all there is." The whistle shone on his coat. "I'll offer myself. They want the hills' way. I'm the one man who can walk it without a paper. Maybe that's worth something to a man who needs a door opened."

It was what she had hoped, laid out in the plainest words. She had meant it as a hope and not a plan.

"The rope," said Ila.

"Yes." He did not look at her. "The rope says you come."

"I know what the rope says."

"I'd leave you here if I could." He said it as flatly as a bearing. "You know I'd leave you here."

"I know."

She did. It was almost the only thing she knew. She stood up on feet that were no longer quite hers, and the floor held.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They went down at the hour when the cliff swallowed the sun.

The shore had gone the colour of a bruise. Ice crept out from the stones in long grey fingers, and the Drum lay beyond, white and flat, with the first of the dusk stars pricking out above the far rim. Ila walked behind Kunal on the shingle and counted her steps under her breath, because it was what her feet did.

*Forty. Forty-one.*

Her feet had stopped telling her what they were doing somewhere around the sixtieth pace. She found the ground by looking at it. She set each boot down where it ought to go and trusted it, the way she had once trusted a chain she had not yet pulled tight.

Kunal did not turn his head. He had the whistle in one fist and the rope loose in the other, and he held his shoulders as he had in the cleft. In the hut, he had not asked again. The question rode his back all the same, and neither of them named it.

*Ninety. Ninety-one.*

Her hands were as dead as stones inside her sleeves. It was a mercy, in its way. She could not feel them shaking.

*I did not draw a false line,* she told herself. *Not one.* It was a ridiculous thing to hold onto. She clung to it all the same, one true coin in a purse of counterfeits, and she knew what it was worth.

A wind came up off the lake and smelled of iron and cold water. Below the shore, the ice answered it with a slow boom, and the pulse went through her boots and up her shins and ended somewhere near the knee, where the feeling stopped.

"Ila," said Kunal. "Are you with me?"

"Eight spans," she said. "I'm here."

"You've gone quiet."

"I'm counting."

"What?"

She looked down at her boots. They were not moving the way she had told them to. Her left foot came forward and set itself down, and she waited for the feel of it and nothing came. It was like standing on a drawing of a foot.

"Everything," said Ila. "I'm counting everything."

The shingle ended. The ice began. She put a boot on it, and it was not her boot any more, and the other one was nowhere, and the rope pulled hard at her wrist and then let go. She heard Kunal say her name from a long way off. The lake rolled its drum-note up through the dusk, and the stars swung slowly over, and the grey ice came up to meet her.

# Chapter 26: The Drum Fold

Ila did not wake when he cut her laces, and that frightened him more than the ice had.

He had dragged her off the lake and laid her on the shingle with his coat under her head. The rope lay between their wrists in a slack loop. Dusk was going fast. Out on the Drum the ice held the last light like a pewter plate, and under the cliff, a short walk off, the fold waited with no lamp in it. He did not look at the fold.

Her boots had frozen into a hitch he could not pick. He sawed the lace with his knife, the handle pinned in the heel of his dead hand, and worked the left boot off, then the sock, which came away stiff as bark.

Her foot was white to the instep. Not pale. White as tallow, with a shine on it.

He put both palms around it.

The cold came up his arms the way water climbs a wick. He had known hill cold all his life, the honest kind that bites and lets go. This had a size to it. It poured into his wrists and kept pouring, and with it came a fact as plain as a stone set on a table: *a lie was told, and it was great.*

He did not learn what the truth was. The cold did not carry that. It carried only how heavy the hidden thing was, and that told him how much it mattered.

*They're gone. I burned them.*

"Don't," said Ila. Her eyes had opened. Her voice was thick. "Kunal. Your hands."

"Be quiet."

"It's mine. I paid for it."

"You did." He held on until the cold stopped arriving. His palms went past burning into something worse, and when he lifted them they were red and tight, with blisters rising along the lines of his fingers. "Now we've both paid."

The foot lay between them. It had pinked up, all but the edge. The two smallest toes were the colour of old candle-ends, with a dull grey bloom under the nails. He touched one with a knuckle. It gave back no warmth.

The cold had reached them before he did.

"Those two," he said.

"I know." She had pushed herself up on one elbow and was looking at her own foot as she would at a stranger's chain. "I couldn't feel them to know. I'd have guessed by dark."

"You told me they were ash."

"I did."

"They aren't ash." He heard how level he sounded. It was the voice he used in council when he wanted to bleed a man quietly. "A great lie, and about the sheets. So you have them. You've had them sewn in your coat the whole morning while I knelt at that hearth counting cinders."

She said nothing. Silence was free, and he had taught himself to hate it in other people's mouths.

"Then I'll do the sum," said Kunal. "There's one person in these hills who'd want them kept from Raghu and from me. One person who has your oath. You've carried that letter sewn into you since the Fair and refused me twice, and now this." He pulled the sock over the ruined toes with the flat of his hand, and it was not gentle. "You'd let Dhanu die for your queen's map."

She took her hands out of her sleeves.

She held them up between them in the dusk, open, the way he had held his own at the fire before the Eye. The fingertips were pink. The nails were clear.

"They're not for the queen," said Ila. "Or Raghu. And they're not for trading you."

Her hands stayed warm.

It was a true answer, and it cost her nothing, and that was the trouble with it. He would have taken a lie. A lie he could have hated cleanly.

"Then what are they for?"

"That's free too," she said. "I won't say tonight."

"Dhanu is on that ice."

"I know where he is." Her voice shook once and steadied. "You asked once what I'd have said, if I'd put a figure to it. If you'd handed them over, I'd have said one in ten. Less. I'd have said it and watched you hate me."

"I might have been the one."

"Yes. You might." She looked at him. "I'd say it again."

He got his arm under her knees and the other behind her back, and stood, and the blisters split across his palms. He did not let it reach his face. A rope's length of cold air lay between him and the fold. He walked into it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The shingle under the cliff was a grey strip, forty paces from the ice to the rock. He had known it since he was nine, when Dhanu taught him to skip stones here. Now it had a lantern on it, shuttered to a slit, and men.

Two pairs stood on the cornice above, four dark shapes against the last of the sky, the rope between each pair hanging in a curve. One held something to his eye that caught the light. Spyglass. Kunal felt it settle on his chest like a thumb.

Raghu waited at the water's edge, bound at the wrist by eight spans of grey wool to a boy.

Kunal had met the captain twice, across the Garh Neshar hall, when Raghu wore the regent's bell on his shoulder and a courtier's smile. He wore no smile now. He was a lean man gone leaner, with ice in his beard and the careful stillness of someone who had not slept since the Snow Fair. The boy tied to him was perhaps twenty. Grey wool, a brass bell on the shoulder, a face too young for the cold on it. Pasang. Kunal knew the name from a Ranu story Meera had told, and could not now have said which story.

"Prince," said Raghu. "You've brought me a woman and no paper."

"I've brought you me."

"I asked for the sheets."

"They're ash." He said it flatly. He had decided on the walk that it was the one thing he could say with a steady face, because it was what Ila had said, and he would stand on her word if it was the last thing he stood on. Her breath was warm against his collar. "I'll give you every turn I know. I walked the Stair. I walked the Moon. I stood in the Eye, and I can walk it again. Take me. I'm the way you wanted. I don't need paper to open it."

"A man who can't produce a thing is an inconvenient man."

"Then I'm inconvenient."

Raghu studied him, as a clerk studies a coin. Kunal let his eyes go past the captain's shoulder, out onto the grey ice, and there they stopped.

Dhanu lay on the Drum, twenty paces out. He lay on his back with one arm flung wide, the lame foot turned in. A scrap of blue wool lay across his chest. He was entirely still.

Beside him, on her side with her knees drawn up, lay Gauri. A strip of cloth was knotted across her mouth. Her wrists were bound before her, and a cord ran from her belt to an iron peg driven into the ice. Her eyes were open. They met his, and they were as dry as stones.

"Dhanu," said Kunal.

The lake gave him back a low note.

"Dhanu."

"He's resting," said Raghu.

Something in Kunal's chest dropped away from its footing. He had been ready to hear that Dhanu was hurt, or starved, or frightened. He had walked the whole shore with Ila's *one in ten* in his ear. He had not been ready for the plain flat fact of a man lying very still on ice.

"Captain," said Pasang, low. He was staring where Kunal stared. His voice cracked. "Sir. You told me the lame one was only..."

"The limping one went home this morning, Pasang," said Raghu, loudly, in the voice of a man reading a notice. "You saw him go. That is a farmer from the east fold. Keep your eyes on the prince."

The boy's mouth closed. He looked at the ice and then, quickly, away.

Ila's fingers dug into Kunal's shoulder.

"His hand," she breathed. "Raghu's. Look at his hand."

It was resting on the hilt of his sword, and the fingertips were white. They were white to the first joint, bloodless, like a row of peeled almonds.

Kunal looked at them and could make nothing of it. A lie, small, told to the boy at his wrist. The limping one went home. And Dhanu lay out there where anyone could see.

He could not do the sum. His head would not take it. Whatever Raghu was hiding from Pasang, it was not that a man was dead, because the whole shore could see that. It was something else, and the thing in front of him was Dhanu's open, frosted face.

"The sheets," said Raghu softly, "or the ice takes the woman. And I'll start with her, so you can watch."

He lifted two fingers to the cornice.

"Eleven paces left of the boom line," said Ila, clear and loud, into Kunal's ear, "then straight."

He ran.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Ila's soundings were in his legs before his mind caught up. He had stood at her shoulder on the Drum, the morning she opened the letter, and watched her chip her holes with his own knife and the iron spike, tapping the haft, listening for the ring. She had called every figure aloud. *Eight fingers. Eleven. Seven here, don't.* He had laughed at her. He had said nobody sounded the Drum, because the Drum could be heard.

Now he ran with her weight crushing his blisters and his heart beating in the burst skin of his palms, and her voice came steady as a chain-man's tally.

"Boom line, there, the pale seam, it's on your right. Eleven paces. Count them. Don't look at it."

He counted. Behind him a shout went up, Raghu's, then the dry slap of boots on shingle and then on ice. He did not turn his head.

"Straight. Straight. The ice is fourteen here. It's good, Kunal. Hold the line."

Behind them a man yelled, "Cut across! Cut him off!" And a second answered, close, and the rhythm of the boots changed. They were coming on the chord, the short way, through the pale seam. The place where the Drum was thinnest was exactly where a man in a hurry would think it safest.

"Don't look," said Ila.

The ice spoke.

It was a single note, enormous, rolling up through the soles of his boots and into his teeth, and then it broke into a sound he had never heard from the Drum, a wet tearing like cloth ripped under water. A man screamed. Another screamed with him. The rope that tied them together dragged the second one under with the first.

Kunal slowed. He could not stop himself. Over his shoulder he saw two dark shapes in a black hole, white with spray, and a rope thrashing at the lip. Then the cornice men were shouting, and Raghu was shouting, and the whole shore was turned toward the break.

"Go on," said Ila, very quietly. "They're gone. Go on."

He did not go on. He veered.

Gauri lay twelve paces to his left, with the lantern's slit of light flung across her bound hands. She had pulled herself up on her elbows. Above the gag her eyes were wide and furious.

He dropped to one knee on the ice, Ila clamped against his chest. The knife was already out. He could not feel the handle. He pinned it with the heel of his dead hand, and sawed the cord at her belt, then the cloth between her wrists. The edge went through her skin as well as the wool, and she hissed. She tore the gag off herself.

"The huts," said Kunal. "Run. Don't stop."

She did not look at him. She looked at Dhanu, lying a few paces away with the blue wool on his chest. Her face was perfectly steady, and he had never seen anything so terrible.

"Gauri."

"I know," she said. It came out a rasp. She put one hand flat on Dhanu's coat, as a woman checks bread, and took it away. "Go."

She ran. She ran badly, bent over, her cut wrists held to her chest, her legs driving at the shingle. Behind her the shore men were wheeling toward the hole in the ice, and not one of them looked at a woman running the other way.

He should have gone to Dhanu. He knew it. He should have knelt, and shut the eyes, and said something, anything. Frost lay in the lashes. A scrap of blue wool lay across the chest. He had known this man thirty years.

"Kunal," said Ila.

"I know."

"We can't."

"I *know*," said Kunal, and ran.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


There was no word for the hour that came after. He ran along the shore until the shingle gave out, and walked when he could not run, and walked when he could not walk, with the rope dragging and the lantern shouts growing small behind. Ila gave him bearings in the dark. Once she said, "Put me down, you can't," and he said, "I can," and she did not say it again.

His hands had stopped being hands. They were two sheets of burning where something wet ran, and he held her with them anyway, because the alternative was to put her down.

They crossed the neck of rock between the Drum and the Eye by the blue stones, which he could see now, faint and steady, a thin chain pulling him on. Raghu's men were bound. They would see them too, if they came. He did not let himself think of it. He kept his eyes on the next cairn, and on the next.

The cleft opened out of the cliff like a shut mouth beginning to speak. He carried her in past the lip and the ice hollow and set her down on the dry gravel, against the wall. Then he stood and looked at what they had.

Nothing. His pack lay at the hut, with the flint and the lamp oil and the last of the bread. Hers was with it. The cleft held rock, and cold, and the sound of water far down, and no fuel. He had pushed a prince's name and nothing in his hands onto a frozen lake, and here was the whole of what that bought.

Ila had her bad foot drawn up under her. She was shaking so hard her teeth clicked. She did not complain, and she did not ask to be warm. She looked at him, and he saw her get ready to say something true.

"Don't," said Kunal.

"I only..."

"Don't say anything true to me tonight." He sat down against the opposite wall, with eight spans of wool between them and his blistered hands lying open on his knees like something he had borrowed. "I'm not sure what I'll do with it."

She shut her mouth. The rope lay slack across the gravel between them.

Behind them, on the ice, beneath a sky going black, Dhanu lay with his face turned up and nobody to cover it. Kunal sat in the dark and counted his own breathing, because it was all there was to count, and did not know yet whether he would hate her by morning, or only wish he could.

# Chapter 27: Past the Rope

The cleft had no fire, and by the second hour Ila could not feel her feet.

She had stopped trying to. She sat against the gravel wall where Kunal had set her, knees drawn up, hands pushed into her armpits. The cold in the cleft was dry and patient. It came up out of the rock and did not care whose it was.

The blue stones ran away into the dark like a string of beads. They were the only light. Kunal sat across from her with eight spans of grey wool lying slack on the gravel between them. He had not spoken since he told her not to say anything true.

His hands lay open on his knees. In the faint light she could see the blisters had burst, and the palms shone wet. The fingertips on the right were grey. He did not tuck them away or blow on them. He looked at them the way she had seen him look at the Drum from the shore, as if they were a distance he was measuring.

She had told herself a number of things to pass the time. That the toes would go. That two toes were a fair price for a prince's life, and a fair price was a thing she understood. That he was in pain and she should let him be.

None of it moved the cold, and none of it moved him.

"Kunal."

He did not look up.

"You'll want to see to your hands. There's a strip left on my hem. I can't reach it. You could—"

"Ila." He said it quietly. "Don't."

She stopped.

After a while he got up. He did it slowly, a hand on the wall, the way a man gets up from a sickbed. He stood looking down the passage toward the mouth, toward the hollow under the ice lip and the lake beyond it. The whistle gleamed against his coat.

Then he raised his left wrist and put the rope's knot to his teeth.

She understood what he was doing a moment too late. The wool was wet and the knot was hers, the warden's double hitch she had first tied at the Meadow Cairn with cold fingers and a good deal of hope. He worked it with his teeth and the heel of his hand. It took him a long time. She watched every turn of it come loose.

Her own end stayed tied at her wrist. His came free. He did not drop it. He wound the loose end twice round his fist and closed the fist on it.

"It ends nothing," said Ila. Her voice came out cracked. "You know it ends nothing."

"I know."

"Then—"

"Dhanu is out there on the ice with a scrap of wool on his chest and nobody has shut his eyes." He did not turn round. "Gauri put her hand on him as if she were testing bread, and then she ran, because I told her to. Raghu is still there. I'm going to walk back."

"They're bound pairs on the cornice. Four of them, with a spyglass."

"Yes."

"You'll be one man with no paper."

"Yes."

He walked.

She watched his back go down the passage between the blue stones. The slack slid off the gravel and followed him in a long grey curve. At the sixth stone it began to straighten. At the eighth span it snapped taut, and she felt it at her wrist, a thin hard tug, with his fist at the far end of it.

The cold hit her then.

It was nothing like lie-cold. That had come in at the fingertips, a blade laid flat. This came from everywhere at once, as though the whole cleft had drawn a breath and held it. Her breath smoked. Her chest tightened. The heat she had been hoarding in her armpits drained out of her like water from a cracked jug.

The blue stones dimmed. She watched it happen. The faint line that had run so steadily down the passage went thin, and thinner, and the stone nearest her guttered like a lamp in a draught.

His shoulders jerked. He stood at the end of the wool, not yet past it, and did not turn.

*One long pull,* she thought.

It was Meera's signal, one long pull for *come*, and every pair in the high valleys knew it. The rope was taut and she had it at her wrist. She could haul on it with both arms and her whole weight against the wall, and it would drag at his fist like a halter. It was not even a lie. It was only a pull. He would turn, because the rope would turn him.

She took hold of the wool with her swollen hands. It was a long way off from her body, and she held it a long time.

Then she let it go slack in her fingers.

It would have been easy to call it kindness. It was closer to arithmetic. If she hauled him back, she would have a man at her side who had been hauled, and he would sit across from her until the thaw knowing she had done it. She had spent a week learning this man's habits. He could bear a good deal, but he hated to be made to stay.

She was cold and afraid, and he was going. But the choice was his, and she would not take it from him.

Far down the passage, his fist opened. The loose end of the wool fell and lay across the gravel like something shed. He walked on.

She filled her lungs. The air hurt going in.

"Kunal!"

His step did not break.

"I lied to keep you alive," said Ila. The cleft threw the words back at her, one at a time, down the long dark. "I told you the sheets were ash, and they weren't. I'd do it again."

Nothing happened. Her fingers in her armpits stayed numb, but they were only cold. No new chill came. The stones did not ring. The truth went out of her and the mountain let it.

"I'd do it again," she said, quieter, to herself. "Every time."

He was a shape between the blue stones, small now. She counted him. It was the only thing she could do. *Eleven spans. Twelve.* The cold had gone from a hand to a fist. Her teeth had begun to knock.

*Thirteen.*

She could not feel her legs at all now, only a heavy, drowned ache from the hip down. She had told him that at twice the length the path vanished. She had read it in every chronicle the survey office held and thought it quaint.

*Fourteen. Fifteen.*

He stopped.

She could not see his face. His shape had stopped, the shoulders had come down, and he stood very still in the thin blue dark, with the passage opening ahead of him toward the lake and the dead and Raghu's lantern.

She did not call again. There was nothing true left that she hadn't said.

Then he did something she had not expected. He put his ruined hands to his own face.

A long time went by. The cold squeezed. Somewhere deep in the rock the meltwater ticked on stone, once, twice, a slow, hateful sound.

Kunal turned round.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He came back slowly, like a man walking up out of deep water. The blue stones brightened as he passed them, one after another, and the iron fist of cold loosened in her chest by degrees. Her breath came easier. Her face was wet before she knew why, and then the tears went cold on her cheeks.

He stopped a pace from her. He did not kneel.

"Your life is behind me," said Kunal. His voice was rough and very low. "That's the sum. I got to fifteen spans and it was the only figure left. If I go on, I go on alone to the cornice, and the path goes at sixteen, and we both die in a gully a night's walk from his body. He'd still be on the ice. Raghu would still be breathing." He looked at his hands. "I'd have done it. I wanted to. I want you to know I wanted to."

"I know."

"Don't be kind about it."

"I'm not," said Ila. "I'm cold."

It startled a breath of laughter out of him, a cracked and ugly thing. He sat down heavily on the gravel. For a moment she thought he would put his head on his knees and stay that way.

"I don't forgive you," he said.

"I didn't ask you to."

"You lied to me. You lied about the one thing I begged you for. You stood there with your hands in your sleeves and said *gone,* and I believed it because I wanted to, and a man died on the ice while I knelt at a hearth counting cinders." He drew a breath. It shook. "You say you'd do it again. I believe you. That's what I can't—" He stopped.

"Say it."

"You'd do it again," said Kunal, "and you'd be right, and I hate it."

She had no answer to that. It was true, so she said nothing. Silence was free.

He did not leave her there. That was the thing she would remember after the thaw, when she turned the night over and read it like a sheet. He did not forgive her, and he did not go. He reached out with one blistered hand, and with the other he took hold of her ankle. He drew her bad foot toward him through the gravel, as one drags a stray lamb closer to the fold.

"Kunal, the cold—"

"I know what it is. I've had it." He worked the bad boot off, then the sound one, the laces already cut. He unbuttoned his coat and the shirt under it. Then he lay down on his side along the gravel, back to the wall, and pulled both her feet against the bare skin of his belly. He drew the coat shut over them.

She gasped. He was warm. He was warm like a hearth.

"Don't talk," he said into the dark. "I can't be kind and not talk to you at once. Pick one."

"Not talking."

"Good."

The hands lay on her ankles, burnt and rough, and held. His breath moved slowly under her heels. After a while his grip stopped being the grip of a man who held a thing and became the grip of a man who held on.

They lay like that for hours.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At some point the dark went thin and she was awake, and the cold had changed.

Her feet hurt. That was good. A foot that hurt had a foot attached. She lay with her cheek on the gravel and her eyes open and watched the blue stones pulse, slow and faint, as the mountain breathed.

She thought of her grandfather.

She had not meant to. She had spent eleven winters not meaning to. Warden Harish, who went out in a night storm to check the cairns for a lost party. Her father, over his clock and his one good eye, saying the gloves were like a pair set out for the morning. A flat stone. Two gloves laid palms together, square to the edge, the fingers pointing the way he had been walking.

She had heard that sentence once, from a tired old man, and had turned it over ever since. She had found it again, plain as a receipt, in the queen's hand. It sat in her like a stone in a shoe.

She had known since the shelf above the Drum what it meant. A man did not take off his gloves at the end of a storm night by accident, and he did not lay them square by accident. It was a payment-sign. It said *here, this is given.* She had worked that out on that cold shelf, reading the queen's hand with her finger under the line, like a sum.

She had not understood why anyone would choose it. Now she did, and it was worse. A person who has gone cold through, who thinks nobody is coming and nobody will forgive him, wants one thing left to do properly. To set something down square. To make the ending tidy, because it is the only warm thing within reach.

A flat stone lay beside her head. It had come out of the cleft wall at some point, a slab as long as her forearm, tilted a little toward the passage. She stared at it.

Slowly, with fingers she could not feel, she pulled off the left glove, then the right. She laid them on the stone. She turned them palm to palm, the left on the right, and set the fingers pointing down the passage, toward the dark, the way a person would walk.

She stopped with her hand a finger's breadth above them.

It would be so easy to finish. The mountain was asking nothing she had not already half agreed to. She was cold, and she was alone in a way that had nothing to do with the man at her feet, because he had said he did not forgive her, and he meant it. He held her because the alternative was to let her die, and that was a different thing from love.

Her grandfather's answer lay on the stone in front of her. It was clear, and it was cold, and she was so tired.

She picked up the gloves.

She did not do it grandly. She did it the way she would put a pencil back in her pocket. She worked her hands into the wool, one finger at a time, and sat with them in her lap.

*Not yet,* thought Ila. *I know that door. I'm not walking through it.*

Against her feet, Kunal's belly rose and fell. He had not stirred. She thought he was asleep, and then she thought he was not.

"What were you doing?" he said into the dark.

"Counting."

He said nothing. The rope lay across the gravel, its free end where he had let it fall. Neither of them touched it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The light came late.

First it was only a greyness at the end of the passage, where the ice lip hung over the hollow. Then it was a line. The blue stones went pale and ordinary in it. Her feet had swollen against Kunal's belly and ached in the long, outrageous way that meant blood. He had finally slept, mouth slightly open, one fist bunched against her ankle.

Boots sounded before she saw anything.

They were light. They came up from the hollow, picking over the glaze, a person who knew the way. Ila lay rigid. She could do nothing else. She had no knife and no feet. Kunal's hand closed on her ankle and she felt the whole length of him wake.

The shape in the cleft mouth was small and broad. A shawl was wound round the head and shoulders. Both hands held something against her chest, a clay pot with a lid pierced in a ring of holes, and out of the holes came a thread of smoke that smelled of juniper.

"Gauri," said Kunal.

His voice broke on it.

She came in. She did not rush. She knelt at the edge of the gravel and set the pot down on the flat stone, the one where the gloves had not stayed. The embers glowed orange through the holes. Ila felt the heat on her face like a hand.

The woman's wrists were wrapped in rag. Her eyes were red-rimmed and very clear. She looked at Kunal's burnt hands, and at the grey on Ila's bare ankle where the sock had come off, and she did not say anything about either of them.

"I came back along the shore in the dark," said Gauri. "The captain's men were gone from the fold by moonset. They took the boy's rope and what was left of their pairs, and they went up the Kuhl side." Her voice was level. It was the voice of someone who had cried everything out of herself in the night and had nothing but the next thing to do. "I sat with him a time."

"Gauri," said Kunal. "I should have—"

"No."

She said it so flatly that he stopped.

Gauri looked at the two of them in the first light, the prince with his shirt open and the surveyor in her coat, and a rope lying between them untied.

"I turned his coat back," she said. "To close his eyes. It was how I found his side, where the shirt was cut." She drew a slow breath. "He was dead before they wrote it."

Nobody moved. In the pot the embers ticked and settled.

"Then who wrote the note?" said Ila.

But Kunal had already gone white to the lips, and the whistle on his chest caught the light.

# Chapter 28: Off a Cold Man

"Raghu's clerk wrote it," said Gauri. "A thin man with ink on his cuff. By lantern, at first light, on a scrap off Dhanu's own tally-hide." She did not look up from the pot. "He read it back twice, to see if it sounded like a man in a hurry."

The whistle lay against Kunal's breastbone on its frayed thong. He could not feel it with his dead fingertips, only its weight. It was small and warm from his skin, and it was not his.

"Tell it," he said.

"There isn't much." Gauri lifted the lid a finger's width and juniper smoke rolled out, sharp and clean. She set Ila's bare foot on a fold of her own shawl, close to the heat, and began to chafe the ankle with her broad thumb without being asked. "They came to the huts at moonrise, while you were still up on the scree, waiting to go into the Eye. Raghu, the boy Pasang, the four from the cornice. They wanted names. Every keeper from the Stair to the Kuhl cut. Every runner. Every house that feeds a pair."

"Mini?" said Ila.

"At my sister's. I had her on the pony and down the valley path before they reached the door. She thinks it was a game." Gauri's thumb did not stop. "She'll ask for her uncle's string."

"And Dhanu."

"Dhanu gave them his ewes." Her mouth moved. It was not a smile. "By name. Oldest first, with their faults. The clerk had written eleven before he understood."

Ila made a small sound. Kunal did not.

"They took him out to the seam," said Gauri. "The black water, where the ice won't hold. They put a rope under his arms and let him down into it, and hauled him up, and asked again. He was not a young man. His heart stopped before the moon cleared the Kuhl shoulder." The thumb went on working the ankle. "That was the first night. Before there was a note to write."

Nobody spoke. The embers ticked.

"They took the whistle off him after," she said. "I watched them do it."

Kunal's hand had come up on its own and closed on the horn. The grey tips felt nothing.

"It was looped over the nail," he said. "By the thong. I took it down and I thought, *he's sent it so I'll know it's him.*" He heard himself breathe. "There was no walnut."

Gauri looked at him then.

"He gave me two. A boy with a red-thread walnut is Dhanu's word, and Dhanu stands behind whatever comes with it. This came with a whistle and no walnut, and I told myself a man with a blade at his back doesn't stop to find his tokens." His voice sounded far off, like someone else's in the next room. "There was no walnut because there was no Dhanu."

"No," said Gauri.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She left them soon after, without ceremony, as she did everything. She set a skin of water by Ila's knee, pushed two flat loaves out of her shawl onto the stone, and went to sit at the cleft mouth with her back to them and her face to the lake.

Kunal stayed where he was. The sum he had refused all night had finally come and sat down in front of him, and he made himself read it.

"A ransom is for a man who is alive," he said. "Raghu never wanted a trade. He wanted a prince on that ice at dusk, standing where he was put. I moved the hour myself. I said, *dusk, so Dhanu doesn't wait another night.*" He turned his dead hand over. "He'd already stopped waiting."

"Kunal—"

"Let me finish, or I won't." He did not look at her. "The paper was never the point. A man who thinks he's bargaining stands still. That's what the sheets were for. I hand them over, I stand where he points, and the glass on the cornice finds my chest while his four finish it." He swallowed. "And you told me to look at his hand."

"I did."

"The fingertips. I couldn't read them. He'd told that boy Dhanu was only hurt, and the boy saw the truth, and Raghu laid a second lie over the first, there on the shore, loud, with his hand on his sword. It was the boy he was lying to. He needed Pasang's hands steady on a rope till it was done, and a boy who knew he was guarding a dead man's body would not have held."

"I knew the shore was lying," said Ila. "I didn't know which part."

"It was all of it." He made himself look up. "If you'd said the sheets were in your coat, I'd have given them over at the water's edge and believed the trade. I'd have stood still. Because I thought they were ash, I had nothing in my hands. So I said *take me,* and when he lifted two fingers I ran. And I ran with you on my arm, calling the ice." He drew a breath that shook. "The two who cut the chord went through the seam. That was the short way, the way a man in a hurry thinks is safe. It would have been mine."

Her hands lay on her knees, in the ember light, with the fingertips pink.

"Your lie cost you two toes," said Kunal, "and it kept me alive. It didn't cost Dhanu his."

"Nothing could have."

"No. I know that now." He looked at the fire. "I couldn't know it then. That's what I'm trying to say. You couldn't have told me."

"I could have said it was a trap."

"You'd have said it with nothing to show, and I'd have told you the sum was mine." He said it plainly, and it hurt less than he had expected, which was its own kind of shame. "You asked me twice about the letter and I made you pay for the asking. You'd have been right and I'd not have heard it. So you went alone, with your hands in your sleeves, and carried the cold, because you couldn't trust me to listen." He stopped. "I wouldn't have listened."

Ila did not say *thank you,* or *it's all right.* Her mouth opened, shut, and opened again.

"No," she said. "I don't think you would."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She pulled her pattu round and sat up straighter, a woman about to hand over a ledger. He watched her do it.

"I said I'd tell you when I could pay it properly," said Ila. "I'm early. I know."

"You don't owe me—"

"I do. I owed it you before the Eye." She laid both hands flat on her knees, palms down, where he could see every finger. "Watch them."

He watched.

"The letter is the queen's, in her own hand, under the archive seal. It names my grandfather. Warden Harish, of the Shilak post. The chronicle says he went out in a night storm to check the cairns and was carried down at the thaw." Her fingers stayed pink. "The letter says the storm was an excuse. He went to the Under-Cairn, beneath the Shilak Cairn, where the Old Bargain is paid. And he paid it. Then he walked out into the snow and took off his gloves and laid them palm to palm on a flat stone, square, with the fingers pointing the way he'd gone."

"The gloves your father spoke of."

"Yes."

"Why does the queen tell you?"

"Because the next spring the crown wrote the Closing Law. So nobody would cross in winter and ask what had happened to a warden at the thaw." Her voice was level. "She wants what's under the cairn found. She wants it read. The crown has never dared. The letter says what is owed is written down there." She breathed. "It says one more thing. *Tell no one Neshar's part.*"

The embers shifted. He had known, he found. He had known since she laid her left hand flat on her coat at the lakeside cairn. He had only not wished to.

"The sheets," he said.

"Not ash. Sewn into the side seam of my pattu, above the hem. All of them. I didn't burn anything." Her hands had not moved. They were still pink. "I'm sorry for the lie. I'm not sorry I told it."

He heard what she did not say, and what she was about to. He saw her mouth shape it, the soft thing, the *it was long ago, it wasn't you, it was his time.*

"Udai," he said, before she could. "My grandfather. He walked a warden up the princes' way in winter, sixty winters ago, to the place under the mountain. And let him pay." He kept his voice flat, because it was that or none. "My family walked your grandfather to his death, Ila, and buried it for sixty winters."

"Kunal. He was—"

"Don't make it smaller. Not for me." He looked at his burnt palms. "Pritam told me the story over supper the week after my father's funeral. Udai showed the way once, to one guest, and was never the same. *The way kills those who share it.* He made it the family rule that night. No prince shows the way to wife, son or friend. I was nine. I thought it was about my grandfather's grief." He laughed, and it was not a laugh. "It was about a grave. The guest died, and my family built a rule round the hole so nobody would look in."

"You didn't know."

"I didn't. I'd have been the next man to keep the rule." He lifted his eyes. "Your hands."

She held them up. The fingertips were warm and clear.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"I said you'd let Dhanu die for your queen's map," said Kunal. "In the voice I use on men I mean to bleed. I knew what it would do when I said it. I was wrong, and I said it anyway."

"I know."

"I'm not asking you to forget it."

"I won't." She did not look away. "I lied to you because I didn't want to argue it out. I could have made you hear me, or tried, with every figure I had. I decided you wouldn't, and decided it for you. It happened to be true. It wasn't mine to find out that way." She said it as she would give a distance. "I'm not saying that to be forgiven."

"Good. I told you I don't forgive you." He turned his ruined hands palm up between them. "I'll amend it. I don't excuse you, and I don't excuse me. I see what you did. I see how you did it, and why, and what it cost."

"That's enough."

"It isn't," said Kunal. "But it's true."

Silence was free. For once he did not hate it. He sat in it and found it held weight.

"The strip on your hem," he said. "The one you offered."

She had it torn off in a breath. She bound his right palm with it, knotting it flat, her warm fingers quick and sure over the broken skin. The wool of the pattu brushed his wrist. When she tied it off she did not let go at once.

"Do you still want to go under the mountain?" he asked.

"Yes."

"With your foot."

"With my foot." She turned her head toward the dark passage, where the blue stones lay faint along the gravel. "My grandfather paid something, and somebody wrote it down. I would rather read it than be told it. I've spent my life drawing what other people could see. I want to read the one thing nobody drew."

"The Meadow Cairn won't wait for Dhanu to be buried."

"No."

"Then I'll take you." He held out his bare right wrist. "Tie it."

She took up the grey wool where it lay across the gravel. Her fingers did the warden's double hitch, two turns, a tuck, a locking half-turn, clean on his wrist, as she had done it at the Meadow Cairn with more hope than she had now. Her end was already tied. It lay between them like a line drawn on a sheet.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Gauri came back when the light had gone a plain grey. She had a lamp under her arm, a skin of oil and a cloth sack that smelled of bread. She set them down in a row, the way a woman sets out a trader's goods, and did not look at the rope.

"I'll stay at the huts," she said. "Till the thaw."

"Gauri," said Kunal.

"I've laid stones on him at the fold. The ground won't take him till the thaw, and he'd not like to be carried." Her eyes were dry. "And someone has to keep the hearth lit for the next pair through. He'd have said so. He'd have said it was no use hating a lamp."

"Let me send someone."

"Send nobody." She said it gently. "Go and do what you've gone for."

He undid the whistle. It took him some time, with the thong stiff and the heel of his hand clumsy against the knot. He held it out.

She looked at it. She did not take it.

"Keep it," said Gauri. "Two long and a short. Blow it when it's clear."

He closed his hand round the horn.

"The pen wall at Ewe's Rest," he said. "Ila showed you where the north side was short. Footed, it'll hold this spring." He had no idea why he was saying it, only that it was the nearest solid thing. "The first pair of lambs born there, whoever's they are. I'll send them to you. The first spring pair."

The rope lay across his wrist. As the words left him it moved against his skin, a low, level hum, as even as a drawn string. It ran up through his arm and into his chest, and across the gravel Ila's head came up. She felt it too.

Gauri heard it. He saw her hear it.

"Then I'll expect them," she said. She picked up the shawl, folded it, and laid it across Ila's feet.

Ila's boots were dry. Gauri had brought those too, with the sack. Kunal pulled them on over the ruined toes while Ila gripped the wall and said nothing, and when he looked up her hands were still on the stone, still pink in the new light.

He lit the lamp from the embers. The flame caught, steadied, and threw their two shadows long down the passage, over the blue stones and the dark beyond them.

"Eighty-one paces to the first turn," said Ila. "Don't count aloud. I'll do it."

"You'll have to forgive me if I count anyway."

"I forgave you," said Ila, "for that, a week ago."

He took the lamp. The rope hummed once more, faintly, between them, and they went back into the cleft.

# Chapter 29: The Under-Cairn

The lamp died in the crawl. After that there was only the tick of meltwater on stone and Kunal's voice, counting.

"Thirty-one," he said, behind her. "Thirty-two."

Ila stopped, crutch planted, breathing. The black pressed against her open eyes like a hand. She had given up trying to see in it a hundred paces back.

"That's my pace you're counting," she said. "Not yours."

"Yes."

"Mine isn't a pace anymore. It's a lurch with a stick."

"I know. I'm counting it as one and a quarter spans, because that's what it measured on the gravel this morning." A pause. "You told me to carry a figure when I didn't have the true one. This is the figure."

She almost laughed. She had been a measurer for eleven winters, and her own stride had gone false under her. Two toes lay dead in her left boot. The right foot had swelled until the laces hurt. Kunal had cut the crutch at the lake huts from a juniper limb, in the grey light, with his knife in his bad hand and Gauri holding the other end. He had padded the fork with his own shirt hem. It dug at her armpit with every step, and she was grateful for it.

She went on. The rope between them lay eight spans slack along the rock and tugged her wrist whenever he lagged.

Behind them, the hollow was a day's grief away. The black water had turned out to be a ledge. With a full lamp and Ila's axe haft sounding ahead, they had found the blue cairn-stones running knee-deep along the left wall, a path half-drowned and invisible by half a lamp's light. At its end the water poured down a six-foot step into a crack, and the way went down with it. Kunal had lowered her on the rope, then climbed after her with his dead fingers hooked in the rock. Neither of them had spoken. After Dhanu there did not seem to be much left to say.

"Fifty," said Kunal. "Fifty-one. The roof's coming down. Duck."

She ducked. Her hair caught on stone. The crawl narrowed until she was on her knees, dragging the crutch, with Kunal's breath loud behind her. For a stretch there was nothing but rock and the cold taste of it.

Then her left hand, reaching, closed on nothing.

"Wait."

"What is it?"

"The wall's gone."

She felt for the edge. It was the end of the crawl. The air in front of her was different, wider and drier. It carried a smell she knew from Taravan's archive, stone and old dust, the smell of a thing kept a long time and not looked at.

And low down, at the very edge of seeing, something was blue.

She thought at first she had imagined it. Kunal's hand found her shoulder. Then, as her eyes learned how little they were being asked to see, the colour came up out of the dark as a stain comes up through paper. Cairn-light. Faint, even, and far dimmer than the stones in the cleft. It lay in a ring on the floor, and she understood that she was looking at a wall of stacked stones, curved, perhaps twice her height at the crown, with no sky above it.

"Light the lamp," she said. "Not yet. Let me look first."

She crawled out onto level floor and stood, her crutch scraping, and let the blue show her what it would.

It was a hollow under a heap. That was the only way she could say it. The mountain had made a vault, and someone, very long ago, had built flat stones up from the floor to meet the roof, so that the whole chamber was a cairn turned inside out. The blue came from between the stones, from the seams. It did not brighten when she looked at it.

"Under-Cairn," said Kunal, very quietly, beside her. He had come out on his knees and stayed there. "I've said that word my whole life as if it were a place in a song."

"It's a place."

"Yes." He didn't get up. "It's a place."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He struck the lamp alight with the flint and the heel of his hand, and the chamber came up gold and small. Ila told him to turn the wick down. She wanted the oil, and she wanted to see how little she could make do with.

There was a door.

It stood at the far curve of the wall, a frame of three squared stones, two uprights and a lintel, set into the cairn-work like a gate into nothing. Behind it was only more dark. Across the lintel and down both uprights ran a carving, deep and clean, though the stone under it had gone the colour of old bone.

Three loops, nested one inside another.

Ila's breath went out of her.

"I know that," she said.

"It's a knot."

"It's *the* knot. Not any knot." She had hobbled to it before she knew she was moving. Her hand, in its glove, hovered over the groove and did not touch. "Meera drew it in the count. She showed me at the drafting table, in Garh Neshar. A bundle in a dead juniper at the Rope-Hut, tied in this pattern. She'd written beside the sketch, *Same as cairn.* She meant the Shilak Cairn. She didn't know it was the same as this."

"Whose bundle was it?"

"Asha's crew's." She spoke slowly. "Asha ties every knot like it. Her grandmother taught her. Meera said she'd never seen her tie another."

Kunal said nothing. She felt him thinking, and was oddly glad of it. Nobody had told either of them that this was a door built for someone's family.

The carving did not stop at the knot. On the left upright, at the height of her shoulder, the loops ran into something else, and she lifted the lamp to it.

Two hands.

They were cut life-size, palm up, side by side, and one rope ran across both, looped once about each wrist, as it did at the Shilak Cairn and on the waystone and in every child's tale of the first crossing. She had seen the cairn carving once, in a dozen rubbings that were all wrong. She had never seen it clean.

One hand was broad. It was a worker's hand, square across the knuckles and thick in the thumb. The other lay beside it, long and narrow, with fingers that tapered.

"Brothers in arms," said Kunal. His voice was odd. "That's the crown's line."

"Hold the lamp."

He took it. She dug in her coat for her pocket rule and the stub of pencil. She had no table, so she did what she had done at nineteen on an abandoned wall in Taravan. She laid her own bare hand over the narrow one, pale against the old stone, and marked with the pencil where her fingers ended.

"Longer than mine," she said. "By the nail's width. And thin through the palm. Compare."

He set his right hand, bandaged, against the broad one, with the lamp tilted. It fitted with room to spare.

"A man's," she said. "That one. The warden's. Saro." She lifted her own. "And I have long hands for a woman, Kunal. I get them from my mother. This is not a boy's hand. The wrist's wrong, the taper's wrong, the thumb sits too low. It's a woman's."

"Hemi."

"Hemi was a woman." She heard it come out of her like a measurement, flat and certain. "Three hundred winters of *two brothers in arms,* and she's carved here in her own hand."

Neither of them moved. The hum of the stones was very low. She could not have said if it was in her ears or in the rope.

Under the hands, in a single line, ran script. It was old and square-cut, with letters she had seen only in margins of route-songs.

"Can you read it?"

Kunal brought the lamp down. His lips moved.

"Some." He did not look at her. "My grandfather taught the route-songs in this hand. It's the same alphabet, with a thousand years of grandmothers' mistakes in it." He traced under the first word without touching, as she had. "'*Carried.*' That's plain. Then a word that's *till*, or *until*, one of the old forms. Then '*no.*'" He frowned. "Then the stone is worn. Something the shape of a hook, and a curl. Hands have been laid on this end more than any other part."

"Worn by what?"

"Fingers. Years of fingers." He lifted the lamp until the carved line glittered. "*Carried till no—* and then nothing I can say."

*Carried.* She thought of the Ladder's route-song, the old hill word for seen across by the hand. She thought of her grandfather's chronicle, *carried down at the thaw.* The same word. One song used it for the living and the post's book used it for the dead, and she had held both in her head for a month without once laying them side by side.

*Carried till no.*

"A promise," she said, almost to herself. "Not a death. A promise has a term. You carry someone till a thing comes. *Till no something.* What would it end at?"

"I don't know."

"Don't guess."

"I'm not." His hand had begun to shake, a little. He put the lamp on the floor to hide it. "I won't guess at that."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She took the rubbing with the paper from her field book, laid flat across the line and held with the heel of her glove. Her pencil leads snapped in the cold. Kunal had been carrying them against his chest since the Hollow Moon, and without being asked he took a fresh one out from inside his shirt, warm, and put it in her fingers. She said nothing. She went over the line with the side of the lead, slow, from the first letter to the worn hook. The words came up white out of the grey, and where the hook had been there was only a smear.

It was a poor rubbing at the end, and she knew it. She pressed harder. The paper tore a little. She stopped.

"Leave it," said Kunal. "We'll know the shape. We'll find someone who can read it."

"Who?"

"I don't know." He lifted the lamp again. "Come and see the rest. The roof is lower here."

There was more. She had been so taken by the door that she hadn't seen what ran beside it, along the curve of the wall to the right. A column of scratched marks, from the height of her shoulder down to her knee.

She counted them, because counting was what her hands knew. Fourteen. Each had been cut by a different hand, in a different year, some deep and clean, some shallow and quick, as if the carver had been in a hurry or afraid. Most were plain strokes. A few had small signs beside them, a knot, a goat's head, a curl that might have been a wave. They went down the stone in the order of their ages. The lowest was a different sort of mark altogether.

It was a goat, cut neat and square. Beneath it, in the same careful letters as the door's line, was a name.

She did not need Kunal to read it. She had written it herself in the margin of her first field book, at nineteen, to see what it looked like.

*Harish.*

She stood in front of it a long time. The lamp made the letters swim. It was a stranger's hand, the cutting of a man who had been taught his letters properly and had not expected to use them like this. Her father had said, once, over the clock, that his own father used to sign his reports with a flourish on the H.

There was no flourish. The H was plain, and very straight.
