"Ila," said Kunal.

"Don't." Her voice was steady, and she hated that. "I'm all right. I'm looking."

"I know you are."

Her glove touched the stone for the first time. It touched the goat, not the name. She had half expected warmth, or cold. It was only stone. She had come all this way to find it and it was only stone, and her grandfather had once stood where she stood and cut it into the wall with a knife.

"There's a shelf," said Kunal, very gently, behind her. "Here."

It was waist-high, a flat slab laid across two stacked piers, close beside the marks. It ran back into the dark of the vault. She turned to it with the lamp.

Gloves.

They had rotted. Wool and leather and a few scraps of felt, gone brown and black and the colour of earth, their shapes slumped but their order unmistakable. Each pair lay one glove folded over the other, palms together, the fingers set square to the edge of the slab. Then a gap, the width of a hand. Then another pair. Then another.

She counted them. She could not have stopped herself.

"Thirteen," she said.

"There are fourteen marks."

"Thirteen pairs." She was seeing it as it came. "Every payer folded his gloves, as the story says. He took them off, and he set them down. They're here because they came *here.*" She turned the lamp slowly to the empty gap at the end of the shelf, where the stone was clean. "Harish's aren't. His were found on a hillside, above the third bend."

"He paid here."

"And walked out. He carried his own gloves to the stone where they found him. Not here." Her voice thinned. "He was the last of them. He knew it was the last, and he wanted somebody to find them."

The vault was very quiet. Meltwater ticked on stone somewhere out of sight.

Kunal pinched the lamp out.

She almost cried out. The dark dropped on her like a coat. Only the cairn-light was left, faint and blue at the seams of the wall, hardly enough to show her where her own hand was.

"Oil," he said. "We have half a skin. I want us to see this by the only light it was ever meant to be seen by."

"That's not a reason."

"It's one reason." A breath. "Sit. Your foot."

She let him help her down on the cold floor with her back against the cairn-work, the crutch laid across her knees. He sat beside her, near enough that his shoulder pressed hers. The rope between their wrists lay slack and humming. She held very still.

It was a peculiar thing to be so afraid and so unfrightened. She had known it on the ice, and now she knew it in the dark under the whole weight of the mountain, with a dead man's stone in front of her and a living one beside her.

"Ila."

"Yes."

"I love you."

She did not move.

It was not said as a question. He said it as he gave his distances, in the same level voice he had used on the Stair when he told her the stone was loose. And it came out of the dark where she could not see him, where she could not look at his hands.

That was the point, she understood slowly. He had put out the lamp. On the Mirror, at the Eye, even on the ice, there had been a place to look, a pair of fingers whitening or not. For a week she had read this man by the colour of his hands, because it was the one honest instrument she had, and he knew it. He had chosen the one room in the mountain where she could not use it.

*He's asking me to take it on his word.*

It was the most frightening thing anyone had ever done to her.

The rope hummed against her wrist, low and even. It was not a proof. It was not even a promise. It was only the sound a thing made when it was true, and she could not have said whether it was the rope or the cairn or her own blood.

She put out her hand into the dark and found his.

She did not take hold of the wrist, where she might have felt the cold. She took his hands, both of them, the burnt palms in their strips of her own shirt, and the dead tips of the right, grey and wrong under the cloth. They were cool. They were not frozen. She held them in her lap on top of the crutch.

"I know what this is," she said. "Touch is how the cold goes over. If it were a lie, I'd feel it, and I'd know. You know that."

"I know."

"It isn't to check." She meant it to come out firm. It came out less steady. "I'm not checking. I'm holding them. That's all this is. I'm holding your hands, Kunal."

His fingers closed on hers, as far as they could.

"I'm not counting," said Ila.

He let out a breath. She felt it go through his whole arm.

They sat in the dark under the cairn, and the meltwater ticked, and the blue stayed even in its seams. She did not say what she had not yet got the word for. It would keep. She had a pencil in her coat and a rubbing with a torn end and a grandfather's name, and for the first time since the Hollow Moon she was not afraid of what came next.

Then the dark behind them changed.

A paleness showed at the mouth of the crawl, low down, the wrong colour, yellow and not blue. It moved. It brightened and slid along the wall of the passage they had come through, and she heard, very far off and then not far at all, the scrape of a boot on stone.

Kunal's hands went rigid in hers.

Lamplight was coming up the crawl behind them, and somebody was carrying it.

# Chapter 30: Two Hands

The light came up the crawl on its knees.

Kunal was on his feet before he had decided anything. He put himself between the passage mouth and Ila, and he got his knife out with the flat of his palm, because his fingertips could no longer be trusted to close on a hilt.

Raghu came out first, stooped under the rock, a lantern low in his left hand and a long knife in his right. His beard was stiff with ice. Behind him came Pasang, the brass bell on his shoulder dull with wet, and between their wrists the grey rope ran taut as a plumb line.

"Highness." Raghu straightened and let his eyes go round the vault: the door, the shelf of rotted gloves, the fourteen marks. They went greedy, then flat. "So that was what the nine holes were for."

"You followed the lamp," said Ila, from behind Kunal's shoulder.

"I followed a prince with a bad hand and a woman on a stick. A child could have done it." The knife swung until it pointed at her coat. "Two things. The sheets you offered me at dusk, and a way out that isn't the way I came. I've no wish to crawl through the Drum water again."

"You never wanted the sheets," Kunal said. "You wanted me on the ice, standing where I was put. Dhanu was dead before you wrote the note."

"Dhanu is at home with his goats."

Kunal looked past the blade to the boy. Pasang was twenty, with a soldier's cropped hair and a child's mouth, and he was staring at the carved door rather than at anyone's face.

"Pasang. You're roped to him by the jogini's own rule. He can lie to me for nothing. He can lie to my keeper's widow, to the whole of Neshar, for nothing. He can't lie to you." Kunal heard how it sounded, a blade picked up and turned. He had used the voice on men he meant to bleed. This time the blade was only true. "So ask him yourself. Not me. Ask whether the limping man went home. If he's nothing to hide, he'll say yes. If he says nothing, that answers you as well."

"A cornered prince's trick," said Raghu.

Pasang's mouth worked. He turned his head slowly, the way a man turns toward a sound he does not want to place. "Captain," he said. "Did he go home?"

Raghu knew the rule. Kunal watched him know it. Silence was free, and silence would tell the boy everything.

"He went home," said Raghu. "Walking. Before the moon was down."

The knife hand stayed steady, and the fingertips around the hilt went the colour of tallow. Not deep. The boy already half knew, so the lie bought little and cost little. It was white enough.

Pasang looked at the hand. Then, slowly, at his own.

"He did that on the shore," said Ila. Her voice was dry and exact, the voice she used for distances. "When he said it loud, with his hand on his sword. I saw the tips whiten from the prince's arms and told him, and he couldn't read it. You were two paces away. You weren't looking at the hand. You were looking at what lay by the peg." She did not raise her voice. "Dhanu died the first night. His wife laid stones over him at the fold. You held a rope for a dead man."

"I thought he was sleeping," said Pasang. It came out of him small. Then, louder: "You said the keepers go free. Every one, when we had the sheets. On the ice, to my face."

Raghu said nothing. His mouth stayed shut. The knife stayed in his fist, and the tips around the hilt stayed tallow through the whole long silence, though no new lie had been told. He did not look at the boy.

"Captain." Pasang's voice cracked. "They go free. Say it."

Raghu looked at the door. He did not speak. Kunal watched the boy understand what the silence told him.

Pasang sat down. He did it slowly, on the cold floor, the rope across his knees and the bell ringing once against stone.

"Get up," said Raghu.

"No."

"Get up, or by the mountain—" He hauled. Pasang did not move. Raghu's boots slid a step, two, to the end of the grey rope, and Kunal saw the cold take him: his breath smoked, his shoulders drew in. He could not go on toward the door, or back to the crawl, without the boy. He could not cross the length of a rope alone. "Cut it."

"Cutting ends nothing," said Kunal. "You know that. Every child in the Kuhl knows that."

Raghu looked once at the door, and once at the pale seam of Ila's coat, where the folded rubbing showed through the cloth. Then he threw himself at her, because it was the only direction left to him.

It was not far. Kunal had spent all winter watching men lunge on ice. He stepped in under the knife arm, caught the wrist with the heel of his dead hand, and drove it against the cairn-work with his forearm across the elbow. The blade rang on the floor. Ila's crutch hooked it away into the dark. Raghu sagged, gasping, his white fingers clutching at nothing, and Kunal held him there against the stone until the fight left him.

"Pasang," he said, without turning. "I can't free a keeper from under a mountain. I won't promise what I can't do. I can walk you into daylight, with your life. I can tell the council you put the rope down in this hole. And I'll ask you where they're held, and write your name under the answer."

The boy was quiet a long moment.

"That's more than he gave me," he said, and stood.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Beyond the door there was a stair.

It was no more than a slope of laid stones, climbing, with blue seams along the left wall and a crack in the roof where meltwater ticked down in the lamplight. Kunal went first with Ila's hand on his shoulder, the rubbing safe in her coat. Raghu came behind with his own belt around his wrists, Pasang at the end of the rope, and nobody spoke for a long time.

The stair ended at a gap between two slabs. Light came through it, grey and thin. Kunal put his shoulder to the slab and it moved. He came up on his knees into cold air, and a ring of flat stones stood round him, chest high, a hum rising from every one.

It was dawn at the Shilak Cairn. Below it the roofs of Kothi Shilak lay under a skin of snow, and goat bells were going somewhere in the village.

Four wardens stood inside the ring, in grey, and in front of them, one hand flat on the cairn, stood a tall man Kunal knew from a drafting table and a guest-house in midwinter. Rohan had a beard of three days. He had plainly stood this watch more than once.

For three nights the Shilak Cairn had hummed from under the ground with no one inside the ring. Gopal had sent for Rohan, and Rohan had stood the dawn watch ever since, waiting to see who the mountain would give back.

"Captain," said Kunal, with his hands where they could be seen.

"Prince." Rohan's eyes went over him, over Raghu, over Pasang on the rope's end, and stopped on his sister coming up through the earth on a juniper crutch. "Gopal. Take that man. Hold the boy at the rope's length, and no closer. He walks with us as a witness."

They did it quickly. Raghu said nothing. When he went by, Kunal saw that his fingertips were still white.

Ila crossed the snow without a word. She stood in front of her brother, and she did not tell him what she had found. Kunal saw her choose it. Her free hand went to the back of his collar, where the queen's seal had once been sewn into the coat, and Rohan reached up and covered it with his own. He looked down at her foot. He looked up at her face. Neither of them spoke. She laid her forehead against his shoulder for the count of three, and then she stepped back and was a surveyor again.

Rohan nodded once at Kunal. It was a clerk's nod over a line that balanced. Kunal gave it back.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The salt house was warm.

It was the first warmth that was not the lamp and a stranger's embers. Bindu's people had lit the chulha in the top room, and the heat came off the clay and went into the beams, and the whole room smelled of salt dust and wool and apricot smoke. Someone had left a basin of hot water by the hearth. Ila's feet were out of her boots and wrapped in clean linen, and she sat on the sleeping shelf with her back to the wall and the grey rope lying eight spans slack across the boards.

Kunal sat on the floor facing her, because he did not trust himself higher.

Her coat lay folded beside her. Her hand went to it before she spoke, and rested on the stiff fold inside, the rubbing with its torn edge and its worn last word that neither of them could read. She did not take it out. She let her hand lie there a moment, checking that it had come up the mountain with her. Then she took the hand away and turned both palms up.

"Look at my hands," said Ila.

He did. They lay open on her knees in the lamplight, flushed to the nail, with the knuckles scraped from the crawl.

"I like you," she said.

"Is that all?"

"It's what I can measure to the hair." She did not smile. "You carry my pencil leads against your chest so they won't snap. You said *forty-one* out loud on the Stair, and you didn't argue the half-step. You make me laugh when I'm furious, which no one has managed since I was nine. I like you, Kunal." She turned her hands over. "On the Neshar road, the first night, I stood eight spans and a bit from you, and I let the rope chill my wrists to the elbow before I'd follow you east. Look at them now."

They were warm. He could see it. The flush of blood in them was easy and slow, as if they had never been anything else.

"I love you," she said.

The rope did not climb. It lay along the boards and gave a low, even note, the sound of a string drawn to its length, and he felt it come up through his wrist and settle in his chest.

"You said it in the dark," said Ila. "I wanted to say it where you could see my hands."

"I didn't need to see them."

"I know. That's why I wanted you to." She drew breath, and he saw the figures go behind her eyes, the way they did when she committed something to a sheet. "There's a third thing, and it's a promise. I'll give Neshar the same map I give the queen. The same lines. The same names. The same hour."

The note in the rope held. It did not rise.

"The Meadow Cairn runs in a few days," said Kunal. His voice was not as steady as he wanted. "After that nothing makes you keep it. Nothing makes either of us keep anything."

"I know the date. I've kept the thaw dates eleven winters." She looked at him over the rope. "I'm not saying it for the mountain."

He had nothing clever to say. He said the plain thing. "Then you'll have the names. All of them, in the hill words. Even the ones that make a fool of me."

The rope hummed once more, even and soft.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He crossed the boards on his knees and she met him halfway, and neither of them let the other go for some time.

She was thirty-one and he was thirty-three, and for a month of days they had been tied together in every way but this. Her mouth tasted of salt tea and apricot. He had kissed women in courts, with calculation. Now he could not calculate anything. When she put her hands into his hair and her bandaged feet came up off the floor, he stopped, because he had seen how she walked.

"Your feet."

"Are on a folded blanket. Put it there." She was laughing, breathless. "Kunal. I'll tell you if anything hurts. I'll tell you the size of the hurt, in spans."

He built her a nest out of the pattu and the blanket, so that her heels lay clear of the boards, and he undid her shirt with his palms because his fingers could not feel the buttons. She saw it. She took his right hand in hers, with its dead grey tips, and laid it against her breast.

"Can you feel that?"

"The heat. Not the shape."

"Then use your mouth," said Ila, "and I'll tell you the shape."

She did. It was a precise, unhurried instruction, given in the measured voice she used on the Stair, and it undid him faster than any caress. She told him where to press and where to wait. When he drew her breast into his mouth she said *there* and then said nothing at all. His palm went down her ribs, over the white scar, over her hip. She gasped when he found her with the flat of his thumb, and she took his wrist and held it where she wanted it.

She turned them herself, with her feet off the floor. She came down over him slowly, her knees on the blanket and her hands braced on his chest, and when she took him in they both went still. He felt the heat of her through every inch of his body that could feel anything. His hands held her hips, heel and palm, steadying her.

"Is it—"

"Don't ask me." Her forehead came down to his. "Look at my hands."

They were braced on his shoulders, warm as bread. He watched them while she moved. He watched them while she shook, and cried out against his neck, and went on moving until he lost the thread of his own name. Then he held her so tight she laughed, and afterwards she lay along him with her ruined foot hooked over his shin and her cheek on his heart, and the chulha ticked.

"After the thaw," she said, after a long time. "Where do you sleep?"

"Garh Neshar. The tower, thirty-nine steps. Forty, you'll say."

"Forty. And in spring?"

"At Ewe's Rest, for the lambing. The north wall needs checking." He laughed, quietly, into her hair. "You could check it."

"I'd want a new pace." She was half asleep. "Mine's gone wrong. I'll have to measure myself again."

He had never in his life heard anyone talk about the thaw as a place a person might live. The rope lay across the blanket, humming almost too low to hear, and he did not look at it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The knock was two knuckles, patient.

Kunal woke with the sun on the boards and Ila's hair across his mouth. The door opened a hand's breadth. Meera stood in it, in her guide's brown with the new licence cord at her belt, the post's brown seal and the council's blue hanging on the cord, and a pardon wax beside them with a thread of red in it. She had the count under her right arm. Her left hand, in its felted glove cut for a thumb and two fingers, rested on the latch.

She looked at the two of them under the one blanket, at the grey rope across the floor, at Ila's bandaged feet, and she did not remark on any of it.

"Day thirty-two," said Meera. "Your meadow's running soon."

# Chapter 31: The Shared Map

On the thirty-fifth morning the rope went quiet.

Ila knew it before she woke. The hum had lain under her wrist for five weeks, so low she had stopped hearing it, the way a miller stops hearing the wheel. Now there was only the chulha ticking and Kunal's breath beside her. She lay with her eyes shut and counted to forty. Nothing came back.

"It's gone," she said.

"I know." He was already awake. "It went at moonset. I didn't want to be the one to say."

They went out into the yard in their boots, without their coats, like children told it was a holiday. The rope was a plain grey cord on the boards. Ila picked it up and found it weighed what wool weighs.

The herders' fold stood at the foot of the cairn path, a dry-stone ring with its gate hung on a loop of hide. She sent him to it. Sixteen spans by her reckoning, which meant twice the rope, which meant the place where the mountain had once taken the path away from both of them.

Kunal walked out on the packed snow, upright and unhurried. At eight spans he did not slow. At twelve he glanced back at her, and she saw him wait for the cold. At sixteen he stood with one dead-tipped hand on the fold wall and breathed out a long white cloud.

"Nothing," he called. "Warm as the salt-house wall."

"Say something false."

"What?"

"Anything. Something small. I want to see the hand."

He thought. "I have never once wanted to turn back."

His fingertips stayed grey, as they had been since the Eye. Grey was all they would ever be. She could not read a lie in them. She could not read a thing in them, and it came to her that this was what he had meant, in the dark under the cairn, when he put out the lamp.

"Come back," she said.

He came back at his own pace, and she did not count it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They worked twelve days in the salt house top room.

Bindu's people had cleared a long table of rope and set it under the south window. Ila pinned two sheets side by side, the same size and the same weight of paper, ruled by the same rule. She drew a line on one. Kunal watched her. Then she drew the same line on the other.

It was slow, and she had never been so glad of slow work. Every peak she had measured, she drew twice. Every bearing from the field book had to match on both sheets to the hair. Where one drifted, she scraped it and began again.

Then Kunal gave her the names.

He did it from the stool beside her, in the hill speech, and she wrote each one as he said it, in the old square hand he had taught her, and tidied not a single word. The black notched pinnacle north of the lambing wall was Old Woman's Tooth. The long seam at the Drum's cliff foot, where the ice rang, was Where the Drum Answers. The crag she had named for a healer in a Ranu storehouse was the Widow's Knuckle, for the hand a widow laid on the rock while she waited. She had loved naming it. She wrote *Widow's Knuckle* in its place and felt something give in her chest, a small thing, like a stitch coming out.

Nimmi's Knuckle stayed in her field book, in pencil, in the margin where nobody looked.

On the fifth day Gauri's word came by a bound pair of runners from the Kuhl side. It was short. Kunal read it aloud and had to stop twice. The hamlets had talked. They wanted the Widow's Ladder on the map. The Long Braid, the hundred and sixty steps, the song. They had kept it from people below for three hundred winters, and they would rather the crown learn it from a surveyor who had climbed it than from a spy who had not.

"They're sure?" Ila said.

"Gauri says the old women voted. She says it took an afternoon and one shouting match." He folded the paper. "They'd rather it was drawn true."

She drew it true. Four pitches, three ledges, the leaning pitch with its fifty-seven steps. She wrote *Widow's Ladder* in the hill hand beside the crown's word, because both were its names. Her pencil did not shake. It was only a line, and it was the line she had been longest in coming to.

On the ninth day one crag was left with no name at all.

It stood east of the Drum, a low grey tooth above the shore, and Kunal had walked past it all his life without a word for it. He sat looking at the sheet a long while.

"You give it one," he said. "That's the habit, isn't it?"

"I'm out of the habit."

"Then I'm asking you back into it. Once." He did not look up. "It's above the fold where he laid the nets."

She understood. She wrote it small, in the hill hand, on both sheets, and he leaned over to watch the letters come.

*Dhanu's Rest.*

Neither of them spoke for some time. Then Kunal put his hand flat on the table, beside hers, not touching.

That afternoon she went out to re-measure her pace.

The yard was fifty spans end to end, laid out with the chalk line. Her left foot was missing two toes and the right still ached from the swelling. She walked the line with her crutch under her arm, three times, and wrote the figures on her sleeve. One and three-quarter spans. Not two and a half. Her father's chalk line and the brass rule at Taravan, and twelve years since the brass rule, had all gone short in a single winter.

Kunal walked beside her the whole way, counting under his breath. When she lurched, he lurched. When she stopped to write, he stopped.

"One and three-quarters," she said. "It'll change again when the foot mends."

"Then measure it again."

"I will. But I'm not going to chain a hundred miles of mountain in a pace that changes with my feet." She looked at the sheet in her hand, then up at the rope coiled over the yard post. "Everyone in the hills knows a rope. Eight spans of the guide's arm. It's a unit a child can carry in her head."

"You want to put a rope on the scale bar."

"I want to give it both. Spans for the crown. Ropes for the hills. Same bar, same length." She wrote it on her sleeve, and under it, *Ila's pace, after.* "Nobody can say which of us the map is for."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She told Rohan on the thirteenth evening, in the post room.

It was a bare room with a stove, a table scarred by forty winters of wardens' knives, and a shelf of post books. He sat at the table with his coat open. In front of him, on the wood, lay Dilan's rope end, the clean-cut end toward him. He had not touched it since she came in.

She laid the rubbing on the table. She did not explain it. She told him in order, as she would report a survey: the Under-Cairn, the door, the fourteen marks, the shelf of gloves. The last name, cut in a plain hand with no flourish on the H. The queen's letter, which she had not opened till the Glass Lakes. What the letter said.

"Grandfather went in to be the payment," she said. "Prince Udai took him. He walked in on his own feet, Rohan. The thing in the chronicle, *carried down at the thaw*, is true, but not the way we were told. He walked out again. He laid his gloves on a stone on the hillside where we'd find them. He knew somebody would."

Rohan did not move. His hand came out at last and settled over the grey wool, not lifting it.

"The gloves," he said. "Father told me, once, when he was drinking. One on the other. Square to the stone." He drew breath. "I spent twelve years thinking it was the last tidy thing a cold man did."

"It was the last tidy thing a brave one did."

"The law was written the next spring." He said it to the rope. "For him. Every hanging I signed was for him."

"For the debt they were hiding. Not for him."

"No." He looked up, and his face was not one she had often seen. "I'd have liked to know that before I signed it."

She did not touch him. She had long ago learned that her brother wanted a witness, not a hand. She sat across from him while the stove ticked, and when he spoke again it was only to say, "Tell Meera. She'll want to write him down."

Meera was in the doorway. Ila had not heard her come. She had the count open on her left forearm, the felted glove steadying the page, and a pen in her right.

"Harish," said Meera, to no one in particular. "Warden. Went in sixty-one winters back, and came out." She wrote it. "That's one I carried and didn't know."

"You didn't carry him," said Rohan.

"Somebody did." Meera blotted the line with her sleeve. "It says *carried* in the song. I'm counting it."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The thaw came to Shilak on the fifty-sixth day, three weeks to the day behind the Meadow Cairn's, a thin bright thread of water running down the cairn's south face while Gopal watched with his hat in his hand.

The queen came by litter from Taravan three days after. It took eight bearers and two mules. Sarala was sixty-two, and the winter had not been kind. She was small in the cushions, with a thin grey face and a stick laid across her knees, and when she climbed out at the foot of the cairn path she did it without help and without hurry. Her eyes went over the ring of stones and the crowd, then found Ila at once. She said nothing.

The hill council had come over the Long Saddle as bound pairs, one rope to a pair, each pair led by one of Meera's crew. Roop went first with Old Bhim, and Chand came behind with a woman from the upper terraces. Meera brought up the last, bound to a farmer from the Kuhl, with the licence cord swinging at her belt. They stood on the Neshar side of the ring in their blue-corded wool, and the queen's courtiers stood on the other, in slate, and between them the cairn hummed.

Ila had a table set inside the ring. Two chairs, two copies, one pen.

She was very calm, which she had not expected. She had gone over her oath so many times that the words no longer meant anything. *I swear to show the crown every way, and no other. I swear to draw for the crown alone.* She had sworn it at nineteen, in the Taravan office, with a clock on the wall.

"Majesty," she said. "Old Bhim. This is the map of the high passes of Hemkot and Neshar. There are two copies. They are the same."

She took off her gloves.

She laid them on the table, one on the other, palms together. It was not meant for anyone but herself. Kunal, beside her, glanced at them and then at her face, and she saw him understand.

"My hands are warm," she said, and held them out where the whole ring could see. "That tells you nothing. The bond ended on the thirty-fifth day. Nothing I say here can be checked by the mountain. So you will have to check it on the paper."

She dipped the pen and signed the first copy. *Ila, surveyor.* Under it Kunal signed, left-handed, in his square hill script. *Kunal of Neshar.* The names went on both sheets, in two hands, over every peak and way and hidden stair.

She laid the sheets before them, one before each, at the same moment. The queen's bearers set the paper down in front of Sarala. Old Bhim took his in both hands, as if it might spill.

The silence was long. A boy near the back coughed.

"You swore the crown's work was the crown's," said the queen, quite quietly.

"I did."

"You broke it."

"Before you. On purpose. So you could see it done." Ila did not look away. "I kept a promise I made to him, and no stone in the mountain made me. I'd rather break the oath I swore than the one I chose."

Sarala's mouth thinned. She looked at the sheet. Her eyes went down it the way an archivist's eyes do, from the peaks to the lines to the names, and Ila watched them stop at *Widow's Ladder* and *Dhanu's Rest* and go on.

"We are angry," said the queen. "Understand that. We are very angry. A surveyor's oath is the one thing a crown can buy that does not need watching." She laid her palm on the sheet. "We are keeping the map."

She did not lift her hand.

"And we will be paid for the oath. The Chief Surveyor's commission goes to another, today, and your name comes off the crown roll. A surveyor who signs for two masters will not draw for one of ours again."

It was the thing Ila had wanted since she was nineteen. She had measured every pass of two kingdoms for it. It left her quite cleanly, like a tooth.

"Then the commission is the price of the map," she said. "Take it."

Kunal stepped forward.

He had a petition, written in his own square hand, and he read it aloud without once looking down. Self-rule for Neshar under the crown, by its own council. No garrison in any hill village, now or in any year. A seat for Neshar at the council in Taravan. And every way on the map, as drawn, open to the queen's eyes and the council's alike, so that neither could keep from the other what both had been given.

He did not soften it. Ila saw his dead fingers curl at his side.

"Neshar asks nothing it cannot bear to see written," he finished. "We have kept our ways for three hundred winters because we believed whoever knew them would own us. I was taught that. I believed it." He drew a breath. "I'd rather be owned by no one and known by everyone."

The queen did not answer at once. Her gaze moved from his face to Ila's, to Rohan at the rear with his arms folded, to Meera by the cairn with her left hand tucked in its glove.

"Granted," said Sarala. "All four." She said it dryly, as one signs a pardon. "The crown has already been paid for them. Gopal. Fetch the clerk."

The ring let out its breath. Old Bhim was laughing and crying, and a woman near the back began a hill song and was shushed and began it again. Kunal stood with his hand on the table and his head bowed, and Ila put her palm between his shoulders and left it there, in front of everyone, for as long as she liked.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The queen sent for her at dusk.

It was a small room off the salt house yard, with a brazier and a stool and a lamp. The stick lay across Sarala's knees. She was alone, and she had dropped the plural. The courtier's voice had gone out of her and left an old woman's.

"You are the first surveyor in forty years who has made me regret a commission," she said. "And the first I would have kept."

"I know what it cost you to send the letter."

"You know very little." There was no heat in it. "I read the archive the day I was crowned. I read his name on its list, and I have never dared read what is written under the mountain. Thirty-one winters, I let the law say what I could not. You went and looked." She lifted her eyes. "You have a rubbing."

Ila's hand went to the hem of her coat before she knew it had moved.

"I would like it," said the queen. "Alone, if you please. Before anyone else reads it."

Ila's hand stayed on the hem. Under the wool the paper lay stiff, with its torn edge and its last word that no one had read. She had told Kiran she would send to him first, before anyone, if she found where the Bargain was paid. And Kunal had crawled the dark beside her on dead hands, and carried her pencil leads against his chest, and she had signed his name beside hers an hour ago in front of two courts.

Whichever way she moved, she would be breaking faith with one of them.

# Chapter 32: The Winter Oath

"Not alone," Ila had told the queen at the door of the little room. "I promised two people they would hear it first. I won't read it to one and tell the others after."

Sarala had studied her, with the lamp low and the stick across her knees. Then she had said, "At the cairn, then. At dusk tomorrow. We would not read a thing like that under a roof."

So the next dusk found Ila at the Shilak Cairn an hour early, with the rubbing in her coat and her crutch sunk in the wet snow, and Kunal beside her.

The ring was quiet. Four days of thaw had left the ground below it black and running, and the stones stood in their chest-high circle with water ticking off their shoulders. Someone had tied Badami's big bell to the east side. It rang once, in the wind, and was still.

"I left the rope in the salt house," said Ila.

"I saw."

"Eight spans of undyed wool from Lata's house in Taravan, and I put it in a chest." She tried to laugh and it came out thin. "I've never been this frightened of a plain ring of stones."

"You've crossed the Eye."

"The Eye told me what it wanted. This doesn't."

He turned to face her. The light was going the colour of slate, with a bar of rose along the Saddle's lip. His right hand hung at his side, its tips grey. She had stopped seeing it as a wound some time ago. Now it was only his.

"There's nothing to hold us to it," he said. "You understand that. The bond's over. If we say it here, it's only us saying it, and if either of us breaks it, nothing happens at all."

"I know."

"I want to say it anyway."

"So do I." She stepped into the ring. The crutch left a hole in the snow. "You first. You've the worse habit."

He did not smile. He put his left palm flat on the cairn and said it the way he said hill names, plainly, as if the thing were already so.

"I, Kunal of Neshar, will keep no way from you. Every route I know, and every one I learn, I'll put in your hand before I put it in my own head."

She put her hand over his, on the stone. It was cold and slick and entirely ordinary.

"I, Ila, will keep no line from you. I won't draw a thing of yours without your name beside it. And I won't tell you a number I don't believe, not to spare you, not to spare myself."

"Every winter," said Kunal.

"We walk it together," said Ila.

Nothing happened.

She had known nothing would. She listened anyway, with her whole body, for the hum, for the low even note that had lain under her wrist for thirty-five days. She would have taken the ringing of a lie, even. There was only the drip of meltwater, the far-off bell, and a boy somewhere shouting at a goat.

The stones had not heard them, or had heard and did not care. The promise lay in the air between them with no mountain behind it. It was only theirs.

Ila was crying a little, and was not ashamed of it.

"It didn't hum," she said.

"No."

"I wanted it to."

"I know." He had a hand inside his coat. "I wanted the stones to say it was true. Then I thought, I've never once believed a thing because a stone said so. Not until this winter." He drew out a walnut with a thread of red wool wound twice round it and knotted. "Dhanu gave me two of these, for runners. I never spent either. Mini's string was a third one, plain, and I dressed it myself. Gauri said one of the two belongs to you."

Her throat closed. "Why me?"

"She said you were somebody's runner now, and should wear the token. She said he would have wanted it carried, not kept."

He tried to tie it to her surveyor's chain. His right fingers could not pinch the thread, so he lowered his head, took the end in his teeth, and worked the knot with his teeth and the heel of his hand. It took him a long time. She stood still for all of it. The chain was the short brass one at her belt that had measured every pass she had ever drawn, and when he was done the walnut hung from its last link, a small brown thing with a bright red knot.

She closed her hand round it.

"It'll knock against my rule," she said.

"Then I'll know when you're coming."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The queen came up the path at full dusk, on foot, with Gopal on one side and Meera on the other. Rohan walked behind with his hands clasped at his back. Sarala refused the stick for the last twenty paces and Gopal looked at the ground.

Kiran came last.

Ila had sent word down to the salt house the day the council crossed. He had come up from there with Roop's crew, a long thin shape in a blanket coat with his torn ear showing under the cap. At the cairn's edge he stopped outside the stones and eyed the ring the way he eyed a rope he had no wish to be tied to.

"Inside," Ila said. "You, too. It's yours as much as anyone's."

He stepped in.

Bindu was already there, with a lantern. Ila had asked for her. She stood with her hands in her sleeves and her eyes on the ground, a woman who was loud with rope and quiet with everyone else, and she looked up only when Ila spread the paper on the flat slab at the cairn's heart.

It was a rubbing of a stone Ila had touched for an hour in the dark. The graphite had gone soft in the damp. Kunal laid his lantern beside it, and Ila put her finger under the top line.

"Here," she said. "The old script. I can't read it. He can."

"Not all of it." Kunal bent. "The hill hands learn it in the cradle, for the songs." He followed the strokes. "Carried. Carried till no." His finger stopped. "And then the stone's worn and the lead tore. A stroke and a loop. A knot sign, I think. I don't know it."

"Let me," said Bindu.

She said it almost too low to hear. Kunal moved his hand.

Bindu leaned over the paper. Her lips moved. After a moment, under her breath, she began to sing a thing Ila had heard her hum in the top room while she wound the red rope. *Two over, one under, and loose it at the end.* She stopped at the last line.

"It's crew work," she said. "When you finish a pattern, there's a sign for the day you let it go. The thaw. We say it when the cold gives and the knot comes loose." She touched the paper. "This is *thaw*."

"Carried till no thaw," said Kunal.

The wind moved the lantern flame. Nobody moved.

"It is the line on the door," said the queen. She had stopped at the edge of the lantern light, small and straight, and her voice was careful. "We hold it in a different hand, in the first chronicle of the Shilak post. *Carried.*"

"What does the archive say it means?" Ila said.

"That a death is owed." Sarala said it as an old woman reads a bill. "Each time the pass calls, a body is carried out of the world. The Bargain was struck so that the mountain would let the living cross, and the mountain took one in return. That is what my mother's mother left me in the drawer." She breathed. "Sixty-one winters ago the pass called, and a warden of the Shilak post answered. His name was Harish."

Rohan made no sound. Ila did not turn to look at him.

"Our grandfather," she said.

"Yes."

"Say it, Majesty," said Meera. "What the chronicle says of his dying. For the count."

The queen did, with one hand on the stone. "He was the last. Harish of the Shilak post went into the snow at the pass's call, and was carried down at the thaw. The Closing Law was written the next spring, that none should be asked again."

"And the hills say it differently," said Ila.

She had not planned to speak first. It came out of her as a tally comes out on a sheet, flat and exact. Every face in the ring turned.

"On the Widow's Ladder there's a song," she said. "*The widow was carried down.* In every hamlet from the Kuhl to the Eye. Kunal told me what it means there. It means she was seen across. By hand, by someone's hand, over the bad ground." Her voice shook and she let it. "The chronicle uses the word for the dead. The hills use it for the living. Same word, same stone. I think the Bargain was written in the hills' sense, and the crown has read it in its own for sixty-one winters."

"You think," said the queen.

"I can't prove it." She made herself hold the old woman's eyes. "I read the marks. A death is owed, or a crossing is. Both fit the line, and neither fits it better. There's nothing on the stone to choose."

"Then we have two readings and no judge," said Sarala, with some bitterness. "We have had that situation for sixty-one winters."

Meera had her count open on her forearm. She wrote, blotted it, and said without looking up, "I'm putting down both."

Kunal had gone quiet beside Ila. She knew the shape of his thought. If the hills' sense was true, no one had ever had to die. If the crown's was true, someone would, at the next calling, and the stone could not say who.

"Majesty," said Rohan. "You called for the thing to be found. It's found. What now?"

"Now we do nothing we cannot take back." The queen's gaze went across the ring. "The Under-Cairn is sealed from today. Wardens at the gap and at the crawl's mouth, day and night, until the fourth winter has passed. No one goes down. Not a prince, not a surveyor, not a captain." She did not say *not a boy who wants to die.* She looked at Kiran for one breath and looked away. "When the pass calls, we will have read it rightly or wrongly, and we would rather learn which with the door shut."

"The door will not stay shut," said Bindu, to the rubbing, and went red to the ears.

Gopal shifted his feet. The queen did not turn her head.

Ila turned hers.

Kiran stood at the edge of the light with his eyes on the last word, the knot-sign, as a man stares down a well. His lips were slightly open. His good ear had gone white at the rim.

She had known him all summer on the chain as the man who joked, who sang rude songs to the links and called her *boss*. She had never seen his face with the joke gone out of it.

"Kiran," she said.

He did not answer. After a time he put out one finger, carefully, as if the graphite might burn, and touched the loop Bindu had read as *thaw*. He took the finger back.

Meera made a small movement toward him, and stopped, and Ila saw her hold herself still the way a guide holds a rope that has not yet gone taut.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The rider came at full dark.

They heard him before they saw him: a lamed pony coming down the pass track off the snow, hooves slipping on the wet stone, and a man's voice shouting a warden's name. Gopal went down at a run with a lantern.

The man was a hill-council guard in undyed wool with a blue cord on the shoulder, grey to the eyes with road dust. He had led the pony the last miles on foot, and two wardens took its rein and held him up. When he was brought into the ring he looked at the queen, then said to Kunal, because he had been sent to say it to him:

"Highness. The regent is out."

"Pritam is in the tower," said Kunal. His voice was quite level.

"He was, three nights ago. The cell door was barred and the guard was at it all night, and in the morning the cell was empty." The man swallowed. "There's a stair, Highness. Inside the north wall, behind the plaster, between the landing and the lower room. He went down it into the kitchen passage. He had a key to the postern. Somebody gave him a key."

Ila heard her own voice before she decided to use it. "Two spans."

"What?" said the queen.

"The north wall is two spans out of true. I wrote it in my field book the eve of the Fair. The room is a full pace short of the outside." The cold went through her from the crown of her head. "I wrote 'void?' and left it a question. And there's a half-step on the landing nobody counts." She turned to Kunal. "I told you forty."

"You told me forty," said Kunal. He was looking at the black Saddle. "My family counts thirty-nine, for the ghosts. The ghost was the stair."

"I measured the void," said Ila. "I put it on the sheet as a wall's thickness, and it was a stair, and I never went to see. Because I thought a map was true or false, and what a wall hid was not my business."

"Ila," said Rohan.

"I'm all right." She was not, quite. "Where did he go?"

"East, Highness. Off the Garh road, and then off every road. Six men, perhaps more, and a pony string. The tracks were two days old when the council's scouts found them. They don't take the Kuhl cut or the lower road. They go up the dry side, toward a way nobody's drawn."

*East.* Ila did not move, her hand on the walnut. She was back on the Neshar road on the first night, with the pale blue line running off over the eastern shoulder, broken at the fourth light, toward a valley that was on none of her sheets. She had left the bearing out of her field book on purpose, and had not let herself think of it since.

He had been going to it all along. Or something had been calling him there.

"Majesty," said Gopal quietly. "Do we go after him?"

"Where?" said the queen. "The passes are free, and he walks them as free as you. But the dry side is bare rock and blown snow. Without a map, we would be sending men to die of cold." She looked toward the dark. "Four winters. The fourth is the one that matters, and Pritam knows it as well as the archive does. He did not escape for a crown. A crown he could have bought."

Nobody asked what he had escaped for.

Ila took the field copy out of her coat.

It was the pencil sheet she had kept for herself, rolled in a tube with her rule, the one the crown had never been shown. She spread it on the slab beside the rubbing. Her hand was steady. She found the Neshar road above the Fair meadow, and the blank east of it where the valley should have been. She laid her rule down and drew a line across the white, dotted, short strokes with spaces between them, the way a surveyor marks a way she has not walked.

At its end, in her small hard hand, she wrote: *unknown way. Pritam.*

Kunal watched her finish the last letter. He put his hand flat on the slab, the dead tips beside her pencil.

"You know what it costs," he said. "That way showed itself to us as cairn-light. Only to a bound pair, and only in winter. To follow him before snow is to walk blind. To follow him at all, we bind again."

"I know."

"And my fingers can't show you a lie any more. This winter the cold told you what I wouldn't. Next time you'd have only my word."

"Then I'll take your word," said Ila. "That's what we swore an hour ago."

He held her eyes. Then he said, "Then we'll find it."

It was not a question. It was a thing chosen with the price in plain view, and she heard it settle in her the way the walnut hung from her chain, small and light and true.

"When the snow comes," she said. "When it closes."

"Every winter," said Kunal. "Together."

Behind them, in the dark of the ring, the bell on the cairn rang once in the wind, and the water ran on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She found the salt house yard full of mules at first light.

She had come down the stair with her crutch and the field sheet in her coat, looking for tea, and stopped on the last step. The big door stood open. In the yard a dozen drovers in Taravan blue were unloading bales the colour of old brick: wool, undyed and madder-red, and the lanolin smell of it filled the room to the rafters.

A tall woman in a travelling cloak stood in the doorway with a ledger under her arm. She was perhaps forty, with a dry, handsome face, and she was surveying the goat pen as though she meant to price it.

"Early," said Meera, from the long table, with her left hand in its glove around a mug. "Nobody sends a convoy at thaw. The pass won't close for months."

"I know it." The woman did not turn. "I'm storing wool in your salt house before the Guild sets its winter prices. Whoever holds the best bales when the snow shuts the pass names the figure. I've waited twenty years to be early for something."

"Lata," said Meera. "This is Ila. Rohan's sister."

"The surveyor." Lata's glance took in the crutch, the chain, the red-knotted walnut. "They sing about your winter survey in the Taravan taverns. Your name's in the last verse, spelled wrong."

"How wrong?"

"Badly." Lata's mouth moved. Then it stopped, and her face changed, and she said to Meera, much more quietly, "A Neshar drover told me a story at the Taravan inns. He'd crossed at the thaw with the council. About the torches at Ranu. About a man who rode up in a slate cloak and told the soldiers to lower them. Grey-bearded. An old soldier's seat on a horse. Nobody will say who he was. The drover asked the healer there himself. She told him, *not mine to spend.*"

Ila felt the stair rail under her palm.

Meera set the mug down. "I never learned his name either. I saw him once, at the Fair. He laid a sword belt on the grass."

"And walked away from it," said Lata.

"Yes."

"Who was he, Meera?"

Meera opened the count on her forearm and turned it so Lata could see. Under the word *Fair* there was a ruled line, and the line was empty. "There's the place for his name," she said. "I've never had it to write. But Ranu does. And Ranu is keeping him."

Lata looked at the blank line, then at the wool and the mules and the white road beyond the gate. She said, to no one, in her warm, tart voice, "Then Ranu owes somebody a name. I'll collect it."

She went back out to count her bales, and Ila stood on the stair a long time with the walnut in her hand, thinking of a dotted line in the east and a name that no one would say.

## Coming next {.unnumbered}


Lata knows the price of everything. Wool by the stone, madder by the pound, a house's good name by the winter. She has kept the accounts of her life tidy for twenty-two years, because what is broken stays broken and you only keep the books.

She still dyes one skein a season in a particular red. She tells herself it sells well.

Now the regent's war is coming, and Lata's convoy of winter wool and her late husband's ledgers must cross the closed passes to Neshar before spring. The ledgers prove who has been hoarding the valley's grain. The pass says she cannot go alone.

Tej was the regent's general. He is exiled now, and crossing back into Hemkot is a hanging offence. He knows the mercenary army that is gathering, and he has to warn the queen. He is forty-five, he speaks like a soldier, he says he deserves nothing, and he handles every borrowed boot and horse as if it were precious.

Twenty-two winters ago his soldiers burned her family's wool sheds. She has never forgotten the smell of that night.

Tied wrist to wrist on a mountain where every lie freezes the liar's hands, neither can soften what happened. Everything he says is true, and so is everything she remembers. Some of it will not balance.

A war is coming. A rope is waiting. A house, a pardon and a long-ago love are all on the account.

Will Lata stand surety for the exiled general whose soldiers burned her family's sheds, and take their second chance across a war?
